## EM Economic & Strategy Outlook A looser leash

A soft landing in the US and a loosening Fed constraint from a measured cutting cycle should provide a relatively supportive backdrop for EM assets. But valuations largely reflect this and risks to the benign base case appear pronounced. We see further room for rates to perform, but are more selective in FX and credit.

Local: The Fed has been a binding constraint for local assets most of the year, but the narrative is quickly shifting with Fed cuts imminent. We think the Fed shifting to a more accommodating stance is enough for the recent repricing in EM rates to stick, and have follow-through in some countries (eg. Mexico, Czech, Israel and India). We see scope for a decoupling between EM rates and EMFX as this plays out, with rates outperforming FX. That is, as central banks ease and carry erodes, there is room for risk wobbles to take USD-EM higher, especially as EM FX reflects very little recession premia.

Credit:<sup>1</sup> We expect a range-bound environment over the next few weeks, with potential bouts of volatility, and recommend taking advantage of opportunities in idiosyncratic HY sovereigns. We upgrade BAHAMA to OW and see further room for KENINT (OW) to perform. Among ongoing/recently concluded restructurings, we recommend buying SURINM (33s and 50s) and UKRAIN B 2034s. The balance of risks for SRILAN (OW) remains positive, in our view, and we think that GHANA's (OW) market-implied exit yields also leave further room for upside. A tug of war between supply and demand is likely to drive performance in lower-beta HY and IG: we upgrade of SOAF to MW (from UW), but recently downgraded PANAMA to UW. Heavy and recurrent supply from MEX and KSA, combined with political/geopolitical considerations, leave us with UW ratings on both. Likely lack of further supply in 2024 should support our OWs in COSTAR and DOMREP. We view spreads in EEMEA/LatAm Corporates as expensive on the whole, but have an OW rating on India HY Corporates in Asia.

## Economic outlook: From inflation fears to growth concerns

In Global Outlook: Calm on the surface, 20 June, we wrote about uneventful and largely favourable macroeconomic conditions (steady growth, uneven but continued progress on inflation, and a gradual easing cycle), while pointing to risks related to optimistic equity markets and political uncertainties. Some of these have materialised over the summer, most notably the market turbulence in the face of the JPY carry trade unwind and the change in the

Democratic presidential candidate. Needless to say, the upcoming US elections, especially the potential of sharp tarif increases under a second Trump administration remains a significant uncertainty to the global outlook.

FIGURE 1. Inflation has continued to moderate...
![](images/f85bb489a16f910ab5900a2d344548d9fc028ccc5f97db654886dc1c9a8ca058.jpg)
Source: Haver Anlaytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 2. ...however, growth fears have increased...
![](images/27cdf50100b4d2e4afe5973ca08bb76c173c35308496abd37886d2ac58c5ef16.jpg)
Source: S&P Global, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

The key macro development over the recent months, however, has been the shift in the balance between inflation and growth risks. While inflation prints have been encouraging, showing continued disinflation, many activity indicators, including a deterioration in global manufacturing PMIs, point to a slowdown in growth. Such a shift was broadly anticipated in our June macro scenario, which is why our growth and inflation forecasts remain largely unchanged. However, the risks to the scenario are now tilted the other way: while in June we may have been concerned whether our disinflation predictions for H2 were too optimistic, the question is now whether our growth projections will hold. At the same time, this has increased our conviction in our expected rate cutting cycles.

## FIGURE 3. ...contributing to the beginning of the global easing cycle

![](images/f7ba56cd26feab61622e352e7b589ffd68f3a5fad1fcaf114d6f24557c923ae6.jpg)
Note: The share of central banks hiking less the share cutting. A central bank is hiking if the 3m change in the policy rate is positive and cutting if it is negative, otherwise it is considered on hold. We use a sample 37 central banks. Source: BIS, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

That said, our baseline scenario remains constructive. We still believe the 'landing' of the US economy, which remains front and centre for the global outlook, can be soft one. We see US cooling further from the robust 3.0% q/q saar in Q2 to below trend in coming quarters (1.5% q/q saar) but without falling into recession. This would be accommodated by a gradual easing cycle of 75bp (three 25bp cuts) by end 2024 and quarterly cuts of 25bp during 2025. The recovery in Europe has disappointed thus far, but in aggregate the European economy is likely to muddle through at a low growth rate (0.7% for 2004).

We are more concerned about China's growth outlook. The economy clearly sufers from debtdeflation dynamics to which policy response thus far has remained insuficient. We downgraded China's 2024 growth to 4.8%, following the poor Q2 GDP print. However, even if oficial statistics may eventually still print a GDP expansion close to the oficial 5% target, the underlying growth dynamics seem much weaker.

Elsewhere in the EM universe, our 2024 growth forecasts for Latin America (1.6%), EM Asia ex-China (5.6%), and EEMEA (2.8%) are now slightly higher than they were in June, but without any notable changes in the dynamics. All this aggregates to global growth of 3.2% in 2024, which is similar, but a touch higher, than the 3.1% we had penciled in back in June. In 2025, we expect global growth to slow slightly, to 2.9% (-0.1pp from June), due to downward revisions led by the euro area and LatAm.

## Growth matters, to a degree

The market swings in August have illustrated how the market's focus on the US growth outlook (and global growth as a corollary), can inject substantial volatility into EM and broader risk assets when data surprises and challenges prevailing market narratives.

## EM HY sovereign credit performance tied to commodity prices

Sharply slowing global and EM growth is of course not a positive environment for EM risk assets in general and it is hence understandable that EM assets can react nervously to recessionary concerns. For EM credit, the commodity angle appears particularly important in this context. The EM HY sovereign credit space contains a large number of smaller, commodity-exporting frontier markets, eg. in Sub-Saharan Africa, and it is hence unsurprising that EM HY-IG spread diferentials have shown some visible sensitivity to commodity prices. Aggregate commodity prices have trended lower since late April and this has been accompanied by moderate HY-IG decompression (Figure 6).

FIGURE 6. EM HY-IG credit spread diferentials have been correlated with commodities
![](images/d4cf08390a9caa94a2f061c0dbca0c367c3d54e0585a932bf80e4f175a0754b5.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 7. EM credit has accumulated steady total returns during the broader recent market volatility
![](images/f53b17f3b9ff4c8ff237847be8be9f4017ea1c2e4d4f8a3de49a97ca109fbc86.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## But overall EM credit returns have limited sensitivity to growth in most circumstances

Despite these risks, EM credit investors can take some comfort from the fact that historically, EM credit returns have not tended to be that closely correlated to growth in most circumstances, for two reasons. First, the usual negative correlation between spreads and rates provides a natural bufer against return volatility, especially during periods of rapid re-assessments of growth prospects and related central bank reactions: for example, as growth concerns surged in early August, rates rallied, allowing EM credit to accumulate steady (total) returns despite the broader market volatility (Figure 7).

Second, there is a good theoretical rationale as to why EM credit should be less sensitive to growth than other EM asset classes, such as EM FX or equities. In Re-thinking Emerging Markets (part 2), 6 December 2018, we applied a Merton-type model to EM assets. In this stylised application of the seminal model<sup>2</sup> , FX and equity holders’ positions resemble a long call option on EM GDP/growth (potentially unlimited upside, but a ‘minimum’ growth threshold needs to be crossed for an investment to deliver positive payouts). EM credit investors, on the other hand, hold a position more akin to a short put position on EM growth (with a ceiling to profit, but losses ultimately only occur when growth is so low that debt-servicing capacity is threatened – Figure 8). In this framework, as long as growth is slowing from comfortable levels, debt should be relatively insensitive. Only if growth were to fall dramatically, threatening the creditworthiness of EM debtors – typically an ‘EM crisis’ situation – then would we expect debt sensitivity to falling growth to increase rapidly. And this is not a scenario we think is likely in the current context, as discussed above.

## EM-DM growth diferentials can matter

Rather than EM growth levels or changes, EM asset returns, including those of EM credit (Figure 9) have shown a clearer correlation with EM-DM growth diferentials historically. This supports the thesis that our base case scenario – a softly landing US economy, allowing the Fed to embark on its cutting cycle, with stable EM-DM growth diferentials – would constitute an overall comfortable backdrop for EM credit returns.

FIGURE 8. Stylised illustration of the Merton model in a sovereign context
![](images/37e77d7db9a9fdea87e93827d32b25f08947dd744f1bfb776e71b6607e7f9046.jpg)
Source: Barclays Research

FIGURE 9. EM credit returns have historically been less correlated to growth than EM FX/equity returns, but EM-DM growth diferentials appear to matter
![](images/c01f57be410068035a9be1015bcc40f4298dc27a6d90dfd217a92aa699733240.jpg)
MSCI ex-China index used for equities, Bloomberg EM USD Agg for EM credit, JMFXGEME Index for EM FX. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## EM FX reflect small US recession premia

We expect the US economy to land softly in our base case, which the market is largely aligned with. In this context, EM FX have already accumulated some premia for potential US recession risk, though it is admittedly small and reflected in LatAm FX, which have underperformed for idiosyncratic reasons.

The FX market response largely depends on the drivers of the underlying recession and surrounding market environment, according to our analysis of past recessions which are summarised in a risk-of index (see FX Quarterly for details).

Figure 10 shows that all analysed US recessions triggered USD outperformance versus EM FX, the size of the sell-of being broadly proportional to our risk-of index. The EU recession also generated a similar EM FX response. The DM FX reaction has been more heterogeneous, with the USD outperforming DM FX in the COVID, GFC and EU recessions, but underperforming in the dotcom recession (as well as in the past few months, in so far as the market is trading such dynamics). This is a function of whether the US is converging or diverging from the rest of the world, particularly visible in the dot-com versus EU recessions. Finally, the analysis shows that LatAm, CEEMEA and G10 cyclical currencies are the most exposed to US recession risks, while low-yielders ofer stability against the USD (Figure 11).

Should the US economy deteriorate further, our model suggest 2001 is the most direct parallel. It sticks out as a mild recession, with hardly any contraction in the economy. Here, the USD weakened modestly against G10 FX, but strengthened against the EM complex (LatAm and CEEMEA in particular), though we acknowledge that the FX regimes have changed since (some were pegged or managed at the time).

FIGURE 10. FX market reactions seem proportional to the risk-of index changes
![](images/5274c039d14e5cdbd800b3c37d61eca29724427af789a124503d566fd9adc563.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 11. Recessions appear heterogeneous across regions
![](images/2475e592032bac92ff8825e523259e6f1bb0024d47e79ff4f9867dbf51e52438.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## New sources of flows support supply-demand technicals in EM credit

The discrepancy between solid performance of the asset class and lack of flows into EM dedicated bond funds has been one of the most discussed topics in our client conversations over the past few months, especially in EM credit. Indeed, flows into EM bond funds have been mostly absent, across both EM local and hard-currency bond funds. At the same time, supply in EM sovereign credit has been heavy, driven by heavy issuance from EM IG sovereigns in particular.

FIGURE 12. EM sovereign credit supply YTD is running well ahead of historical averages...
![](images/99f5b948ef27978426ab17c86053ffc2b48ffa0dd71ae12595b988c765c14055.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 13. ...driven by heavy issuance from IG sovereigns
![](images/dfd24228e5f6324bc0237350b960fac7f2ed990ec949092e14b0ce24a37bef2f.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

These observations imply that incremental demand for bonds, especially in IG, has come from other sources than flows into EM dedicated funds. Of course, a number of issuers have a long history of local demand, such as the GCC region or the Philippines, as a consequence of local dollar liquidity. But there also seem to be new sources of international demand emerging or (re-)emerging. In The China bid goes global, 1 August 2024, we highlighted increasing demand from Chinese investors for ofshore USD bonds, with the GCC region as well as parts of CEE the major beneficiaries in the EM sovereign space (Figure 14). This demand is coming from a low base, but is likely to grow further over time, due to: 1) likely persistent elevated ofshoreonshore yield diferentials; 2) subdued supply but large maturities in the China credit complex; 3) banks' asset/liability management needs amid elevated deposits; and 4) increasing household wealth management demand, shifting away from property. In addition, US credit investors appear to have steadily increased their EM exposure again (Figure 15). These sources of demand are likely to continue to support EM IG primary markets during the autumn primary market season.

FIGURE 14. Chinese investor holdings of EM hard-currency bonds across EM regions\*
![](images/b084be14c9d70a0294a97580444afa741b2e971bd7a338e0ba473ecd890d99e3.jpg)
\*Korea and Singapore not included in EM Asia. As of H2 23 Source: SAFE, Barclays Research
FIGURE 15. US credit investors have increased their exposure to EM again

![](images/22c6c7626b0cf9d31c6095c5c39526d521a4c00d147e1e0bb0539de3cc6955ff.jpg)
Sample of 31 US Agg benchmarked funds with a combined AUM of c.USD1.5trn. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

For HY issuers, primary market access has been more dificult. However, with the overall decline in yields, some EM issuers are approaching levels where market issuance seems possible. In the absence of flows into EM dedicated funds and the alternative sources of demand mostly focused on IG, we think supply in the HY sector may be a more important technical factor and potential source of diferentiation and of tactical trading opportunities. For example, both Angola and Nigeria have indicated their interest in tapping international markets if a window of opportunity opens up. Especially if such issuance were to be combined with liability management, we think this could constitute a positive catalyst, particularly for the short-end of the curves. In other cases, however, we expect potential temporary pressures from (heavy) supply, including Turkiye or Colombia . Overall, our updated country-by-country EM hardcurrency sovereign supply forecasts for the remainder of 2024 (Figure 16) bring our full-year forecasts to USD155-160bn, compared with our previous forecast of c.USD120-125bn as published in our 2024 EM sovereign credit supply outlook, 27 October 2023.

FIGURE 16. Country-by-country EM hard-currency supply forecasts for the remainder of 2024
![](images/ae63013113e160f78c9d07b74284fee7fe7710c6e577ae6a06135b233c84365f.jpg)
Source: Barclays Research

## A looser Fed constraint should support local markets

## The Fed has been a binding constraint for EM local assets...

EM local markets have been heavily constrained by Fed hawkishness. As the constraint is lifted, it should give local easing cycles room to extend, boosting returns for various local curve trades.

A later start to the Fed cycle and fears of a higher US neutral rate left EM rates abnormally high. Expectations of EM central banks cutting to neutral at the end of their own hiking cycles were heavily shaken during periods of firm US data. As the timing of the first Fed cut got pushed back, central banks delayed or slowed their own easing cycles. Moreover, resilient US data led EM central banks to turn more cautious amid risks that their own neutral rates were also higher.

Figure 17 shows the repricing was larger for central banks already easing, but the end result was the same for all: EM central banks were left with terminal rates above neutral (Figure 21).

Since inflation was easing but growth wasn't collapsing, we argued that there was also no rush. Authorities had space for higher rates to protect currencies and prioritize financial stability, with 1y1y spreads to US rates at the tights and margins of safety thin (Figure 18). But this may well be changing.

FIGURE 17. EM pricing has followed the Fed
![](images/1d86bda50d6bbf4c08f2ef4bf3e3108798796a65814bec6cde7554afc6842a78.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 18. Spreads to US rates are still near tights
![](images/e363768816d2c57a3598664dc25e722187c8cb1f84b5a58d47c4a62138048339.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## ...but this constraint may finally be loosening

Fed cuts are now imminent, with the narrative quickly shifting to a US slowdown as labour markets appear to be cooling. EM rates have already followed US rates lower, which shouldn't be that surprising given the strong co-movement between the two over time (Figure 19).

Our economists have penciled in a gradual Fed normalisation starting this month, with lower inflation alone warranting easing. They expect a terminal rate above neutral (3.75%) on the assumption that the labour market will cool, but not deteriorate meaningfully from current levels.

FIGURE 19. EM rates tend to co-move with US rates...
![](images/c38f214d020c6823f789d78d7aedc3c944ec8b898d142fe06460b636b5576ac4.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 20. ...with the risk neutral rate being the driver this time
![](images/566798815116542208f78e808c388630ef4279df14f4a1d54107179564ad1667.jpg)
Source: ACM, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

We think that EM rates should benefit from the loosening of the constraint that a dovish Fed has been. Periods of Fed cuts have broadly seen market expectations of r\* drop (Figure 20). While that drop may be shallower this time, relaxing concerns on this front suggest that at least a source of pressure for EM rates is now fading, as US risk neutral rates have been a larger driver than US term premium this cycle. In turn, we think a normalisation alone gives EM central banks breathing room, especially those that were worried about cutting through Fed Funds before the Fed even started to ease.

## We still see room for follow-through in EM rates...

EM front-end rates repriced lower since the high in US rates in April, with several expecting policy rates to drop close to neutral (Figure 21). Brazil, which we expect to hike, is the exception. We think the Fed shifting to a more accommodating stance is enough for this repricing to stick, and even have follow-through in some countries.

FIGURE 21. EM central banks have repriced lower with a looser Fed constraint
![](images/6ddc13931e23ffe42773e4df7b1746ec766fbb48163f35736b1c59186629e1a0.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 22. A look at 2019 suggests EM rates could outperform
![](images/25a05059d1f17ca5fab432fc6caf75a266f9ea44d7ddd66894c033db4572bbb0.jpg)

## Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Our economists expect a soft-landing in the US. However, it is possible there may not be much resolution on the recession debate in the near term, and markets could still get nervous about a harder landing. We review 11 Fed cutting cycles. Four of those can be considered mid-cycle adjustments, achieving a soft landing outcome (1984, 1995, 1998, and 2019). The remaining seven ended with recessions (1974, 1980, 1981, 1989, 2001, 2007, and 2020). We divide the 2019 cutting cycle into two legs, as it looked like the Fed was heading for a soft landing with rates on hold before COVID-19 thrust the economy into recession.

Figure 23 shows that deeper cuts by the Fed tend to get initially priced in cases where recessions were ultimately avoided, as these harder landing risks are assessed (though its true that ultimately the market overestimates easing, see FX Quarterly for details). This uncertainty, however, should give some follow-through in the recent EM rates moves, and in some cases even potentially push rates below neutral.

FIGURE 23. Soft landings disappoint easing expectations (at least at the extremes)

Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

The 2019 cycle ofers a good illustration. Figure 22 shows that the rally in US rates supported a move lower in EM rates as well, with EM rates bottoming out after the US, as central banks in countries with domestic catalysts took advantage of the Fed cuts.

Things may get more complicated later in the Fed easing cycle, if a recession is ultimately avoided. 2019 is again an interesting template of this, as we mention above, where US rates eventually got stuck as the market gained confidence on a soft landing scenario. In this second stage of the move, spread compression with EM is likely, as there could still be an impulse to ease in these economies, and US rate hedges should become more compelling.

## ...though EM FX could underperform in this environment

In a world where the Fed constraint is lifted, global growth is slow and global goods inflation in check, EM central banks may likely prioritise easier policy over strong currencies. This would be a shift from what has been the case until now, where central banks have eased to the extent they could on lower inflation, but kept real rates elevated to protect currencies. The BCCh is once again the first mover on this front, guiding for a faster normalisation to neutral at this month's policy meeting, while NBH's Virag suggested the Fed will determine how much space there is to ease.

Thus, we see scope for a decoupling between EM rates and EM FX as this plays out. EM rates tend to follow US rates more closely than EM FX. Figure 24 also shows that this divergence with rates tends to be the case around Fed easing cycles, which are shaded in gray. That said, the magnitude of the divergence ultimately comes down to the type of landing.

As central banks ease and carry erodes, there is room for risk wobbles to take USD-EM higher. At the cross sectional level, countries were rates move meaningfully higher when risk aversion rises are similar to the ones where rates are most sensitive to their own currencies depreciating. Figure 25 shows this with a scatter plot of rate betas to the VIX (y-axis) versus rate betas to USD-EM (x-axis). Thus, more sensitive countries such as Colombia, Hungary, Mexico and South Africa could require more caution on the FX front. Brazil is also sensitive, but with the BCB about to start a hiking cycle we think it could outperform the results.

FIGURE 24. EM FX and rates tend to decouple during Fed easing
![](images/3f1b1976820cf8fc6d2548e2cfde7bed2109221df8a9fa2e0faabace2c0235af.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 25. COP, HUF, MXN and ZAR could require caution on easing
![](images/f49409123d2302db647f661477284a30687c9a57458a02b410335323d7860167.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

## EM rates & FX recommendations: Rates to outperform

The lifting of the fed constraint on EM. As we mention above, we think a looser Fed constraint should support EM rates. Central banks over-delivered market expectations in the pats 46 cycles we study across EM, with two exceptions in India. These cycles have been diferent, and slower in delivering cuts. But we think as the Fed eases, central banks will find momentum in their cycles as well, especially the ones that are lagging.

FIGURE 26. Central banks generally over-delivered market expectations in the past...
![](images/2132f680756773fdf521cf044250a46543acb2c9c179cc7bf572d5be1813d89b.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 27. ...this cycle has been a bit slower, but a looser Fed constraint could change this
![](images/075458d938e016ccd78ce5f0cf3272a603c423a8349d16f10127ef552d949c28.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

• Receive 2y TIIE: Banxico has started to timidly ease, and markets have been conservative on the cycle going forward as AMLOs reforms make their way through Congress this month. While the fiscal impact is still unclear, Banxico still has ample space to ease. Headline inflation has rebounded on higher agricultural prices, but Banxico remains focused on the downward trend in core, while services is showing initial signs of easing. Moreover, the Fed constraint is loosening, and real rates are quite restrictive. FX pressures are unlikely to derail the cycle, in our view, as the bank estimates a moderate pass-through and it could opt for intervention if the currency takes another hit from external drivers. Our economists expects consecutive cuts, with an eventual acceleration to 50bp cuts. We think markets are underpricing risks of a faster cycle and stay received 2y TIIE.

• Receive 5y5y CZK: Czech rates are best suited in CEE to compress outpacing its core peers. Czech inflation is already at the CNB’s target and the economy continues to operate below potential. In addition, the fiscal policy remains very conservative. Thus, we think the CZK curve will continue to converge to its German equivalent while the CNB continues its easing cycle. Given ongoing CNB hawkish communication, and front-end rates' recent asymmetric sensitivity to US rates, we prefer to receive the back end of the curve, which should be less volatile.

• Receive 2y2y ILS: Geopolitical risk is well embedded in both the rates curve and FX, in our view. Moreover, local rates are lagging the US rates rally in spite of historically high correlation. We think current rates ofer enough compensation for Israeli-specific risks, while the wide gap between ILS and US rates ofer scope for a catch-up trade.

• Long 10y IGBs: Finally, we think India will start to ease. We expect favourable demand/ supply dynamics and commitments towards fiscal consolidation, as well as bond inclusion, to boost rates.

There are also places where think too much is already priced and will benefit less from the Fed dovishness:

• Pay PLN 5x8 FRA (soon rolling to 4x7): We still see value in paying front-end FRAs, as we expect the NBP to continue to push back against easing amid local inflationary pressures stemming from regulated price adjustments.

• Pay 1y1y HUF: The NBH continues to signal its willingness to cut rates, though it still feels global risk sentiment deterioration is a constraint. This global sentiment translates directly into NBH concerns about the EURHUF exchange rate, in our view. We think the NBH will continue to cut rates, but will be forced to end its cycle early (at around 5%) in order to maintain HUF stability.

Policy divergence and rate hikes. Go short USDBRL: We recommend short USDBRL as we expect the BRL to outperform within EM in the coming months. Brazil spent much of the year in a self-inflicted confidence crisis, where the monetary noise essentially served as a wake-up call that the fiscal anchor is weak. But the coordinated message across the administration and the BCB suggests that pessimism has peaked for now. The fiscal framework remains vulnerable but, for now, we think authorities have done enough to calm markets on this front as risks for 2024 have subsided. Moreover, the BCB is set to start a hiking cycle in September, building carry back into the FX while the rest of the world is easing rates (most importantly, the Fed).

We also see value in being long PLNHUF spot. Monetary policy divergence should support PLN against its regional peers (HUF and CZK), as the NBP maintains its relatively hawkish 'on hold' narrative, while the CNB and the NBH should continue their easing cycles. In addition, the Polish economy should continue to exhibit higher growth momentum fueled by EU funded investments, which should ofer an additional boost to the PLN.

China in a liquidity trap – stay in 10s30s CGB steepener: As China moves towards a liquidity trap in which consumers and investors refuse, or are unwilling, to spend and instead hoard cash, rendering monetary policy inefective and impotent, term deposits and wealth management products have seen strengthening demand. Cash in WMPs ultimately ends up in China’s bond markets, weighing on yields, especially around the 10y part of the curve. The PBoC has attempted to tighten its grip on the CGB yield curve by selling borrowed bonds into the

market, which we think puts a floor under back-end yields. Likely higher supply into year-end should also help.

We also stay long USDCNH. We acknowledge the rising risk of a binary event from Chinese exporters' conversion flows, but see opportunities to build dollar longs, given still weak fundamentals. We expect PBoC eforts to curb CNY appreciation to intensify when USDCNY falls below 7.10 (see North Asia: Gauging potential dollar selling by exporters). The reluctance to boost demand-side stimulus, combined with monetary policy easing amid still-wide US/China rate diferentials, still points to a higher USDCNY.

## EM credit recommendations: Staying focused

## A macro backdrop that is likely to be moderately supportive of EM credit fundamentals

As we discuss above, a softly landing US economy with stable EM/DM growth diferentials, as our forecasts imply, appears a reasonably supportive global fundamental backdrop for EM credit. And while volatility in key commodity prices poses a risk, especially for HY commodity exporters, there is limited evidence so far that the recent volatility has severely dented EM sovereigns' fundamental trajectory on an aggregate level.

The positive rating trend earlier in the year has become more balanced by now (Figure 29), but it would be dificult, in our view, to assign one common global macro driver to recent rating downgrades. Rather, the fallout from idiosyncratic/political challenges (Kenya, Peru, Bolivia) or geopolitical risk events (Israel) have arguably been the driving force. Moreover, as the Fed is likely to embark on a cycle of measured cuts in September, global yields are well below their recent peaks and EM primary market activity has been buoyant, refinancing conditions for EM sovereigns also seem much more benign than they have been for some time. Finally, a number of landmark EM restructuring cases have been completed or appear on their final stretch, which should provide some boost to sentiment for the asset class and allow investors to re-focus their attention on opportunities elsewhere.

## And technicals also seem broadly supportive

We also think that technicals in the EM sovereign credit market are in reasonable shape. Heavy supply (especially in IG) has been met with new sources of demand, as discussed above, and investor positioning does not appear heavy: our latest analysis of EMBI-benchmarked fund holdings (Figure 28) shows that investors have added exposure to some high-yielding names (including Turkey, Argentina, Dominican Republic and El Salvador), but this has been largely ofset by trimming of exposure to credits that can be considered as liquid, high-beta names (such as a number of SSA credits including Angola, Nigeria and Egypt), as well as some restructuring stories such as Ghana and Ukraine. Moreover, reductions in Mexico, Kenya, Romania and the GCC region appear to reflect a focus on idiosyncratic developments, while the increased exposure to low-beta Poland is notable, too.

FIGURE 28. EMBI funds' positioning vs benchmark and changes over the past three months
![](images/68d3ebf983819b19ff550f760028a08b95520ce2dcfb146a079b498f4e8ad091.jpg)
Country exposure is measured by country of risk. Based on a sample of 32 EMBI-benchmarked funds with a reporting lag of 1-3m. Labelling follows the 2-letter ISO codes. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

But risks to our base case are meaningful, likely limiting investors' risk appetite All of the above would argue for spreads to grind tighter over the next few weeks. However, we think the risks to our benign base case are pronounced. Valuations do not seem particularly compelling at the aggregate index level, with the spread of the Bloomberg EM USD sovereign index tighter by c.45bp YTD and just c.15bp above the YTD tights in early April. And relative valuations versus US credit do not tell a compelling story either, especially for EM HY sovereigns: The EM sov HY/US HY spread ratio is now at the most compressed level since December 2023 following recent EM outperformance (Figure 30). Moreover, recent episodes of elevated crossmarket volatility have illustrated investors' sensitivity to data that could challenge the benign base case. Finally, the tight US election race and prevailing geopolitical uncertainties will likely make investors hesitant to aggressively or indiscriminately engage in risk taking over the next few weeks.

FIGURE 29. The EM rating trend has become balanced after a positive start to the year
![](images/2081ba03fea378032e596edd0dba0519faf7405c63aeb130eecdc6c0c0a89394.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research
As of 9 September. Based on the universe of the Bloomberg EM hard-ccy sovereign index. Default situations excluded.

FIGURE 30. EM sov HY/US HY credit spread ratio at the most compressed level since late 2023 following recent EM outperformance
![](images/30a7a16f92f6911932be7275a5559ae0b450bf40f07d9656ed0b95b067712e36.jpg)
Rating and maturity-matched EM sovereign versus US corporate credit spread ratio. Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

As a consequence, we expect a broadly range-bound environment for EM credit over the next few weeks, with potential bouts of volatility, and continued investor focus on idiosyncratic opportunities and credit-specific divergences.

• Taking advantage of opportunities in idiosyncratic HY sovereigns. Our recommendations in this space are focused on smaller credits and/or opportunities that have arisen as various restructurings have concluded or appear close to the finishing line. We upgrade BAHAMA to OW (from MW) and see further room for KENINT (OW) to reverse the underperformance earlier in the summer. Among the recently concluded restructurings, we recommend buying SURINM (2033s and 2050s) and UKRAIN B 2034s (keeping a MW rating on UKRAIN overall). The outlook for SRILAN (OW) is closely tied to upcoming elections, but we continue to see the balance of risks as positive. Similarly we rate PKSTAN OW and recommend switching into PNGIB 2028s from MONGOL 2028s in Asia. GHANA's (OW) exit yields implied by current market prices of c.13-13.5% also leave further room for upside in the complex post-restructuring and medium term, in our view, even if election-related uncertainties may dent investor appetite in the short term.

• A tug of war between supply and demand likely to drive performance in lower-beta HY and IG. Strong technicals and a more promising policy outlook motivate our upgrade of SOAF to MW (from UW), with value concentrated in long-end bonds, in our view. However, we recently downgraded PANAMA to UW (from MW), given wider deficit projections and financing challenges. Heavy and recurrent supply from MEX and KSA, combined with political/ geopolitical considerations that may dent demand, leave us with UW ratings on both. We also expect supply to possibly weigh on TURKEY (UW) and COLOM (MW), while likely lack of further supply in 2024 should support our OWs in COSTAR and DOMREP.

• Spreads in EEMEA/LatAm Corporates are broadly expensive, in our view. However, total returns are likely cushioned from a decent index yield, which is attractive relative to fundamentals. Cyclicals have underperformed non-cyclicals in the August re-compression, but relative valuations are tighter than in Q4 23 and during the US regional banking crisis. In our view, the underperformance has room to run, if concerns about a weakening global economy persist. In HY, carry should remain the main driver for performance given tight spreads. In IG, we think supply will weigh on performance and see intermediate tenors as least attractive on curves. In EEMEA corporates, we like banks, particularly subordinated debt of GCC banks, and based on fundamental considerations, Turkey banks. African corporates also ofer some value as fundamentals and limited supply provide solid anchors, even though performance could be vulnerable to a tail risk scenario of a significant weakening of commodity and oil prices.

• In Asia corporate credit, we have an Overweight rating on India HY Corporates as we think sector performance could be supported by issuers’ solid credit profiles and strong technicals with likely limited supply. We are also constructive on Macau gaming on expected strong GGR growth, deleveraging and policy tailwinds. We see select opportunities in China HG corporates when focusing on China’s new growth engine credits, including TMTs, and idiosyncratic credit stories in corporates and asset management companies (AMCs). We expect the sector to continue to demonstrate its defensive characteristics, along with improvement in earnings and fundamentals. However, we see potential headline noise around US-China relations as election season approaches. We rate Korea HG Corporates at Underweight given tight valuations.

EM Heat Maps

# A comparative overview of EM country metrics

Our heat maps track various key indicators across Emerging Asia, EEMEA and Latin America related to political election calendars, vulnerability risks, and exposure to energy price volatility.

Our heat maps help to diferentiate across EM countries according to their vulnerabilities and strengths.

• Political Heat Map: Provides an election calendar marked by the relevance of each event.

• Vulnerability Heat Map: Provides a look at key metrics regarding the external, fiscal and financial risks of key EM countries we cover.

• Oil Heat Map: Analyzes the degree to which various EM countries are exposed to the volatility of oil prices and the associated efect on their economies.

• Current and Prospective IMF Involvement: We describe current IMF programs across the EM universe and where there might be programs in the future.

(5) Domestic credit by banks to the private sector as reported by World Bank Source: Haver Analytics, IMF, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Emerging Asia

# The Fed can open the door – but not all need enter

Banish the image of Emerging Asian central banks clamouring for monetary policy easing once US rates fall – economic activity generally remains well above levels that would ring alarm bells for policymakers, keeping some central banks vigilant on inflation, others on financial stability.

Trade Summary | China | EM Asia Frontier Economies | Hong Kong | India | Indonesia | Korea | Malaysia | Philippines | Singapore | Taiwan | Thailand

## Overview

• There is a school of thought that the Fed is the primary focus of all the region’s central banks as their outstretched fingers yearn for monetary policy easing. We are sceptical – economic activity generally remains well above levels that would ring alarm bells for policymakers.

• Financial-stability concerns – around hot residential property prices and high household debt – will likely dampen central banks' enthusiasm for lower interest rates in Korea, Taiwan, Thailand, Singapore and Malaysia.

• Indonesia inaugurates its next president on 20 October, Thailand adjusts to another Shinawatra as prime minister and Singapore awaits a general election. Malaysia's willthey-won't-they on RON95 petrol price hikes will likely continue.

The Fed can open the door – but not all need enter

Even with the US Federal Reserve likely on the cusp of a pivot towards rate cuts in September, we expect Emerging Asian central banks to remain relatively cautious and conservative in terms of monetary policy easing.

• Only two major central banks have braved rate cuts ahead of the Fed – and likely have more in store.

° <sup>Following</sup> <sup>rate</sup> <sup>cuts</sup> <sup>of</sup> <sup>10bp</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>7d</sup> <sup>OMO</sup> <sup>rate</sup> <sup>and</sup> <sup>20bp</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>1y</sup> <sup>MLF</sup> <sup>rate</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>July,</sup> <sup>we</sup> expect the People's Bank of China (PBoC) to deliver another three 10bp cuts in the 7d OMO rate – the main policy rate – in Q4 this year, and in Q1 and Q2 25.

° <sup>Outside</sup> <sup>of</sup> <sup>China,</sup> <sup>only</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>Philippine</sup> <sup>central</sup> <sup>bank</sup> <sup>has</sup> <sup>started</sup> <sup>easing</sup> <sup>–</sup> <sup>and</sup> <sup>we</sup> <sup>expect</sup> Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP) to follow up on its 25bp rate cut in August with another in October and three more in 2025.

## CORE

• The other central banks are not exactly rushing for the door in terms of policy easing. Our base case is for the Bank of Korea (BoK) to start rate cuts in October, but this is a close call – financial-stability concerns could delay this to November. Bank Indonesia (BI) seems likely to wait for not only the Fed to start cutting but also for the dust to settle from the US elections in November. The Reserve Bank of India (RBI) will likely need to wait until December for suficient clarity on price pressures to enable rate cuts to start. We think Taiwan's Central Bank of China (CBC) will likely wait even longer – until March 2025 – for financial-stability concerns to subside.

• And then there are those that are likely to defy the global policy-easing cycle – even into 2025. We believe financial-stability constraints will likely continue to keep the Bank of Thailand (BoT) on hold to encourage household deleveraging, and will also discourage Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM) from cutting unnecessarily after only raising its policy rate postpandemic back to pre-COVID levels, especially when inflation concerns will likely linger from continued talk of fuel-price hikes. For the Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS), we think the core inflation outlook also suggests its current FX policy settings need no easing.

The major central banks in the region do not regard the Fed as alpha and omega. True, a Fed pivot would likely make it easier for Emerging Asian central banks to follow suit – but it would be an error, in our view, to think the region's policymakers are chomping at the bit for their chance to commence monetary policy easing.

• As we noted in Emerging Asia: The Fed stoics (7 May 2024), the RBI, BNM, BoT and MAS in particular stood out to us as the central banks whose monetary policy decisions were least likely to be influenced by the Fed or the currency – and these have been precisely the institutions that have yet to show a clear interest in easing.

• On the flip-side, BSP – which we observed has historically kept an eye on the exchange rate pass-through to inflation – was willing to cut in August as price pressures cooled. Likewise, we think the PBoC saw a window to cut rates in July after an 11-month pause amid broad USD weakness and its implied let-up in CNY depreciation pressures (see China: A catch-up rate cut, 29 July 2024).

• While our base case is indeed for the BoK to begin lowering rates in October after the Fed cuts, policymakers could instead opt for a more cautious pace and delay, giving serious weight to concerns around financial stability – as we are expecting with our forecast of a March 2025 start from Taiwan's CBC.

Even BI – the major central bank we identified as being the most focused on the currency in Emerging Asia – has indicated that it plans to stay on hold through Q3 despite expecting a Fed pivot in September, likely to wait out the US elections. Historically, IDR appreciation has not been suficient for BI to cut rates – in 2019, the central bank had also waited for signs that Fed easing was imminent before hitting the button.

This time, we sense it is not just about the Fed, as most market participants likely believe. There appears to be additional caution around the 5 November US elections – and thus our base case is for BI to only embark on rate cuts at its 19-20 November policy meeting. This would notably also come after incoming Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto is inaugurated on 20 October – when there will likely be intense focus on the choice of the next finance minister and what this means for fiscal policy (see Indonesia: BI – Not just the Fed but also US elections, 21 August 2024).

## Still no strong need to ease

It is no surprise to us that Emerging Asian central banks are taking a relatively cautious approach to monetary policy easing – economic activity generally remains well above levels that would ring alarm bells for policymakers.

To be sure, the latest GDP data do suggest sequential growth softened in the region in Q2. We estimate economic activity rose by 0.7% q/q sa on average across the 10 major Emerging Asian economies, versus growth of 1.4% in Q1.

• In particular, we estimate China's headline GDP fell by 0.5% q/q sa in Q2, partly reversing the 3.0% expansion in Q1 that we had previously warned was likely not fully capturing underlying developments in the economy.

• At the same time, economic activity in India surprised to the downside, with sequential growth slowing slightly to an estimated 1.1% q/q sa in Q2 from 1.3% in Q1 (see India: Q2 24 GDP review – Deceleration in headline masks pickup in domestic demand, 30 August 2024).

• Outside of China and India, sequential growth slumped in the Philippines, moderated in Indonesia and Thailand, normalised in Hong Kong – and slipped into an outright 0.2% q/q sa contraction in Korea. Singapore also maintained its relatively tepid growth rate of 0.4% q/q sa. The outliers were Malaysia – where the sequential growth rate nearly doubled to 2.9% q/q sa – and Taiwan, which surprised positively with the pace of expansion edging up to 0.3% q/q sa from 0.1% in Q1.

However, our estimates suggest output gaps continue to tilt slightly positive in the region – and where these might be negative, they do not seem particularly deep. Taking a simple average of GDP-based output gaps across the 10 major Emerging Asian economies, our estimates suggest the region's output gap narrowed to a still-positive 0.09 standard deviations above trend in Q2 from +0.24 in Q1.

• Reflecting their sequential contractions in Q2 following outsized expansions in Q1, we estimate output gaps in China and Korea tumbled back into negative territory, normalising from one-of positive spikes. The positive output gaps in Taiwan, the Philippines, Singapore and India also narrowed and closed, by our estimates.

• At the other end of the spectrum however, our estimates suggest Malaysia's strong sequential expansion swung its output gap back into positive territory. We estimate the positive output gaps in Indonesia and Hong Kong also widened slightly while the negative output gap in Thailand narrowed further.

FIGURE 4. EM Asia output gap (simple average)
![](images/2042f25874f94b9883470c3e62fdaa4f24bb100d9ae89944afae2e462bc8cff7.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 5. Output gaps by economy
![](images/48000baf2bcdebb9677cb17d323015944c91f5e9bb734566087a5093137da156.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

These output gap dynamics help to explain why central banks across Emerging Asia are displaying such varying attitudes towards policy easing.

• China and the Philippines cut rates ahead of the Fed as their positive output gaps evaporated. For China, the plunge into negative territory likely dashed hopes that the Q1 outperformance heralded a recovery; for the Philippines, the closure of the positive output gap – coupled with more benign CPI prints – likely convinced policymakers that it was time to turn attention to growth and start stepping back from restrictive monetary policy.

• As noted above, Korea's recent output gap dynamics look similar to China's and seem also to call for imminent monetary policy easing – unlike China however, financial-stability concerns have restrained the BoK from cutting so far. Our base case of an October cut is thus predicated on a cooling of the residential property market in Korea after a tightening of

macroprudential measures in September – which implies the risk of a delay should concerns of overheating persist.

• Output-gap dynamics are also softening in India and Taiwan but will likely need more time to deteriorate to levels that spark greater concern over whether monetary policy is too restrictive – especially in Taiwan, where the output gap is still positive and there are also financial-stability concerns arising from the residential property market.

• While BI remains as pro-growth as ever and is likely looking for an opportune moment to start cutting rates, the widening positive output gap is likely bolstering the central bank's patience – which should allow the Indonesian central bank to wait for the dust to settle from the US elections and minimise the risk of currency turmoil.

• With the output gap swinging back up into positive territory in Malaysia and the negative output gap in Thailand narrowing, there is little reason for BNM and the BoT to de-prioritise financial stability and cut rates in the near term, in our view. And while Singapore's positive output gap seems to have closed, the estimated 1.5% slope setting of the SGD NEER policy band is likely not restrictive enough to require easing unless there is a more serious shift into negative territory.

## No panic necessary

Our latest economic growth forecasts also do not point to any particular cause for policymakers to panic.

## China's slowdown

Our 2024 GDP growth projection for Emerging Asia as a whole now stands at 5.2% – similar to our estimate in the June edition of the Emerging Markets Quarterly – implying only a slightly slower pace of expansion than 2023's 5.3%.

• We have reduced our China GDP growth forecast – which tends to dominate the regional aggregate due to the sheer size of its economy – to 4.8% from 5.0% previously. This, in turn, came mainly on the back of the weaker-than-expected oficial Q2 GDP data – which, as noted above, followed a Q1 outperformance that we had previously warned was likely not fully capturing underlying developments in the economy. Our current 4.8% forecast for 2024 implies a more visible slowdown from 5.2% in 2023.

• Outside of China, we also trimmed our GDP growth forecasts since June for Thailand (by -0.2pp) and Korea (-0.1pp) – but raised them for Malaysia (+0.7pp), Taiwan (+0.4pp), Hong Kong (+0.3pp) and India (+0.1pp), ofsetting the downgrades at the aggregate level.

For 2025, we continue to forecast GDP growth of 4.6%, which still represents a slowdown from our 5.2% projection for this year.

• We have left our 2025 GDP growth forecast for China unchanged at 4.0% – implying a slightly less-dramatic drop from our downgraded 4.8% estimate for 2024.

• Outside of China, we have reduced our 2025 GDP growth forecasts for Thailand (by -0.3pp) and Malaysia (-0.2pp) – but ofset this with upgrades for Taiwan (+0.2pp), Hong Kong (+0.2pp), India (+0.1pp) and Indonesia (+0.1pp).

## Better 2024 growth elsewhere

In contrast to the regional aggregate, which China tends to dominate, a simple average of our GDP growth forecasts across the 10 major economies in Emerging Asia would in fact imply a slightly stronger pickup from 3.6% in 2023 to 4.2% in 2024 – versus 4.1% previously – followed by moderation to 3.8% in 2025. Specifically, we continue to project GDP growth rising in 2024 in Taiwan, Singapore, Korea, Thailand, Malaysia and the Philippines – and falling in India, Hong Kong and China. GDP growth should then moderate in 2025 for most economies, except for Thailand, the Philippines and Indonesia.

## Exports still on a gradual, bumpy, uneven recovery path

Partly underpinning the resilience in the region's economic activity is likely to be a continued gradual – if bumpy and uneven – recovery in Emerging Asian exports. Into Q3, the available data suggest Emerging Asian exports edged up in August after trending up through July from the slide into a sharp dip in March, based on our Barclays EM Asia Export Cycle Tracker – which aims to provide a broad-based and timelier measure of the region's overall export cycle. The region's export cycle now appears to be around trend, with shipment levels looking much improved compared with their Q2 23 lows (see Emerging Asia: A new tool to track the export cycle, 15 April 2024).

Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 10. Seasonally adjusted export levels relative to trend: Rest of Asean
![](images/61336c06a3c2d4b637e26ef97e6db3707f6901ec5d3eb87aefb289aa5cbda4bc.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

The export data continues to show a gradual broadening of the tech upcycle out of North Asia into Southeast Asian economies, especially Malaysia, Singapore and even Thailand.

• The data suggest tech exports from Korea and Taiwan bottomed out as early as H1 last year before staging a visible upswing in H2, helped by the artificial intelligence boom. In recent months however, Taiwan's tech exports appear to have lost some steam – in contrast to Korea, where shipments have continued to outperform, possibly reflecting the deeper decline that its more commoditised semiconductor products faced earlier in 2023.

• Southeast Asia had lagged – likely on account of greater exposure to legacy chips such as those for smartphones and server/PC demand – but the data seem clear now that tech exports there also finally bottomed out around Q1 this year, which is increasingly reflected in the tech industrial production data.

## Inflation is not completely dead

Therefore, while continued disinflation has – together with the Fed – cracked the door open for monetary policy easing, still-resilient economic activity is keeping some central banks wary of complacency over price dynamics.

Our forecast remains for CPI inflation across Emerging Asia as a whole to moderate from 2.2% in 2023 to 1.8% in 2024 – similar to our estimate in the June edition of The Emerging Markets Quarterly – before picking up to 2.2% in 2025 (-0.1pp versus June).

• For 2024, we have reduced our headline inflation forecasts versus June for Indonesia (-0.4pp), Malaysia (-0.1pp) and the Philippines (-0.1pp) but raised them for Taiwan (+0.2pp) and India (+0.1pp).

• For next year, our headline inflation forecasts are lower compared with June for India (-0.5pp), the Philippines (-0.5pp), Indonesia (-0.4pp) and Malaysia (-0.1pp) but up for Singapore (+0.8pp), Thailand (0.3pp) and Taiwan (+0.1pp).

• For Singapore specifically, our headline inflation forecasts mask a general reduction in our core CPI projections – we have lowered these to 2.7% from 3.0% for 2024 and to 1.9% from 2.0% for 2025.

In contrast to the 2025 rebound in the regional aggregate however – which China tends to dominate – the simple average of our forecasts shows a drop from 3.2% in 2023 to 2.2% in 2024 and to 2.1% in 2025.

• We estimate the simple average of Emerging Asian food inflation has cooled further, falling to 3.0% y/y in July from 3.7% in Q4 23 – possibly reflecting a lagged efect of earlier declines in global food prices – which is still notably higher than the 2015-19 average of 2.7%.

• This has been partly ofset by the y/y changes in energy costs turning northward, reflecting the base efects from last year's drop in Brent crude oil prices towards USD75/bbl in May-June – though this looks likely to flip towards disinflation in the coming months. Our estimates indicate the simple average of the region's energy costs rose by 1.8% y/y in July, compared with Q4 23 when they fell by 1.0%.

• More fundamentally, we estimate the simple average of core inflation in the region cooled to 2.0% y/y in July – back to its 2016-19 average – from 2.6% in Q4 23. We suspect continued progress towards the untangling of supply-side bottlenecks and normalising inflation expectations have helped to moderate price pressures across the region despite still-low unemployment rates and relatively tight labour market conditions.

## With greater comfort around inflation has come room for monetary policy easing in some Emerging Asian economies.

• Growing confidence that price pressures were contained led to the BSP, on 15 August, being the first central bank in the region outside of China to cut its policy rate – not only ahead of the Fed but also despite a surprise jump in the Philippines' headline inflation print for July.

• The drop in Korean inflation – which reached 2.0% y/y, the BoK's target, in August – has removed price pressures as an impediment for rate cuts, leaving financial stability as the remaining, but not insubstantial, hurdle.

FIGURE 26. Latest CPI readings versus central bank comfort zones
![](images/b70a81e07745f75132e506d7cc6f940e17bb3ec33e85e62f43fe4432a5f7bfa3.jpg)
Note: Figures refer to latest headline or core CPI reading. For Indonesia and Thailand, we compare core inflation readings with their central banks' headline inflation targets as we believe policymakers are currently more focused on the core measure. For Singapore and Malaysia, where there is no inflation-targeting regime, we assume a comfort zone of 2% for core inflation.
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

That said, not all are ready to ease – some central banks remain wary of complacency over inflation dynamics.

• We doubt the RBI will view the downshift in headline inflation to 3.5% y/y in July from 5.1% in June as a green light for rate cuts – not when it is so clearly the result of base efects that will reverse in a matter of months. Suficient clarity on price pressures – following the end of the monsoon and harvest season – should only come by December for rate cuts to start (see India: Lifting inflation fog reveals path to December pivot, 3 September 2024).

• Despite the recent surprisingly sharp drop in Singapore's core inflation, to 2.4% y/y in July, we believe the MAS will focus on underlying price pressures – which remain elevated – and hold of on FX policy easing. The downside surprise in core CPI was driven largely by a sharp drop in travel costs – which, due to the volatility of this segment, we expect the MAS to look through (see Singapore: July CPI – Don't trip over travel, 23 August 2024).

• With core inflation at 1.9% y/y as of July in Malaysia, BNM should be sitting comfortable on the CPI front – but, in our assessment, it is the most hawkish major central bank in the region at the moment because it is ready to hike if a RON95 petrol price hike sparks of broader second-round inflation efects. To be clear, our base case remains that the government faces significant political challenges in hiking RON95 petrol prices in its current term. However, the risk that this materialises and ignites second-round inflation efects – potentially requiring rate hikes – will likely keep BNM wary of easing policy prematurely.

## Financial stability: The often underappreciated factor

And then there is financial stability – a theme that we see increasingly confounding monetary policy considerations at more and more central banks in Emerging Asia in the current cycle.

At the risk of oversimplification, one way to understand the relevance of financial stability for monetary policy is not as a mysterious "third factor", separate from growth and inflation, but as part of the growth factor itself – specifically, in terms of the tradeof between near- and medium-term growth.

• Lower interest rates support near-term economic activity by encouraging households and firms to borrow for the purpose of consuming and investing today – resulting in a build-up of debt, which will not only constrain how much more they can borrow tomorrow, but also requires the servicing of that debt out of future income, weighing on medium-term economic activity.

• At the same time, lower interest rates – by not only reducing the cost of leverage but also encouraging a hunt for yield – can spur excessive risk-taking, raising the risk of a significant negative growth shock should a financial-stability event occur in the future, such as a bursting of asset bubbles.

While these efects on medium-term growth might be manageable when debt levels are low and risk-taking is manageable, the trade-ofs can become more serious when this is no longer the case.

• Under such circumstances, rate cuts might be growth-positive today but ultimately – and more spectacularly – growth-negative tomorrow.

• As a corollary to this, a rate hike might also be growth-negative today, but – by averting future disaster – be overall growth-positive tomorrow.

A near-term slowdown in economic activity in the specific areas where financial-stability issues have arisen is therefore often the point – not a reason to cut rates. That said, a lot depends on how much weight a central bank places on the near- and medium-term in its intertemporal allocation of economic growth. Should the deleveraging or de-risking process prove too economically painful in the near term – potentially jeopardising the medium-term outlook, which tight monetary policy was meant to secure – policymakers could change their minds.

In practical terms, the financial-stability imperative can thus manifest itself in two seemingly surprising forms of hawkishness.

• Central banks wary of a buildup of financial-stability risks can hike rates despite no obvious need to do so based on the near-term outlook for economic activity and consumer prices.

This was most recently demonstrated by BNM – the central bank we view as most focused on financial stability in the region – which surprised many market participants by starting its rate-hiking cycle in May 2022 without even waiting to see the post-pandemic reopening lift in the economic data (see Malaysia: BNM – Financial stability first, 7 May 2024).

° <sup>Likewise,</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>BoT</sup> <sup>carried</sup> <sup>on</sup> <sup>with</sup> <sup>rate</sup> <sup>hikes</sup> <sup>through</sup> <sup>late</sup> <sup>2023</sup> <sup>even</sup> <sup>as</sup> <sup>economic</sup> <sup>activity</sup> was starting to weaken (see Thailand: BoT – Many objectives, one priority, 7 May 2024).

• Central banks may also prove more cautious than usual to cut rates, even when economic activity is softening – which likely is the current regime prevailing in Korea, Taiwan and Thailand.

° <sup>Both</sup> <sup>growth</sup> <sup>and</sup> <sup>inflation</sup> <sup>already</sup> <sup>point</sup> <sup>to</sup> <sup>rate</sup> <sup>cuts</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>Korea</sup> <sup>–</sup> <sup>but</sup> <sup>BoK</sup> <sup>Governor</sup> <sup>Rhee</sup> Changyong has been warning of the costs of provoking greater property market speculation in Seoul, raising the risk of a delayed start to monetary policy easing. Since the BoK's policy meeting in July, property prices in the prime areas of Seoul have gained traction, and even apartment prices on the outskirts of Seoul are starting to increase at a faster pace. As noted above, our base case of an October cut assumes a cooling of the residential property market in Korea after a tightening of macroprudential

measures in September, which implies the risk of a delay should concerns of overheating persist (see Korea: August CPI – Finally back at 2%, 3 September 2024).

° <sup>Likewise,</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>CBC</sup> <sup>is</sup> <sup>likely</sup> <sup>far</sup> <sup>from</sup> <sup>ready</sup> <sup>to</sup> <sup>ease</sup> <sup>monetary</sup> <sup>policy</sup> <sup>–</sup> <sup>not</sup> <sup>after</sup> <sup>it</sup> <sup>hiked</sup> banks’ RRR by 25bp in June and with home prices remaining on a tear in Taiwan. While the CBC also tightened macroprudential policy on the residential property market in June, this is running up against government eforts to encourage home ownership, complicating the central bank's eforts to contain financial-stability risks on this front. With economic activity still holding up, the CBC is likely to stay patient and only start cutting rates next year (see Taiwan: CBC – Policy focus on property sector, 13 June 2024).

As noted above, the BoT has not seemed to be its usual pro-growth self since the pandemic, when household debt surged from around 80% of GDP to 95% at its peak before stabilising at a still-high 90%. BoT Governor Sethaput Suthiwartnarueput has been arguing that a rate cut will not do much to reinvigorate economic activity in Thailand and that the kingdom's troubles are more structural in nature – an argument we view as suggesting that the buildup of household debt has been so severe that the near-term benefits of a rate cut for growth are now in doubt. Therefore, our forecast remains that the BoT is unlikely to cut rates even into 2025 – though, of course, the central bank could change its mind should the deleveraging process prove too economically painful.

Even if BNM were to conclude that the government will not hike RON95 petrol prices – taking the inflation constraint out of the picture – we would still be sceptical on financial-stability grounds that rate cuts are coming in Malaysia. Where some investors might see an opportunity for BNM to take advantage of benign inflation to further support growth with a rate cut, we believe the central bank is more likely to see such policy easing as an unnecessary fanning of financial-stability risks – especially given that after the pandemic the policy rate was only raised to its 2019 level of 3.00%, a setting that is not obviously restrictive.

° <sup>To</sup> <sup>the</sup> <sup>extent</sup> <sup>that</sup> <sup>a</sup> <sup>sharp</sup> <sup>fall</sup> <sup>in</sup> <sup>Singapore's</sup> <sup>domestic</sup> <sup>interest</sup> <sup>rates</sup> <sup>might</sup> <sup>be</sup> <sup>viewed</sup> as potentially reigniting the property market, policymakers there may also be reluctant to see such a slide occur. While the MAS has chosen to focus on the exchange rate as its policy instrument, in practice the central bank appears to be able to exert some control over domestic interest rates, likely through its management of liquidity conditions. With Prime Minister Lawrence Wong choosing to highlight the need to improve housing afordability in his first National Day Rally speech on 18 August and macroprudential measures being tightening on 20 August, we believe policymakers are likely to keep a close eye on domestic interest rates (see Singapore: Property market measures – A demonstration of resolve, 20 August 2024).

## Politics: What we are watching

• Incoming Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto will be inaugurated on 20 October – and  there will likely be intense focus on his choice of finance minister and what this means for fiscal policy. Our view remains that the incoming Prabowo administration is unlikely to tinker with the fiscal rules limiting the deficit to 3% of GDP and government debt to 60% of GDP. That said, the risk is of continued noise around fiscal policy that keeps investors on edge.

• In Thailand, the increase in political noise we had warned about in the June Emerging Markets Quarterly culminated with the surprise removal of Prime Minister Srettha Thavisin on 14 August – and the almost equally sudden appointment of his successor, Paetongtarn Shinawatra, daughter of former Prime Minister Thaksin Shinawatra. We believe some near-term stability may be at hand as the new prime minister takes charge. However, the risk that political noise returns remains, especially as July 2025 approaches – the scheduled start for Mr Shinawatra's lèse-majesté court case.

• Our base case is for Malaysia's will-they-won't-they on RON95 petrol price hikes to continue to yield a lack of clarity, even at the upcoming 18 October budget. With Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim's allies having voiced concerns that the diesel price hike in June may have led to a by-election defeat, the government will likely find reasons in the recent strengthening of the currency and continued gradual fiscal consolidation – even after accounting for the state of Sarawak's takeover of gas distribution there from state-owned oil company Petronas – to delay action on RON95 subsidies.

• There are still few signs that Singapore's next general election will be called imminently – in line with our base case for polls to only be called next year, once there is more progress in bringing down residential property prices and core inflation. Even so, these cost pressures will likely remain challenging issues that Prime Minister Lawrence Wong and the ruling People's Action Party (PAP) will have to address in the coming polls.

## China: Weaker data to trigger more easing

Jian Chang, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Yingke Zhou, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Ying Zhang, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore

We think the fast slowdown in investment and production, weak consumption thanks to a weakening labour market, contracting private-loan demand and falling home prices have fuelled concerns of a Japan-style balance-sheet recession. Risks to our 4.8% GDP forecast are tilted to the downside, barring bolder and concerted policy support. We expect GDP growth to slow further, to 4% in 2025, versus Bloomberg consensus of 4.5%. We think the boost to consumption from the reported plan to allow homeowners to refinance their mortgages<sup>3</sup> would be limited, based on past experience and surveys. Our base case expects monetary and fiscal policy to provide incremental support, instead of large and aggressive stimulus.

• Credit: Despite a worrisome macro backdrop, we think there could be select opportunities in HG China corporates focused on China’s new growth engine credits, including TMTs, and idiosyncratic stories in corporates and asset management companies (AMCs). We expect the sector to continue to demonstrate its defensive characteristics, along with improvement in earnings and fundamentals. However, we see potential overhangs, including headline noise around US-China relations as US elections progress.

• FX: We expect USDCNY to rebound following its recent positioning unwind-led move lower, with limited follow-through below 7.10, below which PBoC eforts to curb CNY appreciation should intensify. The reluctance to boost demand-side stimulus, combined with monetary policy easing amid still-wide US/China rate diferentials, still points to a higher USDCNY. As such, we maintain our long USDCNH trade idea. We turn neutral on CFETS, after the recent quick fall and likely step-up in central-bank intervention. We think levels recovering to 100 would look attractive to turn bearish over the coming quarters.

• Rates: We expect a steeper CGB yield curve as the PBoC tightens its grip on both ends of the curve and the market digests the central bank's selling of borrowed bonds. We see limited risks of any sharp upside move in yields given still-soft growth and ongoing deflationary pressures. 1) Further policy rate and RRR cuts and 2) the PBoC's bond management will keep the front end of the curve under downward pressure. We expect 3y and 10y CGB yields to consolidate around 1.5% and 2.1%, respectively, by Q4 24, and 1.50% and 2.20% by Q3 25, respectively. We continue to like 10y30y CGB steepeners.

Following a big miss in the Q2 GDP data, we think the weak July-August activity data suggest risks to our 4.8% GDP growth forecast are tilted to the downside. The fast slowdown in investment and production amid deteriorating profitability, weak consumption thanks to a weakeing labour market, contracting private loan demand, and falling home prices, suggest a gloomy growth outlook in the remainder of H2.

Policy support announced in past months, including trade-ins of consumers goods and stateled housing inventory de-stocking still have not moved the needle, and failed to boost consumer and business confidence and stabilise the housing market. We look for growth momentum to slow to c.4.5% q/q saar (4.6% y/y) on average in H2 from 5.3% q/q saar (5.0% y/y) in H1 in view of weakened private consumption, the prolonged slump in property activity and sustained decline in home prices amid a widespread absence of confidence, and moderating

manufacturing investment amid slower export growth and weaker domestic demand. Moreover, we expect GDP growth to slow further to 4% in 2025, versus Bloomberg consensus of 4.5%.

We think the growth slowdown reflects more deeply rooted secular issues. The collapse of the housing bubble has ravaged household balance sheets and spurred a deleveraging cycle that may be only half done. Even with interest rates being cut to record lows, there are still not enough households willing to borrow for consumption or to buy property. Households are experiencing a painful period of balance-sheet repair, which we think could easily take a decade (or longer) to complete, drawing from the Japanese and US experience. Moreover, private firms have become more cautious in terms of: 1) taking on more loans and credit; and 2) increasing capex, despite regulators' stepped-up eforts to support credit growth. Moreover, we see some worrisome signs of a vicious cycle between wages and prices, with declining wage growth/rising unemployment coming hand-in-hand with falling price indicators (GDP deflator stayed negative for the fifth consecutive quarter in Q2).

We think the risk of entrenched deflation expectations is rising. On the surface, China's headline CPI inflation rose for a second straight month to 0.6% y/y in August. If we take out the contribution from volatile food prices, we estimate consumer goods inflation was actually around 0%, nearly falling into deflation. Moreover, August core CPI hit a 42-month low, and PPI deflation worsened. The broad-based moderation in prices of most non-food segments - healthcare, recreation, housing and transportation - suggests a risk that deflation expectations could become entrenched. In this respect, we think it is worth highlighting that the GDP deflator (the broadest measure of prices in the domestic economy) has now been negative for five consecutive quarters, the longest period of negative readings on record. We expect the GDP deflator to remain negative for at least another two quarters (Q3-Q4). Moreover, we think the housing rental CPI is not fully capturing underlying trends in that sector, as private data showed a 1.9% decline in rental prices in August versus a 0.3% drop in the oficial data.

In response to the bleak outlook for economic fundamentals, the government is considering cutting interest rates on existing mortgages in two steps to lower borrowing costs for households and boost consumption. According to Bloomberg<sup>4</sup> , under the plan, financial regulators have proposed reducing rates on outstanding mortgages by a total of 80bp, with the first cut coming in the next few weeks and the second move taking efect at the beginning of 2025. By our estimate, if the interest rates on existing mortgages were lowered by 80bp, it could lead to up to \~CNY250bn in annual savings on household mortgage payments.

According to a survey by the PBoC's Chongqing Branch in July, only \~30% of the residents plan to use the saved interest expenses to increase consumption. The small percentage likely reflects the fact that a majority of households are likely to use saved interest payment either to repay their mortgage early or save for future use.

Our back-of-envelop calculation suggests it would provide a limited boost to annual consumption of \~CNY80bn, or 0.1-0.2pp in annual retail sales, assuming the 30% of afected households fully use saved interest payment on consumption. Indeed, past experience suggests the boost to consumption may not be visible in a downturn. After the last round of cuts in existing mortgage rates by \~70bp, implemented in Q4 23, retail sales growth slowed by 2.5pp, to 4.7% y/y, in Q1 24.

Our base case expects monetary and fiscal policy to provide incremental support, instead of large and aggressive stimulus. Following rate cuts of 10bp in the 7d OMO rate – the main policy rate – in July, we expect the PBoC to deliver another three 10bp cuts in the 7d OMO rate in Q4 this year as well as in Q1 and Q2 25, as economic fundamentals weaken and the widely expected Fed rate cuts (likely to start in September) ofer a window for the PBoC to cut rates. However, we think the pace of rate cuts will be measured, given that the record low level of banks' net interest margin remains a constraint. Moreover, we expect the PBoC to deliver a 50bp cut in banks’ reserve requirement ratio (RRR) in the coming weeks, likely in September, followed by another 50bp cut in H1 25. Speaking at a press conference on 5 September, Zou Lan, head of the PBoC's monetary policy department, said: 1) there is still space for more cuts considering the average RRR is at about 7%; and 2) there are constraints on decreasing deposit and lending rates further due to narrowing bank profit margins and money flowing into asset management products.

On the fiscal front, we expect the MoF to continue to accelerate the issuance of localgovernment special bonds (66% of quota was issued in the first eight months of the year) and complete the planned full-year issuance by late October or early November, which could lend some support to infrastructure investment.

Our base case expects growth in infrastructure investment to remain robust, at 8%, in H2 following \~8% growth in both H1 24 and in 2023. While it is not our base case, we think the risk of additional CNY1trn issuance of special treasury bonds by the end of this year cannot not be ruled out, if growth slips too far below the oficial target of 5%.
