## EM Asia Frontier Economies:

Credit Strategy: Avanti Save, CFA, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Economics: Amruta Ghare, BSIPL, India

## Credit Strategy

• Mongolia: We retain a Market Weight rating on the sovereign. Valuations screen tight compared with other single-Bs but the sovereign's credit trajectory is stable-to-improving. Given this, we recommend switching out of MONGOL '28s into PNGIB '28s to pick up 240bp in yield for a similar rating bucket and commodity-exporter exposure.

• Sri Lanka: We retain our Overweight rating. The SRILAN bonds have sold-of steadily since the Joint Working Framework was announced in July and as election-related noise has ramped up. Even though posturing and politicking around the agreement is likely in the runup to the elections, we do not expect the new President to renegotiate the agreed bond restructuring terms. Exiting the default state for the country will remain a focus for the new President, in our view.

• Pakistan : We maintain our Overweight rating. Confirmation of IMF executive board approval for the new package should support valuations, even though the complex is  now trading inline with bonds of peer sovereigns. We remain watchful of the recent uptick in political noise, especially as the coalition is experiencing stresses from the rising cost pressures imposed by the FY25 budget.

• Papua New Guinea (Not Covered): The external position continues to benefit from commodity exports with foreign reserves at nearly six months of imports. The PNGIB 8.375% '28s (B2/B-) are not very liquid but are quoted c.9%. At these levels, we think they ofer value versus similar tenor Mongolia bonds.

## Mongolia: Sailing smoothly

Valuations fair, stay Market Weight. We have a constructive view on the fundamental backdrop for Mongolia, and expect the sovereign's credit ratings to gradually improve amid likely limited pressure from bond supply. However, compared with the start of the year, we think the good news is mostly priced-in. The MONGOL bonds are now trading broadly in line with other low-single B sovereigns and tighter than mid-single Bs.

Foreign reserves of USD4.7bn end-August. The country's external position has improved on the increase in commodity exports and structural eforts to improve shipment of product (such as accelerating the cross-border railway projects).  In recent years, Mongolia has benefited from steady access to concessional funding from multilateral and bilateral partners. The current account deficit is projected to be 4% of GDP (Fitch Ratings).

Credit ratings on upward trajectory: Mongolia is rated B/B3/B all Stable. Based on the favourable macro backdrop, we think a one-notch rating upgrade is possible before the end of this year. We think a big factor in favour of such rating actions is a likely falling debt burden.

Bond supply limited. We believe the sovereign will be opportunistic about issuing bonds. We also believe that any issuance is more likely to be accompanied with a liability management exercise.

## Pakistan: Budget, IMF, Overweight

Ambitious targets. The Finance Minister, Muhammad Aurangzeb, set a deficit target of 6.9% of GDP for the FY25 budget (federal)<sup>5</sup> . Notably, the expected fiscal consolidation is underpinned by a tax collection target of PKR13trn, which the government aims to achieve by raising taxes and removing tax exemptions. For reference, the revised budget for FY24 had a target of PKR8.4trn in tax revenues (original target was PKR7.5trn).

Debt burden constraining fiscal space. The FY25 budget includes higher expenditure mainly due to larger interest payments and higher subsidies to the power sector. Interest payments are estimated to be PKR9.8bn (89% for domestic debt), which is equivalent to 55% of the government's target for tax and non-tax revenues in FY25. While Pakistan's stock of external commercial debt is not at alarming levels, it is worth noting that ongoing access to funds and robust economic growth are necessary to keep debt within sustainable levels. In this context, we estimate that the external debt servicing costs for FY25 will total around USD25bn; we believe the government plans to rely on rollovers from bilaterals to meet these costs.

## Awaiting IMF executive board approval for Extended Fund Facility. Even though the staf-

level agreement was achieved in July<sup>6</sup> , the USD7bn EFF has yet to receive IMF board approval<sup>7</sup> . Prime Minister Sharif has continued to indicate a commitment to the programme but IMF approval is contingent upon confirmation of financing assurances from bilateral partners. In addition, local press has reported that lack of additional financing  and unpaid energy subsidies has resulted in the delay<sup>8</sup> .

See Pakistan: Budget, IMF, Overweight, 18 June 2024 for full details.

Ranil Wickremsinghe, SJB: Samagi Jana Balawegaya (party of candidate Sajith Premadasa), AKD: Anura Kumara Dissanayake Source: Barclays Research
Sri Lanka: Election season
FIGURE 32. Potential timelines for bonds exchange
![](images/5f8a3ea635240b1446c6ae5d8ab6c846216530bf4948ee2e72da381c6593b3a8.jpg)

Election-related newsflow increasing. The presidential election on 21 September has assumed greater importance as it will be the first to be held since the country's economic crisis erupted in 2022 and with multiple candidates, the likelihood of a second count has risen.

Second and third preferences of voters will likely matter. In the event of no candidate securing a straight majority, voters' second and third preferences of the eliminated candidates will be counted for the respective top two candidates.

Uncertainty about results. The increasing popularity of Anura Kumara Dissanayake (AKD), a left-leaning candidate, may raise concerns about potential implications for the IMF programme and bond restructuring, based on his previous criticisms of the some terms of the agreements<sup>1</sup>.

But renegotiating IMF programme or bond deal likely not on the agenda. While election timeline and potential outcomes are likely to weigh on sentiment, we do not expect the new President to renegotiate the agreed bond restructuring terms. Exiting the default state for the country will remain a focus for the new President, in our view.

See Sri Lanka: Election season, 13 August 2024, and Sri Lanka: Re-timed, 9 September 2024.

## Maldives: Heightened debt repayment pressures

Economics: Forex reserves continue to dwindle, reflecting elevated trade deficit and debt servicing requirements. As of July, forex reserves had reduced to cover just about 1.3 months of imports (at USD395.4mn, compared with USD590.5bn in end-2023). Signs of dollar shortage were evident in: 1) media reports suggesting that usable forex reserves were likely to become negative by end-August<sup>9</sup> ; and 2) the Bank of Maldives (BML, a national bank) suspending dollar transactions on bank cards on 25 August (a move which was reversed the same day)<sup>10</sup> . The Maldives Monetary Authority denied the media reports, stating that the country's dollar reserves were expected to rise by the end of August<sup>11</sup>  . At the same time President Muizzu claimed that BML's decision was an attempted coup against the government<sup>12</sup> .

Near-term relief on debt repayments, but concerns around elevated external debt persist. The decline in forex reserves has raised questions over the Maldives repayment ability for its ’ external debts: the sovereign credit rating was recently downgraded on concerns over external financing and liquidity <sup>13</sup> . The World Bank estimates debt service costs of USD512mn will be due in 2024 and 2025<sup>14</sup> . Additionally, the country’s monthly import bill averages around USD283mn. Taken together, this is much higher than the current reported level of forex reserves.

However, the Maldives has several ways to deal with its impending debt and trade repayments. The country's largest external lender, China, was earlier this year reportedly considering granting concessionary terms or a grace period of five years on all due interest and principal payments (bilateral external debt with China accounts for 20% of total central government debt)<sup>15</sup> . Further, the government could renew currency swap lines for its import payments, as well as the likely enactment of an FTA with China in September<sup>16</sup>  to facilitate local currency payments for Chinese imports (11.5% of total merchandise imports). Near term, a USD50mn

treasury bill subscribed by a public sector bank in India is set to mature in September, and it remains to be seen if this debt will be rolled over in a similar fashion to one in May 2024<sup>17</sup> . We think  bilateral financial support will likely be required to avoid a potentially disruptive outcome.

Implementation of subsidy reforms proposed in the Budget is likely to be delayed: The phasing out of the universal-subsidy programme to more targeted direct income transfers was initially supposed to come into efect in July 2024. However, the change is yet to be implemented, with no oficial word on the timeline. This implies spending pressures will likely continue (subsidies account for \~9.5% of revenue expenditure), and may slow the pace of fiscal consolidation in 2024

## Papua New Guinea: Favourable export outlook, but debt service issues remain

Economics: Papua New Guinea’s growth outlook for 2024 received a boost from the reopening of gold mine operations in Porgera<sup>18</sup>  in December 2023, but lower LNG prices pose downside risks to export values. Production from the Porgera gold mine and the upward trend in international gold prices in 2024 are expected to contribute to positively to PNG’s current account surplus and revenue receipts. However, a landslide near the mine in May 2024 (which temporarily interrupted access) and civil unrest earlier in the year (January 2024) may have dampened the growth momentum<sup>19</sup> .

Fiscal consolidation on track, but medium-term debt payments pose risk of debt distress. The government has managed to reduce the budget deficit consistently since the pandemic (2023: 4.4% of GDP; 2022: 5.3%), but debt remains elevated (2023: 52.6% of GDP, 2022: 48.3%). The IMF estimates PNG’s debt service payments in 2026 and 2028 could pose a liquidity risk and hence classified the economy at high risk of debt distress in its second review of the EEF/ECF for PNG (July 2024).

## Despite a large current account surplus, PNG is at risk of FX shortages, necessitating

depreciation of the PGK<sup>20</sup> .  PNG's CA surplus is supported by a sizeable trade surplus, but forex inflows are lower than what export numbers would suggest, as some proceeds are kept ofshore (in 2023, foreign currency inflows amounted to USD5.6bn compared with export receipts of \~USD14bn). Combined with forex outflows (USD7.2bn in 2023, in part from repatriation of profits by foreign stakeholders in LNG plants), this leads to a net dollar shortage. The government has accordingly operationalised a gradual depreciation of the Kina since January 2024, which was also part of the reforms required under the IMF’s programme. That said, FX reserves as of June 2024 cover over six months of imports, which is considered adequate as per IMF definitions<sup>21</sup> .

## Hong Kong: Not out of the woods

Ying Zhang, Barclays Bank | Yingke Zhou, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Jian Chang, Barclays Bank, Hong Kong | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore

Although Hong Kong’s Q2 GDP growth accelerated to 3.3% y/y from 2.8% the prior quarter, the sequential picture is not as upbeat; economic expansion eased notably to 0.4% q/q sa versus 2.5% previously, and exports, deemed one of the main drivers of the economy, reversed to fall for the first time since Q4 2022. Moreover, private consumption remained soft and investment declined for a third quarter. While a Fed rate cut may ofer some relief to Hong Kong’s economy, we see extended softness in domestic demand and the property market. Uncertainties also remain from any slowdown in external demand or any tarif escalation between China and major economies.

• FX: We expect USDHKD to stay close to the middle of the 7.75-7.85 convertibility undertaking, before falling into end-2024 amid tailwinds from Fed rate cuts and Q4 seasonality. In the near term, pressure on the HKD is expected to continue as positioning in carry trades has lightened up, and we think the market will likely try to add back long USDHKD exposures even in the initial stages of the Fed's expected gradual cutting cycle. Further out, we expect the Fed cutting cycle to weigh on USDHKD to trade in a 7.75-7.80 range in 2025.

• Rates: We expect HK rates to bear flatten in the near term, before bull steepening after the Fed starts its easing cycle. Short tenors should inch higher, reflecting the uneven distribution of domestic liquidity, despite short-tenor US rates likely being anchored. We recommend a pay 1y HKD IRS trade, entry 3.58, target 4.14, stop loss 3.30

Hong Kong’s economy grew 3.3% y/y in Q2, up from 2.8% in Q1, surpassing both consensus and our forecast (Bloomberg: 2.7%, Barclays: 2.5%). Brisk exports contributed to the continued expansion in GDP, ofsetting the slowdown in private consumption and drag from the sluggish property market. That said, the sequential picture is not as upbeat. GDP growth slowed to 0.4% q/q in Q2 from 2.5% in Q1, and exports fell for the first time since Q4 2022. The start of the Fed rate cut cycle, which is expected to begin this month, should provide some relief for Hong Kong's economy. However, we think uncertainties remain from: 1) the risk of slowing external demand and further escalation in the imposition of tit-for-tat tarifs by China and major developed economies; 2) likely extended softness in private consumption amid a relatively strong currency and changing consumption patterns of both residents and visitors; 3) continued drag from the property downturn; and 4) geopolitical tensions, the slowing mainland economy and still-elevated borrowing costs, which may depress business confidence and weigh on investment.

On consumption, retail sales by value fell to HKD29bn in July, the lowest level in almost two years. That marked the fifth straight monthly decline (which returned to double digits) following 15 months of expansion after China reopened in December 2022. We expect weakness in consumption to extend through the rest of this year. In particular, according to the latest survey from Hong Kong Retail Management Association, which covers around 4,000 retailers with 780,000 employees, 90% of the polled members experienced sluggish sales in August, and 60% of surveyed retailers expect a single-digit to as much as a 40% fall in sales in September and October<sup>22</sup> .

We think deteriorating household balance sheets amid falling home prices and a weak stock market will continue to weigh on local demand. Changing consumption patterns in both

residents and visitors also pose further challenges. First, Hong Kong residents are flocking to neighbouring cities such as Shenzhen due to the availability of high-quality services for lower prices amid the stronger HKD. Second, visitors are not spending at their pre-COVID levels. In particular, while visitor arrivals in H1 24 reached 69% of the level in the same period in 2018, visitor spending, as measured by exports of travel services, recovered to only 51% of the 2018 level. In addition, the latest data showed that the sales of discretionary goods, such as jewellery, watches and valuable gifts, fell 25% y/y in July following a 23% drop in June. Amid weak sales, Hong Kong's government has rolled out various measures to spur consumption, including hosting over 100 “mega events” in H2 24 to attract tourists and continuing to support retailers<sup>23</sup> .

The property market continues to trend downward as boosts from February's property-easing measures wear of. Residential transaction volume fell for the fourth month in a row, to 3,654 units, in August. That was a 1.9% m/m drop from July and was the lowest level in half a year. Falling property prices also add to fragile homebuyer sentiment. Overall property prices dropped almost 5% from December 2023 to July 2024, and are down 25% from the recent peak in September 2021. The Centa-City Leading Index, which measures secondary private residential property prices in Hong Kong, also shows that home prices have trended downward since May. Moreover, amid sluggish sales and fierce price competition, with developers extending discounts to attract homebuyers, one of Hong Kong's largest property developers, New World Development, warned of its first annual loss in two decades<sup>24</sup> .

Overall, we expect the property market to remain soft for the rest of the year. A Fed rate cut cycle, which likely kicks of in September, may ofer some relief to Hong Kong's property market, and we note some banks have already resumed ofering mortgage rebates in anticipation of an increase in transactions following the Fed move<sup>25</sup> . That said, we think the still-elevated mortgage rates and subdued labour market, especially in the financial sector, will hold back any substantial rebound in property market this year.

On the external sector, merchandise exports rose in double digits for the fourth straight month, with the pace accelerating to 13.1% y/y in July versus 10.7% in June, supported by relatively resilient demand from DMs and major Asean countries. That said, we think challenges persist, and downside risks could come from any slowdown in external demand or potential tarif escalation between China and the US/EU.

Corporates are experiencing more liquidity dificulties amid the prolonged property downturn and slowing demand. Tighter loan standards at banks are adding to the financial stress, and weakening balance sheets. As per data from the Oficial Receiver's Ofice, 780 bankruptcy petitions were filed in July, which marked a 17% increase from the same month last year. Courtissued bankruptcy orders also saw a 54% y/y increase in the month. Against this backdrop, financial institutions are becoming more cautious due to increasing credit risks. The reported NPL ratio for Hang Seng Bank, a major local bank in Hong Kong, surged to 5.32% as at H1 2024, from 0.2% in 2019. Meanwhile, bad loans associated with the government’s 100% Loan Guarantee Scheme have risen by 76% from last October to HKD13.2bn currently, making an NPL ratio of 9.2%<sup>26</sup> . As the value of property used as collateral for loans is falling,  at least 20 firms across a range of industries have been asked by banks for additional collateral or early loan repayments<sup>27</sup> . To tackle complaints around financing struggles, the Hong Kong Monetary

Authority (HKMA) established a joint Taskforce on SME lending in August<sup>28</sup> . Fourteen banks active in lending to small firms have vowed to step up their lending eforts<sup>29</sup> .

## India: Primed for a pivot

Shreya Sodhani, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Amruta Ghare, BSIPL, India | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Imtiaz Shefuddin, Barclays Bank, Singapore

India's growth mix is re-balancing, with a pickup in private consumption and exports in Q2 24. A favourable monsoon so far bodes well for a revival for rural consumption, but a broad-based uptick in private investment is not yet visible. Inflation is likely to trend lower over the remainder of the fiscal year, primarily driven by lower food inflation. We expect the RBI to embark on rate cuts from December, once it gains more confidence in the trajectory of inflation.

• Credit: We think India HY corporates (Overweight) are likely continue to be supported by solid credit profiles helped by their proactive management of operating costs and strong balance sheets. In addition, supportive technicals on another year of likely limited supply should drive demand for the sector’s outperformance over the remainder of the year. In the India HG corporate sector (Market Weight), we think improvements in corporate credit profiles will likely be muted due to high interest costs and weakness in FX, while the economic expansion should remain broadly supportive of the sector.

• FX: We expect USDINR to continue to gradually edge higher over the forecast horizon to around 84.60 by end Q3 2025, but for INR to outperform other Asian FX on any USD rebound. India’s attractive growth story implies continued inflows, in both equities and bonds. However, we expect the RBI to continue to absorb capital inflows, resulting in a further strengthening in FX reserves, while capping potential INR appreciation pressures.

• Rates: We expect IGB yields to extend their declines over the forecast horizon, albeit at a modest pace, driven by favourable demand/supply dynamics, monetary policy easing, and commitment towards fiscal consolidation. We expect 10y IGB yields to reach 6.75% by end-Q4 24 and move gradually lower to 6.50% by end-Q3 25. Similarly, we look for 5y IGB yields to fall to 6.65% by end-Q4 24 and 6.40% by end-Q3 25.  We recommend long 10y IGBs.

We expect India's GDP growth to hold up in FY2024-25 at 7%, though some signs of a deceleration in activity were visible in Q1. Recently released data showed GDP growth slowed to 6.7% y/y in Q1 FY24-25 (Q2 24), from 7.8% in the previous quarter. The slowdown was driven by: 1) lower profits by manufacturing firms as input costs were higher y/y; 2) lower government spending in Q1 (due to elections), which has admittedly driven overall investment for the past two years; 3) slower growth in 'net taxes' revenues (taxes minus subsidies). An acceleration in private consumption, exports, and fixed investment were positive signs, indicative of growth turning more balanced. For the rest of the year, we expect a pickup in private consumption, driven more by rural areas, as inflation moderates further and assuming a normal monsoon. Urban demand momentum is likely slowing from elevated levels last year, as visible in lower auto sales and power consumption in recent months. Within investment, we think the much-anticipated pickup in private investment is yet to be seen clearly: while capacity utilisation continues to rise, other indicators such as growth in bank credit to industry and business confidence indices have come down.

We maintain our CPI inflation forecast at 4.3% for FY2024-25, slowing from 5.4% in FY2023-24. Even discounting the recent base-efect led moderation in inflation in July, we think inflation is on a downward trend for the rest of the year, barring some weather or commodity price shock. The moderation is likely to be driven by lower food prices, given the above-average monsoon so far (which should support kharif production) and increase in reservoir storage

(which bodes well for the rabi crop harvest). Elevated food prices over the past year have proven to be persistent and sticky, rising by more than the usual seasonal upticks. In contrast, core inflation, which touched a series low in May, has likely bottomed. We expect core to rise in Q3 and Q4 FY24-25, but the march upwards is likely to be contained, and will average lower in FY24-25 compared with the previous year.

We continue to expect the RBI to embark on monetary easing from December. The MPC’s communication has remained firmly focused on inflation: the committee has maintained policy settings for more than a year now, as elevated food inflation prevented a durable trend in disinflation in headline CPI. The MPC is wary of this spilling into non-food components, though we think the evidence on this is limited so far. Households' inflation expectations have risen, and the RBI’s business surveys also indicate that firms expect increases in input costs and selling prices, giving further reason for the MPC to remain cautious. That said, in our view, the inflation outlook justifies a December rate cut as: 1) food inflation is trending lower, with favourable monsoon conditions and clarity over kharif production; and 2) only a gradual rise in core inflation is not threatening the alignment of headline to target. Uncertainty on the policy path may increase as the terms of external MPC members expire in Q3 CY2024, while the terms of Governor Das and Deputy Governor Patra are set to expire in December 2024 and January 2025, respectively.

The final Union budget for FY24-25 presented in July signalled policy continuity from the new government. The fiscal consolidation agenda is proposed to be accelerated, with a budgeted fiscal deficit for FY24-25 at 4.9% of GDP (Interim budget: 5.1%, FY23-24: 5.6%). The government expects to reduce the deficit even with a slight increase in overall expenditure, owing to higher projections of revenues. A smaller fiscal deficit implies lower budgeted gross borrowings for the year: gross borrowings have been reduced by INR120bn to INR14.01trn, from the INR14.13trn forecast in the interim budget (FY23-24: INR15.43trn). The composition of expenditure also has been largely maintained, with a continued focus on capex. While the recent announcement of the Unified Pension Scheme<sup>30</sup> will likely increase the central government’s expenditure moderately, we think the deficit target remains on track for this year.

India's external metrics bolster macro stability. FY23-24 ended with a BoP surplus of USD63.7bn. This was driven by a financial account surplus as portfolio flows increased, helped by a decline in the current account deficit y/y. For FY24-25, we expect the current account deficit to rise (1% of GDP, FY23-24: 0.7%), on higher imports (coming from higher commodity prices and increased consumption demand) and only a gradual recovery in exports. Despite a wider CA deficit, we think this gap will still be comfortably financeable, with passive foreign fund inflows coming through since India’s inclusion in a global government bond index (June 2024). Net FDI flows are also likely to recover modestly in FY24-25, from decadal lows in FY23-24, assuming easier global financial conditions this year and next. Taken together, we expect the financial account surplus to widen in FY24-25, contributing to a BoP surplus of \~USD52.8bn.

# Indonesia: No longer just about the Fed

Brian Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Sarah Beh, Barclays Bank, Singapore

The pre-pandemic trend for GDP growth to hew close to 5% y/y remains in place while CPI inflation remains benign. However, despite our – and Bank Indonesia's (BI) – expectation for the US Federal Reserve to start cutting rates in September, policymakers look likely to wait a little longer for the dust to settle from the US elections and minimise the risk of currency turmoil. We thus expect BI to begin cutting its policy rate only in November – which would notably also be after incoming Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto is inaugurated on 20 October. There will likely be intense focus on the choice of the next finance minister and what this means for fiscal policy. Our view remains that the incoming Prabowo administration is unlikely to tinker with the fiscal rules limiting the deficit to 3% of GDP and government debt to 60% of GDP. That said, the risk remains of continued noise around fiscal policy that keeps investors on edge.

• Credit: We are neutral on Indonesian HG corporates overall, as we expect them to maintain stable fundamentals and supply is likely to be muted over the rest of the year. In additions, Indonesian SOEs are likely to receive continued strong government support. We retain our Market Weight rating on the sovereign on continued noise around fiscal issues. While we do not expect the incoming Prabowo administration to breach the 3% fiscal-deficit threshold, a tolerance for a looser fiscal policy could keep investors on edge, and pose a risk to bond supply. Additionally, we remain watchful of the government's stance toward natural resource downstreaming, as well as the compensation and subsidy schemes for the SOEs as we have noted that the disbursement of compensation payments to the SOEs have been opportunistic in the past. In 2024, net supply so far from the hard currency INDON and INDOIS complex has totalled USD2bn. In contrast, supply from Indonesia SOEs has been a negative -USD0.9bn.

• FX: We see some room for limited downside in USDIDR to around 15,250 by end-2024. Into next year, we expect the IDR to depreciate gradually amid some rebound in the USD. Inflows into Indonesia have picked up over recent months and should continue to be supportive for the currency. We also expect Bank Indonesia to cut later and less aggressively than the Fed this year, while a broad improvement in Indonesia’s terms of trade should be supportive too.

• Rates: We expect yields to consolidate around current levels before slipping to around 6.50% by year end and look for IndoGB yields to gradually slip to around 6.00% by end-Q3 2025. Fiscal concerns may recede further and a likely widening in the fiscal deficit to 2.5% of GDP this year is unlikely to prompt an increase in risk premium. Encouraging signs of a return of foreign investment flows into IndoGBs will likely persist in the months ahead.

Similar to 2023, we continue to expect GDP growth to remain at 5.0% in 2024 – in the upper half of Bank Indonesia's (BI) 4.7-5.5% forecast range for this year – before edging up to 5.1% in 2025. GDP growth edged down to 5.0% y/y in Q2 from 5.1% in Q1, in line with our view that the GDP indicators have returned to the pre-pandemic trend for economic growth to hew close to 5% y/y. Before COVID-19 lockdowns and social distancing that resulted in visible swings in economic activity, quarterly GDP growth in the pre-pandemic era was unusually stable, ranging between 4.7% and 5.3% y/y, and averaging 5.0% y/y through 2014-19. This pattern appears to have resumed, with all seven quarterly GDP prints since Q4 22 hovering around 5.0% y/y.

With the H1 current account deficit averaging 0.8% of GDP, we raise our full-year forecasts to 0.8% of GDP from 0.5% for 2024 – near the top end of BI's 0.1-0.9% forecast range – and to 0.9% from 0.8% for 2025. While the Q2 current account deficit came in only slightly higher than we expected, at 0.6% of GDP, the Q1 figure was also revised wider, to 0.7% from 0.6% of GDP, and the July trade surplus turned out to be surprisingly narrow, shrinking to just USD0.5bn from USD2.4bn in June. We thus take a slightly more cautious view on the current account deficit despite our Comodo indicators – which aim to quantify the net terms-of-trade efects of global commodity price developments – showing significant improvements into August and early September, helped by lower crude oil prices. That said, our forecast of small current account deficits in 2023-25 would represent a relatively benign outcome, in our view, compared with the 2012-19 period, which saw quarterly deficits average 2.5% of GDP.

In terms of the CPI, we expect headline inflation to cool from 3.7% in 2023 to 2.4% in 2024 – around the middle of BI's 1.5-3.5% target range – and to 2.0% in 2025. Volatile food price inflation has slowed to 3.0% y/y as of August from its 10.3% peak in March – by contrast, core inflation has crept up to 2.0% y/y in May from 1.7% in February and, despite a likely drift higher through the rest of this year, should still average a relatively benign 2.0% this year. Notably, the latest unemployment rate of 4.82% nsa as of February 2024 has finally fallen (slightly) below its February 2020 level of 4.94% – lagging most other Emerging Asian economies, where jobless rates quickly slipped below pre-COVID levels as economies reopened after the pandemic. This is in line with our estimates suggesting the output gap may have turned positive in Q1 24, which may support firms' ability to pass on cost pressures to consumers, fuelling a gradual pickup in core inflation.

Despite our expectation for the US Federal Reserve to start cutting rates in September, BI looks likely to wait a little longer for the dust to settle from the US elections and minimise the risk of currency turmoil. While BI remains pro-growth as ever and is likely looking for an opportune moment to start cutting rates, the widening positive output gap is likely bolstering the central bank's patience.

• Historically, IDR appreciation has not been suficient for BI to cut rates – in 2019, the central bank also waited for signs that Fed easing was imminent before lowering rates.

• This time, we sense it is not just about the Fed, as most market participants likely believe. There appears to be additional caution around the 5 November US elections – and thus our base case is for BI to only embark on rate cuts at its 19-20 November policy meeting.

• BI Governor Perry Warjiyo notably also stated on 21 August that the central bank's focus in Q3 was on further strengthening the IDR despite also expecting the Fed to cut in September – which suggests policymakers want to widen the interest rate diferential by not cutting in lockstep – before space opens up for easing in Q4 (see Indonesia: BI – Not just the Fed but also US elections, 21 August 2024).

Thus, while we acknowledge the risk that the central bank could still go as early as 18 September if it assesses US politics as likely to be less of a concern for the IDR by then, we expect BI to begin cutting its policy rate only in November. BI's November meeting is on the 19th and 20th of that month – enough time, in our view, for the dust to settle from the US elections on 5 November. Our base case is for BI to cut by 25bp in November and December this year, before cutting three more times in Q1, Q2 and Q3 next year, to a terminal rate of 5.00%. This is in line with our US economists' forecast for Fed cuts in September, November and December this year followed by March, June and September in 2025. Should BI start cutting earlier in September – in line with a likely start to Fed cuts – the next moves would likely be in November and December this year followed by two more cuts in Q1 and Q2 25.

On the fiscal front, we raised our 2024 budget deficit forecast to 2.5% of GDP from 1.9%, but maintained our 2025 projection at 2.2%. The latest figures indicate that the 2024 year-todate fiscal deficit has continued to widen in recent months, reaching a IDR93.4trn deficit as of July – exceeding our earlier projections, as we estimate monthly government spending has continued to trend higher amid relatively stable revenue collection. Assuming last year's pattern of spending broadly holds – especially the sharp increase in disbursements at the end of the year, possibly to state-owned enterprises such as to Pertamina to compensate it for temporarily bearing the fiscal burden of fuel subsidies – we estimate that the full-year fiscal deficit could reach 2.5% of GDP.

## Our fiscal deficit forecasts are slightly below the government's latest estimates.

• In his 2025 budget speech to parliament – his last before his term ends in October – President Joko Widodo proposed a fiscal deficit equivalent to 2.53% of GDP (IDR616.2trn) for incoming President Prabowo Subianto's government in 2025. This represents a step-down from the government's latest 2024 deficit estimate of 2.70% of GDP (IDR609.7trn), which was itself raised visibly above the 2.29% (IDR522.8trn) initially budgeted for this year.

• The 2025 budget appears to be based on a 6.9% jump in revenue – which looks relatively aggressive compared with the 1.0% increase implied by Finance Minister Sri Mulyani Indrawati's latest 2024 figures and even the post-pandemic 5.3% expansion in 2022 collections. That said, government disbursements have tended to disappoint due to administrative hurdles, and we suspect the fiscal deficit is still likely to remain broadly under control.

Our view remains that the incoming Prabowo administration is unlikely to tinker with the fiscal rules limiting the deficit to 3% of GDP and government debt to 60% of GDP. As we noted in Emerging Asia: Finally, rate cuts (1 August 2024), key individuals related to the incoming administration have consistently described the reported plans to raise government debt to 50% of GDP by the end of the next five-year term as being reasonable precisely because it falls within the 60% limit on government debt. In our view, this suggests no strong appetite to cross the 3% fiscal-deficit threshold either. That said, the risk is of continued noise around fiscal policy that keeps investors on edge.

There will likely be intense focus on the choice of the next finance minister and what this means for fiscal policy once incoming Indonesian President Prabowo Subianto is inaugurated on 20 October. In particular, we would see where Mr Prabowo's nephew, Thomas Djiwandono, ends up in the new administration. We had noted the possibility that Thomas Djiwandono is on a path to eventually become finance minister – if not right after Mr Prabowo's inauguration then at a later date. Notably, Thomas Djiwandono was appointed as second deputy finance minister in the outgoing administration on 18 July and, based on his prominence in the political transition – he was earlier in charge of synchronising the incoming administration's plans with the Ministry of Finance – we would not be surprised if this proves to be part of a trajectory to eventually place him in the ofice of finance minister.

## Korea: The time for a pivot

Bum Ki Son, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Yimin Weng, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Jit Ming Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore

We maintain our above-consensus 2024 GDP growth forecast at 2.6% as we expect the export recovery to continue into early 2025. We track strong enterprise demand for tech exports but drags from consumption and construction are likely to continue. We believe this should maintain the wide divergence between the external and domestic sectors and the current account surplus large. This should pave the way for the BoK to pivot, with our expectation for back-to-back rate cuts in October and November. However, the recent rally in housing prices in the Seoul metropolitan area have sparked some concerns about financial imbalances, which may delay the timing of the first rate cut.

• FX: We see USDKRW trading in a 1,300-1,360 range in the short term. For TWDKRW crosses, we expect 41.3-43.0 to hold. Despite continued stellar performance in the external sector, headwinds from capital outflows on the KRW from domestic investors as their outbound investments in dollar assets were outweighing foreign-investment flows into domestic assets. FX conversion flows by Korean corporates this year have been relatively "smooth and decisive," with strong dollar sales at above 1,390.

• Rates: We expect the rally in Korea rates to stall in the short run, but think any corrections over 10bp could ofer attractive entry levels, with curve steepening tracking UST yields. We think WGBI inclusion will likely take place in 2025. The worsening supply/demand dynamics driven by the highest gross KTB issuance in history will shadow Korea rates over the medium term, but the inclusion next year should bring USD59bn of passive inflows as the KTB weighting in the WGBI climbs to 2.3%, in our estimate, and help absorb part of the increment in KTB supply.

• Credit: We rate the HG Korea Corporates sector Underweight on tight valuations. We see a mixed picture for corporate fundamentals – Korean semiconductor makers’ credit metrics are likely to remain robust given strong demand for AI products, while uncertainty over the trajectory of EV sales likely will weigh on the earnings and credit metrics of EV battery producers.

We keep our above-consensus 2024 GDP growth forecast at 2.6%, trimmed from 2.7% after the Q2 GDP print. Q2 GDP underscored our core view that domestic demand remains a drag, particularly around construction and consumption. This reafirmed our view that some of the boost in Q1 was one-of and underlying momentum remains weak. In contrast, we continue to see strong exports driven by the recovery in semiconductor exports. A slowdown in facilities investment, as manufacturers focus more on profitability than market share, has kept imports relatively low.

We expect GDP growth to slow to 2.4% in H2 from 2.8% in H1, but see some changes of trend from H1. On domestic demand, we expect a sharp turnaround in facilities investment. In H1, facilities investment was much weaker than expected, partly due to: 1) delays in aircraft imports due to the production shutdown at a major producer; 2) slow capital spending by semiconductor producers; and 3) relatively soft imports of chemicals. We now see stronger signs that imports of semiconductor manufacturing equipment will increase as a more broadbased recovery in chip demand takes hold. This is likely to boost facilities investment and raise import growth into H2, in our view.

Given the pickup in imports, we expect net exports’ contribution to decline notably. That said, we remain constructive on tech exports as we continue to see a restocking cycle for global tech. We have continued to track strong enterprise semiconductor demand for servers, with demand broadening to less advanced chips. One of the major Korean semiconductor producers will be also supplying the HBM3e to a major AI vendor starting from Q3, which should continue to keep growth strong. On the other hand, consumer-related products, such as smartphone and PCs, have restocked only gradually. While some note signs suggesting exports will slow in H2, we believe the recovery is likely to continue into early 2025, albeit at a slower pace.

We continue to note relatively soft consumption and construction demand. We expect consumption to recover gradually as inflation slows and wage growth picks up on incentive payments. That said, we still expect growth to remain below trend. We also expect construction sector activity to remain muted despite the recent pickup in property prices. Property price gains have been focused on Seoul; outside Seoul, prices remains under pressure. Against this backdrop, we expect construction –  both commercial and residential – outside of Seoul to remain under pressure.

Given the relatively strong outlook for exports and still-tepid domestic demand, we look for a wider current account surplus. We raised our current account surplus forecast to USD77.7bn from USD68bn, given the strong goods surplus. We maintain our view that tech exports are likely to hold up into H2 on steady volume growth. Given positive H2 seasonality, we upgrade our full-year exports forecast to USD720bn from USD690bn, above the high of USD694bn in 2022. We believe the lower oil prices in recent months should also keep the current account surplus relatively wide, despite the headwind from increased machinery imports. Our view remains that Korea continues to accumulate net foreign assets at a rate of 4-5% of GDP per year, slightly more than the size of current account surplus.

On capital flows, portfolio inflows continue to drive the KRW. Inflows into Korean capital markets have been significant, particularly flows in the equity market following the Corporate Value-up Program (CVP). Another leg of inflows story could come from Korea's inclusion in the World Government Bond Index (WGBI). Korea has cleared some of the preconditions, as Korea treasury bonds and Monetary Stabilization Bonds become Euroclear-able from June. We estimate the inclusion could generate inflows of about USD60bn from passive buying. That said, NPS reforms are becoming more tangible, with the government announcing its reform proposals. The reforms should keep the size of the NPS outflows sizable – close to USD30bn per year for another 5-10 years.

Given slowing domestic demand growth, we trim our 2024 CPI inflation forecast to 2.5% from 2.6%. We have noted relatively softer inflation dynamics, especially in indicators of underlying price pressures. Supply-side factors, such as energy and agricultural prices, continue to pose some upside risks, and upside from agricultural prices is likely to continue into the Chuseok holiday in September, in our view. Our forecast also assumes a 5-10% increase in electricity prices in Q4 24 and increases in some administered prices such as public transportation. Under our scenario, we expect inflation to print below 2% during September and October, before it edges up in November and December.

While softer domestic demand and inflation should pave the way for a BoK policy pivot in Q4, concerns about financial imbalances have risen with the strong rebound in the property prices in the Seoul metropolitan area. Our assessment of the rebound of property prices is that it is primarily driven by: 1) rate cut expectations; 2) supply constraints from the project-financing restructurings; 3) delays in macroprudential policy; and 4) jeonse price increases due to aversion to detached homes. The government has been relatively proactive in

rolling out aggressive plans for new housing supply and tightening of macroprudential policy.
We believe this should broadly help to clear the path for the BoK to cut relatively soon.

Against this baseline, we expect the BoK to cut from October followed by another cut in November. Our baseline has been that the overall growth dynamic is not challenging, but some sectors face dificult conditions. This means the nature of the cuts is more like insurance, and we believe it is better to front-load the cuts in order to loosen financial conditions for the vulnerable segments and also anchor expectations for a relatively high terminal rate – given front-loaded cuts should keep the yield curve relatively steep. However, we also see the risk of the BoK being more reactive with the first cut only in November and a prolonged hold through H1 as the risk of property prices pick up continues to feature. In such case, we expect the BoK to back-load the cuts with 75bp of cuts in H2 2025.

## Malaysia: Will they, won't they

Brian Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore

While GDP growth has surged since the start of the year, core inflation remains benign, even after the hike diesel prices – providing little reason for Bank Negara Malaysia (BNM) to hike its policy rate. Our base case remains for Malaysia's will-they-won't-they on RON95 petrol subsidies to continue to yield a lack of clarity, even at the upcoming 18 October budget. Even without a RON95 petrol price hike – and with the state of Sarawak's takeover of gas distribution there from Petronas – we believe the government would be able to remain on a gradual fiscal consolidation path – in turn reducing the pressure for action. With RON95 petrol prices unlikely to be hiked, we thus expect BNM to leave its policy rate unchanged through 2025.

• Credit: We maintain our sovereign rating on Malaysia at Market Weight, as we think the good news surrounding the gradual fiscal consolidation has been priced in. Additionally, a pick up of noise in the Malaysian bond complex as Petronas cedes the gas aggregation business in Sarawak to Petros could warrant a level of caution<sup>31</sup> . This is simultaneously balanced by our expectation for robust supply side to continue. We note a divergence in performance within the Malaysian complex YTD, which has seen TNBMK and AXIATA bonds have outperformed spread wise, while the Malaysian sovereign has lagged and now screens wide.

• FX: We expect the MYR to underperform peers over a one-year horizon, after front-loading a greater-than-two-standard-deviation rally in Q3. With external pressures easing, we think BNM is likely to opportunistically reduce its record-high forward sales book, keeping the currency pair relatively supported over the medium term. We recently recommended a long 2m SGDMYR trade (entry: 3.34, target: 3.47, stop-loss: 3.27), reflecting our short-term view of potential \~15-20bp SGD NEER gains into the MAS October review and relative MYR underperformance as BNM rebuilds its reserves.

• Rates: We expect MGS yields/IRS rates to consolidate in Q4, followed by measured easing over a one-year horizon. We forecast 10y MGS yields at c.3.8% by year-end, before moving lower to 3.60% by Q3 25. We expect curves to flatten over the medium term, reflecting some degree of fiscal consolidation and with BNM unlikely to reverse course with rate cuts. We expect 3m Klibor to edge to a higher range in Q4 on seasonal efects.

With GDP growth surging to 5.9% y/y in Q2 after an already solid rebound to 4.2% in Q1 – averaging 5.1% for H1 – we forecast GDP growth forecast rising from 3.6% in 2023 to 5.0% in 2024, the top end of Bank Negara Malaysia's (BNM) 4.0-5.0% forecast range, before moderating to 4.5% in 2025. Our 2024 forecast profile incorporates some degree of caution around Q3, lest there be some pullback following Q2's sequential surge in economic activity. That said, the latest export data continue to suggest a clearer bottom is forming in Malaysia's tech sector – in line with our view that the tech upcycle is broadening out of North Asia – though this has yet to be reflected clearly in the industrial production data (see Malaysia: Surprisingly little sign of sustained repatriation in Q2 data, 16 August 2024).

By contrast, with the current account surplus coming in at just 2.0% of GDP in H1, we reduced our full-year forecast to 1.5% of GDP for the full year 2024 – and expect a similar 1.5% outcome in 2025. The Q2 current account surplus had retreated to a surprisingly small 0.6% of GDP after spiking to 3.5% in Q1 from 0.2% in Q4 23. Notably, the net outflows in the primary income account – the main driver of the Q1 outperformance – rebounded in Q2,

suggesting there may have been relatively less repatriation of investment income by government-linked entities to support the MYR in Q2. Beyond Q2, the trade surplus surprisingly narrowed to MYR6.4bn in July – its smallest reading since 2020 when pandemic-related lockdowns disrupted logistics operations – after spiking to MYR14.3bn in June from MYR10.0bn in May. This suggests the current account surplus may remain under pressure in Q3, even after incorporating some inflows from likely repatriation by exporters.

Despite the floating of diesel prices in Peninsular Malaysia, we reduce our CPI inflation forecasts for both 2024 and 2025 to 2.0% from 2.1% previously. In terms of core inflation, our projections now imply a drop from 3.0% in 2023 to 1.9% in 2024 – down from 2.0% previously – and 2.2% in 2025. These are at the bottom end of BNM's earlier 2024 forecast range ranges of 2.0-3.5% for headline inflation and 2.0-3.0% for core – perhaps acknowledging the low figures so far this year, the central bank more recently highlighted in its 5 September policy statement that both headline and core were unlikely to exceed 3%.

The slight reduction in our CPI forecasts reflects two factors: fuel price cuts and lowerthan-expected core inflation.

• Following the hike in diesel prices on 10 June – which had only a marginal efect on headline inflation, as we expected – the government has started reducing fuel prices, likely tracking the drop in global crude oil prices. This began with diesel on 8 August – which had been ostensibly floated on 10 June but, in practice, was kept inexplicably steady for more than eight weeks. More surprisingly however, RON97 petrol prices – which are supposed to be floating and not subsidised but were also kept inexplicably steady since 21 September – have begun edging down since 29 August. While the current administration's inclinations remain to be seen, we suspect the risks to diesel and RON95 petrol prices are asymmetric – previous governments have touted the benefits of floating fuel prices when global crude oil prices were low only to quietly and inexplicably keep fuel prices steady when oil prices picked up.

• At the same time, core inflation has been recently registering marginal downside surprises versus our projections. This is all the more notable in the context of the jump in GDP growth since the start of this year – and it suggests that there is suficient slack to absorb the economic recovery without resulting in demand-pull price pressures, reducing the need for BNM to hike in response to strong activity data.

Our CPI forecasts do not incorporate any RON95 petrol price hikes – our base case remains for Malaysia's will-they-won't-they on this front to continue to yield a lack of clarity, even at the upcoming 18 October budget. We continue to believe the government faces significant political challenges in hiking RON95 petrol prices in its current term. With Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim's allies having voiced concerns that the diesel price hike in June may have led to the Sungai Bakap by-election defeat<sup>32</sup> , the government will likely find reasons in the recent strengthening of the currency to delay action on RON95 subsidies (see Malaysia: BNM waits to see second-round inflation efects, 11 July 2024).

Even without a RON95 petrol price hike, we believe the government would be able to remain on a gradual fiscal consolidation path – in turn reducing the pressure for action. We expect the government to further reduce the fiscal deficit from 4.3% of GDP in 2024 to 3.9% in 2025, the halfway point to the 3.5% target for 2026 stipulated under the finance ministry's Medium-Term Fiscal Framework presented last year – helped by the extraction of dividends, possibly from sovereign wealth funds such as KWAP, but almost surely from state-owned oil company Petronas.

Our fiscal path accounts for the state of Sarawak's takeover of gas distribution there from Petronas. Sarawak has set a 1 October deadline for Petronas – which is owned by the federal government – to cede control of the distribution and sale of natural gas extracted from within its jurisdiction to its own state-owned oil company Petroleum Sarawak Bhd (Petros). While the financial impact on Petronas could be serious, the hit on the federal government's petroleumrelated revenues seems likely to be manageable – in part because we expect the Ministry of Finance to continue extracting relatively large dividends from the company.

• The federal government seems likely to merely shift to collecting petroleum income taxes and export duties on Sarawak-related activities from Petros instead of Petronas, assuming profit margins are unafected and production is not diverted to domestic use. To our understanding, the petroleum royalty is derived from upstream activities – and, to the extent that Petronas's existing production-sharing contracts with upstream players are unafected in Sarawak, this should also imply insulation for this segment.

• The segment where there is likely to be greatest variability is the Petronas dividend, which the company might seek to trim in response to any financial hit from the Sarawak split – but, historically, there are signs that the size of the Petronas dividend has been more the result of agreements with the federal government. While it remains unclear exactly how the Sarawak split will afect Petronas's financials, even our more extreme scenarios suggest there should still be enough net income to maintain large dividend payouts to the government – albeit just barely. Even if net income were to turn into losses, we doubt this would deter the federal government from extracting relatively large dividends, given Petronas's cash reserves and ability to borrow.

With the fiscal backdrop unlikely to deteriorate dramatically, even if fuel subsidies remain elevated, we expect the government to prioritise its political goals rather than embarking on the challenging path of hiking RON95 petrol prices. Even if the government does raise RON95 petrol prices, the magnitude of the hike is unlikely to be large and the fiscal savings may prove to be relatively limited as policymakers will likely try to contain its political impact with exemptions and cash transfers – as was the case with the diesel price hike in June. As the window for RON95 petrol price hikes closes in the current term of government, we suspect the Anwar administration will shift gears and aim to cut these subsidies – which would open up room to also reduce the Petronas dividend – only once re-election has been secured.

We thus expect BNM to leave its policy rate unchanged through 2025. Despite core inflation remaining benign at 1.9% y/y as of July, BNM is likely the most hawkish major central bank in Emerging Asia at the moment because it is ready to hike if a RON95 petrol price hike sparks of broader second-round inflation efects. Our phrasing is deliberate: the experience with the recent diesel price hike suggests BNM is unlikely to hike pre-emptively in the event that RON95 petrol prices are indeed hiked – the central bank will likely wait, monitoring the "pervasiveness" of inflation for evidence that such second-round efects are materialising before it responds.

On the flip-side, even if BNM were to conclude that the government will not hike RON95 petrol prices – taking the inflation constraint out of the picture – we would still be sceptical on financial-stability grounds that rate cuts are coming in Malaysia. Where some investors might see an opportunity for BNM to take advantage of benign inflation to further support growth with a rate cut, we believe the central bank is more likely to see such policy easing as an unnecessary fanning of financial-stability risks – especially given that after the pandemic the policy rate was only raised to its 2019 level of 3.00%, a setting that is not obviously restrictive.

## Philippines: Turning, but patiently

Shreya Sodhani, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Avanti Save, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Sarah Beh, Barclays Bank, Singapore

The BSP has started its easing cycle, leading the region, as falling inflation has allowed its attention to turn to growth. Despite being aggressive in hiking, we think the move down will be slow and measured. We expect only one more cut by the BSP this year, with its focus maintained on upside risks to inflation, while retaining its relatively optimistic outlook for growth.

• Credit: We maintain our Underweight rating on the Philippines sovereign. The country's external balances continue to register improvements, with another BoP surplus expected in 2024 on a declining current account deficit and widening financial account surplus. However, we think valuations still look tight, especially in the front-end where PHILIP bonds trade in line with the INDON and INDOIS complex. In 2024 so far, net supply in the hard currency PHILIP complex has totalled \~USD2bn.

• FX: We expect the PHP to resume some near-term appreciation into year end, with seasonality positive in Q4 due to strong remittance flows. A narrower current deficit should also prove more manageable. In 2025 we expect the PHP to resume depreciation amid and expected broad-USD recovery and a relatively softer growth profile, but we expect its depreciation to be at a slower pace than most other Asian FX..

• Rates: We expect 10y RPGB yields to edge lower over the short term and extend declines into next year. Lower policy rates expected in the US and Philippines, declining inflation pressures, relatively stable FX, and limited upward pressure from US yields, suggest there will not be any significant headwinds to a further, albeit gradual, decline in RPGB yields into 2025, with 10y yields edging towards 5.50% by Q3 2025.

Bangko Sentral ng Pilipinas (BSP) became the first central bank in EM Asia to embark on an easing cycle, after being the most aggressive one in the region on the hiking cycle. The bank's 25bp cut on 15 August followed a slew of dovish commentary, with the bank noting that it is "somewhat less hawkish than before".

Softer-than-expected inflation prints and outlook were the primary factors driving the rate cut. Price pressures have eased and we now expect inflation to average 3.3% this year. The BSP's turn in tone was preceded by softening inflation figures, which, while expected to breach the bank's 2-4% target range in Q2, averaged 3.8% , making the bank "more comfortable" that inflation in 2024 will be within the target range. The nail in the cofin was the reduction in rice import tarifs, which the bank termed as a "big deal" to reduce inflation pressures as rice inflation (9% weight in CPI basket) had been in double digits for a year, as of August. Following the rice tarif cut, the BSP noted that the risks to inflation had moved to the downside in June, for the first time since May 2022 and  the bank reduced its risk adjusted forecast below its baseline inflation forecast to reflect this outlook. With demand-pull inflation turning benign and rice inflation likely to move into single digits shortly, we now expect inflation to average 3.0% in 2025.

The easing in inflation also opened the door for the bank to shift focus to growth, which slowed sequentially in Q1 and Q2 as the impact of the aggressive hiking cycle became more evident. Indeed, Governor Remolona flagged in the August meeting that the bank is "somewhat more confident" in inflation coming down than it is in growth accelerating, which, in efect, meant the policy rate had to be reduced, in our view. Specifically, the bank noted that

consumption – which accounts for 67% of the economy – had been slowing by more than trend due to high inflation and the high policy rate, which was weighing on growth. We continue to expect GDP to grow by 5.6% in 2024, suggesting more pain is likely from still restrictive monetary policy in H2 24, even though the bank has started easing. In 2025, we expect some improvement, with the economy likely to expand 5.8%.

Finally, we think the recent appreciation of the PHP – supported by improving external balances – despite expectations of the BSP lowering rates before the Fed also allowed the bank to be more comfortable in easing. While services exports remain elevated, we think the goods trade deficit is likely to be larger than we expected earlier, even while narrowing y/y. We think the outlook for electronic exports remains poor, with the upswing in the semiconductor cycle not yet helping the Philippines, and we also do not expect capital imports to increase despite the government's infrastructure push as bottlenecks are holding up progress on the ground. Overall, we have raised our current account deficit forecast for 2024 to USD6.8bn (1.4% of GDP), but now see a wider BoP surplus of USD4.2bn.

Despite being aggressive on the way up, in lockstep with the Fed, we do not expect the BSP to be overly dovish on the way down. The bank has noted that the current macroeconomic outlook "supports a calibrated shift to a less restrictive monetary policy stance" (italics are ours). This suggests rate cuts are likely to be gradual and measured, and likely fewer than the quite dovish outlook the BSP projects for the Fed's easing cycle. Additionally, the BSP does not sound bearish on growth, highlighting robust employment conditions and supportive public investment. In fact, the bank has noted that with rate cuts and falling inflation, consumption is likely to improve through the rest of the year. Finally, the BSP remains focused on inflation, noting that it will "remain mindful of lingering upside risks to prices". This implies to us that the bank will not cut too much too fast lest the impact of the ricetarif cut is ofset by demand-pull pressures.

We expect only one more 25bp cut from the BSP this year, following August's cut, which will likely be followed by 75bp of cuts next year. As for the timing of the next cut, the BSP has noted it will lower rates either in October or in December. We do not think the bank's cut timing will depend on inflation, with prices expected to be on an easing path through the rest of the year. While Q3 GDP numbers will not be available before the October meeting, we think the bank's communication suggests that one more cut is happening this year, and growth is unlikely to be high enough to suggest no more cuts are needed. We therefore think the bank will deliver the next cut in October, which will help it keep optionality for a December move.

We also expect fiscal policy to be supportive of growth going forward, with the path of capital outlays elevated. We think the government is more focused on expenditure to support the economy than it is on fiscal consolidation. While we maintain our forecasts for the fiscal deficit, we flag the risk that with growth in revenues being low, the government could revise its fiscal consolidation targets again this year.

## Singapore: Don't trip over travel

Brian Tan, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore

With GDP growth still holding up, helped by the tech upcycle broadening out of North Asia, we continue to doubt the Monetary Authority of Singapore (MAS) will ease FX policy settings in its last meeting this year (likely 14 October) or even through 2025. The recent sharp drop in core inflation was mainly the result of volatile travel costs – which the MAS will likely look through. More importantly, underlying price pressures remain relatively firm, inflation expectations are still elevated and there is clearly a strong political focus on this front. The recent tightening of macroprudential measures on the residential property market continues to suggest policymakers may be reluctant to see a slide in domestic interest rates that might revive sentiment there. Our base case remains for the general election to only be called next year.

• FX: We expect SGD NEER to trade c.150-175bp above the mid-point of the policy band until the MAS October review, before easing to c.125-150bp above mid by year-end. USDSGD is likely to remain around multi-year lows over the coming quarter before edging higher over a one-year horizon, reflecting the USD regaining some ground and regional headwinds from USDCNY settling in a higher 7.20-7.25 range. We recently recommended a long 2m SGDMYR trade (entry: 3.34, target: 3.47, stop-loss: 3.27), reflecting our short-term view of potential \~15-20bp SGD NEER gains into the MAS October review and relative MYR underperformance as BNM opportunistically rebuilds its reserves.

• Rates: We expect SGS yields/SORA OIS rates to move lower in Q4, before edging higher over a one-year horizon. The Fed commencing easing, seasonally light Q4 supply and an appreciating SGD NEER slope are tailwinds for Singapore rates in the coming quarter. With the 2024 SGS auction calendar completed this month-end, we see scope for long-tenor SGS (10y and beyond) to outperform on the curve over the coming quarter. However further out, we expect Singapore rates to underperform relative to US/basket rates, with the convergence reflecting a moderating SGD NEER outlook and as the DM easing cycle gains further momentum.

With GDP growth averaging 3.0% y/y in H1 24, we continue to forecast a full-year 2024 expansion of 2.5% – in the middle of the revised oficial growth forecast range of 2.0-3.0% – and maintain our 2025 estimate at 2.5%. In sequential terms, Q2 GDP growth remained a tepid 0.4% q/q sa, similar to Q1. Our expectation of similarly steady if uninspiring sequential growth for the rest of this year implies a slowdown to 2.1% y/y in H2 – mainly reflecting an unfavourable base efect from the relatively robust 1.0% and 1.2% q/q sa expansions experienced in Q3 and Q4 of last year. We continue to expect a relatively gradual – not robust – manufacturing recovery, though the performance is likely to remain bumpy and uneven. In particular, we maintain our view that the tech upcycle is broadening out of North Asia into Southeast Asia, including Singapore.

A sharp drop in travel-related costs, however, led us to reduce our core inflation forecasts to 2.7% from 3.0% for 2024 – in the lower half of the Monetary Authority of Singapore's (MAS) 2.5-3.5% forecast range – and to 1.9% from 2.0% for 2025. For headline CPI, we forecast 2.3% for 2024 and 1.8% for 2025 (MAS 2024 forecast range: 2-3%). Looking more closely at the unexpectedly sharp 0.33 percentage point (pp) decline in the MAS's measure of core inflation to 2.5% y/y in July from 2.9% in June, we estimate 0.21pp stemmed from the travel segment alone. Further excluding travel-related items as well as uncooked food and energy from the MAS definition of core CPI, our measure of supercore inflation demonstrated much greater stability, only edging down to 2.9% y/y from 3.0% in June. While the pace of disinflation in underlying price pressures may indeed also have been faster than the MAS had expected, any beat has likely been only slight (see Singapore: July CPI – Don't trip over travel, 23 August 2024).

We thus continue to doubt FX policy easing is likely at the MAS's last meeting for this year (likely 14 October) or even through 2025. While we view the risks as being more balanced now between that of a tightening and easing (via a 50bp adjustment in the slope from an estimated 1.5% currently), we would stress that the probability of any adjustment is still low overall.

• The MAS is likely well-aware of the volatility of travel costs and will likely look through the downside surprise in the July core inflation figure and focus the other components of the CPI basket where, as noted above, underlying price pressures are remain relatively firm.

• Our revised 2025 core inflation forecast of 1.9% in 2025 also remains broadly in line with the MAS's expectation of "around 2%" – which is likely the central bank's current objective, notwithstanding its lack of an oficial inflation target – implying little need for recalibration, especially as GDP growth is still holding up and the estimated 1.5% slope setting of the SGD NEER policy band is not particularly restrictive.

• More importantly, inflation expectations remain relatively elevated and there is clearly a strong political focus on price pressures – Prime Minister Lawrence Wong's first National Day Message on 8 August<sup>33</sup> notably focused on inflation and even gave a shout out to the benefits of tight FX policy.

To the extent that a sharp fall in domestic interest rates might be viewed as potentially reigniting the property market – with all its implications for housing afordability and financial stability – policymakers may also be reluctant to see such a slide occur. While the MAS has chosen to focus on the exchange rate as its policy instrument, in practice the central bank appears to be able exert some control over domestic interest rates, likely through its management of liquidity conditions. With the prime minister choosing to highlight the need to improve housing afordability in his first National Day Rally speech on 18 August<sup>34</sup> and macroprudential measures on Housing Development Board (HDB) loans being tightened on 20 August, we believe policymakers are likely to keep a close eye on domestic interest rates (see Singapore: Property market measures – A demonstration of resolve, 20 August 2024).

There are still few signs that the next general election is about to called imminently in Singapore – in line with our base case for polls to only be called next year, once there is more progress in bringing down residential property prices and core inflation. Most notably, the base efect related to the 1 January 2024 goods and services tax (GST) hike will reverse from January 2025, reducing inflation figures, at least optically. Even so, these cost pressures will likely remain challenging issues that the ruling People's Action Party (PAP) will have to address before the next election – possibly via the 2025 budget. We expect another overall budget deficit in FY2025, equivalent to 0.5% of GDP, as the government continues to address cost-of-living issues. The latest fiscal data suggest the government ran a marginally smaller overall budget deficit equivalent to 0.9% of GDP in FY2023, compared with its 16 February 2024 revised estimate of a deficit equivalent to 1.0% of GDP (see Singapore: 2024 budget – Hawks everywhere, 16 February 2024).

## Taiwan: AI tailwinds continue

Bum Ki Son, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Lemon Zhang, Barclays Bank, Singapore

We maintain our positive growth outlook on Taiwan with an above-consensus growth forecasts for 2024 and 2025 of 4.0% and 2.3%, respectively. We expect steady export growth to continue in H2 2024 and this should keep the investment recovery intact. We think inflation concerns are subsiding as the electricity price hike has not translated into a shock. That said, a more pressing issue is strong credit growth from mortgage loans. But we see initial signs this is easing, as residential rental prices have been cooling since the CBC's surprise hike in Q1 and RRR hike in Q2. Our baseline view is for a gradual slowdown in the property-related sector, slowing domestic demand growth, and the TWD starting to strengthening as many other central banks start to cut rates from Q1 2025.

• FX: We expect the TWD to weaken over the next two months before likely strengthening in December, after the recent rebound against USD on the back of exporters' conversion flows and Taiwan lifers adding back FX hedges. Taiwanese exporters' dollar holdings stand as \~USD5-10bn, based on our estimates. The release of a rule that allows lifers to expand their FX volatility reserves and ultimately reduce FX hedges should have a gradual, long-term negative efect on the TWD. We continue to expect the TWI to consolidate in the lower half of its range.

• Rates: We expect TGB yields to move lower and TWD IRS to outperform TGBs by falling towards TGB yields. We think the IRS-TGB spreads still screen rich, but the next leg of narrowing should be volatile, given that inflation prints are likely to stay sticky and lifers demand for TGBs is lacking. Further out, moderating inflation should be supportive for FI, balancing out the pressure from the growth recovery.

We have raised our forecasts for Taiwan's GDP growth to 4.0% in 2024 (June EMQ: 3.5%) and to 2.3% for 2025 (from 2.1%). Our 2024 forecast is slightly above the Bloomberg consensus (3.9%) and Taiwan's Central Bank of the Republic of China's (CBC) forecast (3.77%). Q2 GDP surprised to the upside, extending the strong growth trend from Q1. Tailwinds from exports remained strong and the investment turnaround was also significant in Q2. We see an improving growth outlook for 2025 and we remain constructive on exports as well as investment picking up into next year. We think there will also be a relatively steady positive spillover from the manufacturing upcycle into domestic demand.

Q2 GDP was another surprise – consumption slowing and the net exports contribution becoming smaller was in line with our expectations, but the biggest driver came from the significant improvement in investment. We think the increases in investment, especially in the tech sector, reflect capacity constraints in high-end chip production, and bodes well for a further recovery in exports. We also track inventory levels broadly falling, which suggests that the restocking cycle is likely to continue among major customers of Taiwan's tech companies.

We continue to expect a gradual acceleration in Taiwan's export growth, on strong support from AI demand, as well as an overall broadening of the tech recovery cycle into more traditional tech applications. Samsung’s supply of HBM3e to the Nvidia<sup>35</sup> should boost Taiwan’s imports of semiconductors from Korea and exports of AI servers to the US even more. The launch of a new iPhone launch and AI chips in late-2024 also bodes well for  a further recovery in Taiwan's tech exports, in our view. Meanwhile, the strong turnaround in investment is likely to keep the contribution from net exports negative for some time, in our view. While

capex guidance from Taiwanese corporates has been muted, we believe capex will likely pick up in H2 against a low year-earlier base.

Despite strong exports and an investment boom, we believe consumption growth is likely to stay below-trend. We forecast around 0.4-0.5% q/q sa consumption growth into next year as income growth has been relatively muted, despite a tight labour market, while increased mortgage burdens and high price levels are keeping consumer sentiment relatively soft. If anything, we expect some tailwinds from increased tourism, but this is likely to be concentrated in Taipei and surrounding areas.

While we maintain our narrative of relatively muted domestic demand pressures, we revise our 2024 CPI inflation forecast higher to 2.2% y/y from 2.0% in June. While the electricity price hikes from April proved to be no big deal for CPI inflation, a series of negative supply shocks around fresh foods and eggs has been more substantial than we expected. That said, we still expect CPI inflation to start trailing lower towards 2% from September. Residential rental inflation has also started to slow as the macroprudential policy tightening and RRR hikes have curbed speculative lending demand. Prices pressures in entertainment-related services have also started to wane towards more trend-like levels. Against this backdrop, we expect core inflation to average 1.8% in 2024.

In terms of monetary policy, we continue to expect the CBC to start cutting rates from Q1 25. While we think the bank's policy stance remains restrictive to curb still strong loan demand in the property sector, we believe its initial line of defence should be a further strengthening of macroprudential policy. After the CBC hiked policy rates in March and raised the RRR in June, the latest data shows that residential rental price inflation has started to come down. We believe this trend is likely to continue and reduce property-related lending demand. With consumption growth below-trend and inflation further slowing, we believe domestic conditions will keep the door open for the CBC to pivot. Meanwhile, the external backdrop of global central banks commencing a cutting cycle in H2 2024 should also support the CBC’s pivot towards easing, in our view.

However, we still expect the easing cycle to be relatively shallow, with risks tilted to a slower and shallower cycle. The current balance of risks are tilted towards less urgency to cut given the above-trend growth in 2024, overheated property-related credit growth, and a weaker currency. Our baseline view is for a gradual slowdown in the property-related sector, slowing domestic demand growth, and the TWD starting to strengthening as many other central banks start to cut rates. Furthermore, we assume M2 growth slows to the midpoint of the CBC's reference range of 2.5-6.5% by the end of the year, with some slowing in credit growth. However, we think any upside surprises could delay the first rate cut or make the cycle even shallower.
