## Thailand: Don't fret over policy or politics… yet

Shreya Sodhani, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Mitul Kotecha, Barclays Bank, Singapore | Audrey Ong, Barclays Bank, Singapore

The BoT's recent communication has engendered views that the monetary policy stance may be set for change, amid an already fluid political situation. However, we think a change in monetary policy in the near term is unlikely, given the bank's outlook for credit conditions and growth. We also expect political uncertainty to fade in the near term. We think the timeline to watch for both a potential change in monetary policy and possibly more political uncertainty is H2 25.

• FX: We expect the THB to appreciate and outperform forwards in Q4, before USDTHB resumes a grind higher over a one-year horizon. We have turned constructive on the THB and see room for gains in the coming quarter, reflecting receding political/fiscal risk premia, positive tourism seasonality, firmer gold prices and less of a carry drag. Further out, the broad USD regaining ground and regional headwinds from a still-weak CNY should be an overhang for the currency, even as external-sector fundamentals remain on an improving trend over the medium-term.

• Rates: We expect ThaiGB yields/THOR OIS to ease measurably over a one-year horizon, with 2.50% in 10y and 2.00% in 2y likely key short-term support levels. We see room for front-end Thai rates/THOR OIS to move higher, as we think the window for BoT's pivot to easing remains narrow in Q4 2024. The THOR OIS curve also appears dovish relative to our baseline view of no BoT cuts through 2025. The likely staggered financing timeline for the remainder of the digital wallet program over FY2025 – should it materialise – implies room for mid to longtenors to outperform, especially as fiscal risk premia gets further unwound.

The Bank of Thailand's Monetary Policy Committee stayed on hold for the fifth straight meeting in August, but the policy statement led to expectations from market participants that the MPC may be open to easing soon. Indeed, for the first time in this cycle, the MPC members noted that they deem it "essential to monitor deterioration in credit quality," which could weigh on borrowing costs and the economy. With the bank giving priority to financial stability over growth during the current period of unchanged rates, the statement likely made some perceive that there has been a change in stance from the bank.

In our view, it is too soon to suggest that the bank's bias has shifted to the dovish or lesshawkish side, since neither growth nor credit conditions suggest that the BoT will see a need for lower borrowing costs in the near term. First, household loan growth has been slowing for the past three quarters, in line with BoT's objective of reducing household debt. Second, system-wide aggregate nonperforming loans are 2.8% of total loans, a small increase from Q4 23. The MPC focused on deterioration on the serviceability of household debt due to the slow recovery in personal income. We note that as a percentage of total loans, nonperforming personal consumption-related loans have risen by more than the aggregate. However, NPLs in housing and personal consumption as a percentage of housing and personal consumption loans, respectively, seem lower than recent highs. Credit cards and auto loans could be a cause for concern for the BoT, but not immediately. Additionally, the bank has instituted programs (eg, Debt Clinic) that should prevent a surge in NPLs.

As for economic activity, we think the Thai economy is past its worst, with GDP growth likely to average 3.2% in H2 24, leading to full-year growth of 2.6%. While the FY2025 budget is likely to pass timely, we have not raised our growth forecast in order to incorporate the impact of recent flooding, which may intensify if La Niña conditions emerge. That said, we think outside of private consumption, all growth contributors – investment, government consumption, exports of services and to some extent goods – are better placed in H2.

• Tourism is once again becoming an engine of growth. With tourist arrivals and spending increasing, we now expect the current account to register a surplus of USD13.7bn (2.6% of GDP) in 2024. We continue to expect tourist arrivals to increase to 36mn in 2024, while tourism-related receipts are likely to increase to \~70% of 2019 levels. Despite our less-thanoptimistic view on merchandise exports, we now expect a wider BoP trade surplus in 2024 with a large gap between the BoP and customs trade surpluses.

• Fiscal spending will be another contributor to economic growth, especially under our expectation that a tranche of digital wallet will be disbursed as cash transfers in September. The government has flagged the possibility of some cash payments, and we think 14.5mn citizens (13.5mn welfare card holders and 1mn disabled) are likely to be paid THB10,000 in cash within FY2024 (ends 30 September) under the new government's digital wallet plan<sup>36</sup> . With the marginal propensity to consume being higher for low-income earners, we think private consumption will receive some support.

With the recovery likely to continue into 2025, we think the BoT is unlikely to lower rates specifically to support growth. We expect Thailand’s economy to expand 3.0% in 2025 (BoT: 3.0%), in line with the BoT's estimate of the economy’s potential growth rate. Additionally, the recent increase in NPLs, is in line with the bank’s expectation. The bank also expects private consumption to moderate in 2025 to 2.5% from 4.2% in 2024. We think for a cut to materialise, the MPC would need to see the outlook for private consumption worsening much more, due to a rapid and unexpected surge in NPLs. With better income recovery through Q4 and Q1's peak tourist seasons and likely fiscal cash transfers, we do not expect a material increase in NPLs and consequent drop in consumption to materialise until H1 25. That said, we acknowledge that if the increase in NPLs proves to be rapid and weighs on private consumption, the BoT may be open to easing in H2 25.

We also note that the bank is unlikely to be worried about inflation, with inflation likely to average 0.8% in 2024 by our forecast. The bank expects inflation be lower, with government subsidies and high competition from imported goods weighing on prices. We also expect inflation to remain benign in 2025 and average 1.3% (BoT: 1.3%). Finally, the BoT MPC is unlikely to fret over global central banks easing and will continue to focus on domestic conditions.

With the economy being in a relatively better spot, attention turns to politics. The disqualification of a sitting PM and the dissolution of the largest elected party in the parliament have brought the political clouds that have hovered over Thailand for years back into focus. The quick appointment of the new PM and cabinet have helped resolve the immediate uncertainty. We expect low volatility on the political front in the near term. However, the likelihood of more political turmoil in 2025 cannot be discounted, especially around the time of former PM Thaksin Shinawatra’s royal defamation case, which is scheduled to be heard in July 2025.

# Emerging Europe, Middle East and Africa No one size fits all

EEMEA defies global headwinds with resilient domestic demand supporting growth. Yet, disinflation has led to divergent central bank action, leaving some in CEE limited room to cut while others are yet to embark on easing. There are downside risks amidst geopolitics and elections; the US being pivotal for conflict areas.

Trade Summary | Czechia | Egypt | Ghana | Hungary | Israel | Kazakhstan | Kenya | Nigeria | Oman | Poland | Romania | Russia | Saudi Arabia | Senegal | South Africa | Tunisia | Turkiye | Ukraine

## Overview

EEMEA countries have continued to defy global headwinds including tight monetary policy conditions, geopolitical volatility, and ongoing conflicts. Yet, idiosyncratic factors led by domestic politics have been a driving force of policy direction. Election uncertainty has often delayed pressing reforms, exacerbating upward fiscal pressure. Yet, even when incumbents have gained a new mandate, fading election uncertainty has quickly translated into policy action (eg, Turkey, Egypt) or political stalemate (eg, Senegal).

## Manufacturing sector still under pressure

The manufacturing cycle has been a mixed bag so far, with most EEMEA economies still below the expansion threshold. This was led by new orders, except in Russia where war-related manufacturing has boosted activity albeit with the rate of expansion consistently slowing since March 2024. The relatively resilient global trade has not necessarily benefited EEMEA – new export orders also remain below the expansion mark, although Turkey rebounded 50.5 in August likely helped by its improved price competitiveness. Encouragingly, signs of excess capacity have continued to ease, particularly in CEE where orders minus output have been consistently improving.

## Domestic demand still has legs

Economic growth has been broadly resilient, with global and regional headwinds (slowdown, policy tightening, regional conflicts) ofset by tailwinds (strong labour markets, regional financial support). Although selected EEMEA central banks have already delivered considerable monetary policy easing, which should support growth, the lagged impact of global tightening is yet to be seen across the region. For example, Figure 3 shows how narrow money supply growth leads private domestic demand with a 9-month lag, pointing to potential downward pressure in the remainder of the year.

![](images/d695499d055cc2bb031d305cec32a2b0aacecab524c394a829f72216aa46d692.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 2. Trade has been under pressure
![](images/05ed49e30154d5fb1cf00f27269a7bc70dd3bb3419dfad70ee3bf5c635fa052d.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

For the whole of EEMEA, we forecast relatively stable real GDP growth this year, at 2.8% y/y. This might be seen as a surprise as growth was very weak in CEE in 2023. However, countries such as Turkiye are going through a diferent growth cycle, afecting the headline figure. While narrow money supply growth points to a potential slowdown in private domestic demand, strong labour markets, particularly wage growth across CEE, provides an instrumental boost. Disinflation has also boosted households' purchasing power while some central banks (eg, NBH, CNB) have already delivered considerable policy easing, which should support the credit cycle. Yet, headline inflation is set to rebound in some countries and the lagged efects from global policy tightening may still carry on. While domestic demand has remained in the driving seat, EEMEA has lagged the rebound in global trade volumes, in contrast to Latam and EM Asia (excluding China). Capex has been the biggest drag on growth, led by Hungary, where capex fell by 13.3% y/y in Q2, followed by South Africa (-7.7% y/y) on the back of unfavourable base efects, weak business confidence amid election uncertainty and some cyclical factors. Capex also slowed sharply in Turkey (-5.6% y/y in machinery investment). Moreover, oil exporters (eg, Saudi Arabia) may have been pressured to use capex as an adjustment valve as commodity prices remain under pressure.

FIGURE 3. Leading indicator points to weaker demand
![](images/406086f94d18fde4be11368b5d724d4d650e97840a1178831c8412ad0cde70ae.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 4. A post-pandemic divergent growth rebound
![](images/e79a0d0c7000f691604ac13d69a9d671783bed5660f2293f41a04423b8322c83.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

Sequentially, real growth was below expectations across the region, especially in Turkey and CEE. In Turkey, investment and net exports weighed on economic performance and all leading indicators point to a further slowdown from Q3 24, which should help macroeconomic rebalancing. We see risks slightly skewed to the downside and keep our forecasts unchanged at 3.0% in 2024 and 3.2% in 2025. In South Africa, we also maintain our forecasts, at 0.8% in 2024 and 1.5% in 2025, amid a turnaround in electricity production and positive political sentiment that is underpinning household and government consumption; and anticipated policy rate cuts should help stabilise growth.

In contrast, CEE has shown greater divergence: Poland delivered a much stronger performance in H1 24 (2.3% vs 0.6% on average for its CEE peers) thanks to robust household demand on the back of strong wage growth, stronger-than-expected investment and an election-led fiscal impulse. We thus raise our forecast by 0.2pp to 3.1% for 2024 but keep our 3.9% forecast for 2025. Hungary has lagged its CEE peers since mid-2022 with an even weaker performance in H1 24, led by a record 15.4% y/y contraction in fixed investment (the biggest since 1996) amid limited EU funding and the freezing of public investments. We hence trim our growth forecast for 2024 by 0.9pp to 1.6% and and for 2025 by 0.5pp to 3.0% despite a lower base. Romania has seen some slowdown in growth but remains the best performer across CEE thanks to elevated wage growth, steady EU funding and election-led fiscal impulse. Amid the ongoing conflicts, which continue to weigh on growth, we trim our growth forecast for Israel in 2024 by 1pp to 0.9% reflecting still-elevated uncertainty but we trim 2025 forecasts by 0.4pp to 3.1%. Russia has been an outlier, showing strong resilience as the economy continues to adapt to international pressure and with strong domestic demand (supported by fiscal spending, state orders, high wage and credit growth rates) ofsetting negative spillovers. As a result, we raise our 2024 growth forecast by 0.3pp to 3.6% but trim 2025 by 0.3pp to 1.8% due to monetary tightening and unfavourable base efects. The current dynamics of demand pose upside risks to our 2024 forecast, while risks of tighter monetary conditions and lower oil prices next year may slow economic activity more than we expect.

## No one size fits all across EEMEA central banks

The average policy rate of the six main central banks in our EEMEA universe (Hungary, Poland, Russia, South Africa, Czechia and Romania) fell 0.9pp to 8.1% in the first half of this year, before ticking up to 8.4% in July when Russia delivered a 200bp hike to 18%. As the pace of disinflation has slowed materially in recent months, there are some questions about the ability of central banks to deliver more cuts. For the rest of this year and into 2025, we expect very limited

improvement in inflation, which we expect to remain c0.5pp above pre-Covid-19 levels at YE25. Also, some central banks argue that the neutral real interest rate (r\*) will be higher than the prepandemic level.

This backdrop might suggest that there isn't much room left for rate cuts. However, while we agree that the room for cuts is more limited than in the past two years, we still see reasonable scope: we forecast 0.4pp on average in the rest of 2024 and 1.3pp on average in 2025, for three main reasons. First, the region's growth has recovered more weakly so far in 2024 than previously expected and is likely to remain below potential over our forecast horizon. Following some normalisation in the inflation trend, some central banks in the region, like the CNB in Czechia, have started to focus more on growth, with more balanced rhetoric in terms of taking growth (as well as inflation) into account. Second, there was a major improvement in the expost real policy rate from around -8% in early 2023 to +3.4% in July 2024. This is materially above the historical average of +0.6% over 2016-2020. According to our policy rate and inflation forecasts, we expect a very gradual fall in the ex-post real policy rate to 2.7% by YE25. Third, the upcoming easing cycles by the Fed and the ECB are likely to provide some relief and make it easier for EM central banks to cut their policy rates. The latest statement of the NBH in Hungary notes that there may be scope for cautiously lowering interest rates in the coming period, depending on: 1) the policy rate trajectories of global central banks; 2) the domestic inflation trend; and 3) Hungary's risk perception. At the press briefing, Deputy Governor Virag said that the rate decisions of global central banks will be the most important criteria for the NBH.

Fourth, the disinflation process is still well underway in South Africa, where easing is only likely to commence from September, in our view, and should continue, albeit gradually, for some 6-9 months. We still expect a relatively shallow cycle of some 100bp of easing, and now believe that the final cut will occur in May 2025 (previously July). Similarly, Kenya has only just begun its easing cycle too, and we expect the pace of accommodation to gather steam in the coming months. Nigeria's inflation only peaked a month ago, and we believe the monetary authorities there may start to engineer monetary accommodation at the final meeting of the year, in November. However, this could be delayed until early 2025 given the unexpected 40-45% increase in domestic fuel prices that was implemented during the first week of September - especially if the renewed FX stability/appreciation that we expect on improved sentiment from the coming online of domestic refineries does not occur. In Turkey, we now expect the CBT to start easing its policy rate in November (vs our previous forecast of January 2025). We expect the CBT to continue with 250bp cuts in each meeting, taking the policy rate down to 25% by August 2025, with the ex-post real policy rate turning negative in September 2025. In Israel, we expect the BoI to be guided mainly by local factors and to stay on hold until mid-Q2 25, with risks that easing may be postponed to H2 25.

The NBR in Romania delivered two 25bp cuts in the summer to 6.50%. Romania's ex-post real policy rate at 1.9% is roughly in line with Poland's and lower than the 2.7% in Hungary and the 2.6% in Czechia. Given Romania's lower credit rating, looser fiscal policy and limited improvement in the inflation outlook, we see limited space left for the NBR to deliver more cuts in the rest of this year. That said, we think the risks to our call are clearly to the downside (ie, of more cuts).

Russia is the only country in the EEMEA space where we see risks of further tightening, from the current 18% rate by 100-200bp, if inflation pressure, heated by strong domestic demand and high and unanchored inflation expectations, does not ease.

## Election cycle is not over yet

As well as the heavy EEMEA election calendar and growing interplay with geo-economic factors, US elections have traditionally been of outsized importance for EEMEA through diferent channels of multi-lateralism, foreign aid and geopolitical risks. The heavy election calendar has been pivotal to some policy shifts (eg, Turkey, Egypt) where election uncertainty has led to policy reform, and where electoral outcomes have held the promise of reform (South Africa). Yet, fiscal policy has equally faced upward pressure amid populist measures ahead of polls (eg, Ghana, Romania and Turkiye earlier this year). The past four years have been dominated by a greater return to multi-lateralism, especially during the outbreak of the Russia-Ukraine conflict, with closer coordination with European and NATO allies. While a potential Harris win could mark some policy continuity, a Trump win may revive tensions with Iran, similar to his 2018 'maximum pressure' policy, which may ultimately exacerbate an already fragile geopolitical landscape. The unusual binary nature of US elections has kept uncertainty relatively high, shaping policy choices through 2024. For example, the Russia-Ukraine conflict has been closely shaped by upcoming US election uncertainty.

The EEMEA election calendar has been eventful so far this year, and will remain so for the rest of the year. Romania is set to hold pivotal presidential and general elections on 24 November and 1 December, respectively. Recent polls<sup>37</sup> show the ruling coalition to be well positioned, although the election campaign has come with some fiscal loosening. In Ghana, too, fiscal discipline is likely to weaken ahead of the vote – we forecast a 5.5% of GDP deficit that is higher than the oficial projection at 4.2%. Encouragingly, however, both the ruling party and the opposition have committed to post-election fiscal discipline, albeit difering on modalities. In Mozambique, campaigning has begun in earnest, with the electoral air waves heavily dominated by ruling party Frente de Libertação de Moçambique (FRELIMO), which has ruled the country since independence in 1975 and whose flag-bearer Daniel Chapo will face leading opposition party Resistência Nacional Moçambicana's (RENAMO) Ossufo Momade and 33 other parties in the upcoming 9 October elections. Meanwhile, the newly formed Democratic Alliance Coalition has continued to attract leaders from across the political divide. A FRELIMO victory would likely be seen as a continuation of the status quo, as Chapo is unlikely to deviate significantly from the policies of current president Filipe Nyusi. A RENAMO victory, on the other hand, may present the country with an opportunity to explore a more liberal framework, given its historical calls for FRELIMO to abandon its socialist policies. RENAMO, which has 60 out of 250 seats in parliament, won 21.5% of the 2019 general elections. Tunisia's 6 October presidential elections should be the last step in the broad-based consolidation of power over the past three years, laying the ground for a new period of longer policy continuity which is not punctuated by electoral schedules. This should quickly shift policy priorities to long-delayed unpopular but necessary reforms and the resumption of IMF talks. Jordan's general elections on 10 September could mark the start of a new path for a multiparty system there, following the 2022 constitutional amendments and new electoral laws. However, despite a reshaped parliament based on more organised parties, the 2022 amendments have considerably expanded the king's powers and the voting system still highly favours tribal connections. This points to only modest incremental shifts in the political backdrop over time.

The election calendar will be considerably lighter next year but elections will still drive policy choices in some countries. For example, in Poland's presidential elections in May 2025, if the candidate of the Tusk-headed government wins, this would likely be a positive development in terms of the reform agenda as some critical reforms required by the EU need presidential approval. According to a recent poll conducted by IBRIS conducted on 30-31 August, the coalition government maintains a leading position with total support slightly over 50%. Egypt will hold parliamentary elections in October 2025 which may weaken fiscal discipline eforts in the run up to the polls. In Ivory Coast, President Ouattara has still not confirmed if he will be standing for a fourth term in the October 2025 presidential election or hand over to a younger constant, as he has consistently said he would. Meanwhile, former President Gbagbo, who has indicated that he will contest the elections, has called for all opposition parties to unite under a single umbrella that would likely be led by former Credit Suisse head Tidjane Thiam. We would expect markets to respond positively to both a Thiam (younger generation, fresh ideas) and a Ouattara (peace and continuity) victory, as both leaders are perceived to be market friendly. In Cameroon, voter apathy has helped extend the rule of Paul Biya, Africa’s second longest serving president, of more than 40 years, and we see no reason why this will change in the upcoming October 2025 elections. The ruling party currently has 152 out of 180 seats in the parliament.

## Geopolitical risks: neither out of sight nor out of mind

Relative resilience to recent bouts of market volatility may contrast with growing geopolitical risks. The Middle East has often been central to the latter, but the summer lull has been challenged by a much broader malaise, from Venezuela's election crisis across LatAm to EM Asia's popular uprising. As with other EM regions around the world, risks have been broader across EEMEA following Ukraine's incursion into Russian territory and violent popular protests in Kenya and Nigeria against policy tightening. Such geopolitical tensions have been compounded by a heavy global election calendar, higher global rates and growing concerns about the health of the global economy and how La Niña may afect agriculture and the mining sector across all EMs.

Risks to regional stability with potential ripple efects to the global economy are, in our view, their most elevated since the outbreak of conflict between Hamas and Israel on 7 October. Yet, we think the risk of major escalation remains contained but with sustained de-escalation primarily dependent on domestic political factors more than regional diplomacy. Regional spillovers have been broadly averted so far despite regular Houthi attacks, which have curtailed the maritime route through Bab al Mandeb, hostilities between Hezbollah and Israel in the north and the unprecedented direct Iranian attack on Israel. Yet, the assassinations of Hezbollah's Shukr and Hamas's Haniyeh have significantly raised the risks of a dangerous escalation that could prove far more challenging to contain. As such, active international diplomacy has aimed to revive ceasefire talks in Gaza. Yet, it  has not delivered meaningful results, while many aspects of a potential deal remain unresolved. In addition, pressure from

PM Netanyahu's far-right coalition are a challenge to any ceasefire agreement, keeping the risk of potential retaliation relatively high. Similar to April, the US has reinforced its military assets across the region (NY Post, 12 August), but this time mutual deterrence may be stretched by any escalation that could quickly spill into a more regional conflict. Indeed, Iranian oficials have repeatedly stated the need to retaliate for the assassination of Ismael Haniyeh on its soil (New York Times, 9 August). We believe Israel's domestic political backdrop remains crucial to the balance of risks since any early elections could quickly shift the focus inwards, helping defuse regional tensions.

## Czechia: Turning focus to growth

## Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

Growth is recovering gradually but it will to remain below its potential in 2024. In addition to the slow economic recovery, an ongoing normalisation in wage-growth pressure, a disciplined fiscal outlook and the rate trajectories of global central banks should help the CNB to continue easing, with at least two measured cuts of 25bp from the remaining three meetings in 2024. We expect an additional 75bp of cuts, to 3.25%, in 2025, likely to be delivered in H1 25.

• FX: We maintain our negative bias on the CZK and expect it to underperform EUR and its CEE peers. The Czech economy remains weak. Lacklustre external and domestic demand are unlikely to change given the grim outlook for the German manufacturing sector. Consequently, we expect the CNB to continue its easing cycle and to potentially change its CZK projections, allowing for more prolonged weakness. The carry erosion along with revised CNB FX projection should reinforce the CZK depreciation trend.

• Rates: Czech rates should be supported across maturities by global and local factors. The start of core central bank easing cycles (the ECB in particular) should support the front end of the Czech curve together with the ongoing CNB easing cycle. Moreover, there is a non-trivial risk that the CNB might need to reduce its policy rates to a sub-neutral level since the economic recovery is slow, while inflation is already at the target. Weak growth, monetary policy on an easing track, as well as tight fiscal discipline should support the back end of the CZK curve, where we expect rates to continue to converge to German Bunds. As a result, we see value in receiving 5y5y CZK IRS.

## Growth is recovering, though remains below potential

Growth in Q2 24 was strong by Czechia standards, at 0.3% q/q. That followed stronger growth of 0.4% q/q in Q1 24, which was the fastest q/q expansion since Q1 22. An annualised growth rate of 1.3% is primarily supported by final domestic demand at 0.6% q/q or 2.6% in annualised terms.

Following a contraction of 1.2% q/q in the previous quarter, fixed-investment growth in Q2 24 also returned to a positive 1.9% q/q. Exports of goods remain weak, expanding only 0.3% q/q, after a negative print of 0.1% q/q in Q1 as growth in Czechia's main export partners remains weak.

Strong wage growth and employment conditions continue to support domestic factors, albeit at a slower rate than previously expected as households’ continue to save more. Additionally, wage growth slowed to 6.5% y/y in Q2 24, the lowest print since Q4 22. In real terms it corresponds to 3.9% y/y, lower than consensus expectation of 4.3% and the CNB's forecast of 4.6% y/y, published in early August. Likewise, we do not expect stronger investment growth in Q2 24 to be sustainable in H2 24 as funding flow from the EU funds are likely to be below longterm average due to cyclical factors. Against this backdrop, we maintain our growth forecasts at 1.4% y/y for 2024 and 2.2% for 2025.

## Fiscal policy remains tight

Czechia is one of the few countries in the region, or even in the world, undertaking successful fiscal consolidation. The central state budget narrowed to CZK176bn in the first five months of this year, down from CZK194bn in the same period in 2023. While keeping expenditures stable, revenues increased 2% y/y, helping to improve the budget balance by 10% y/y. In terms of consolidated balance, the oficial deficit target of 2.2% for 2024 looks ambitious, given last year's figure of 3.7%, but we expect the YE24 figure to be very close this, at 2.5%. In line with our expectations, the finance ministry revised up its budget deficit forecast to 2.5% of GDP, and

aims to improve this figure to 2.1% of GDP in 2025, or CZK 230bn, down from CZK252bn in 2024. In terms of financing the deficit in 2025, the main channel will continue to be long-term domestic borrowing, planned to be cover 96% of the budget deficit, up from 83% in 2024.

## Inflation to remain within the target band in the forecast horizon

A negative output gap and fiscal consolidation have made possible a strong and sustainable disinflation path. CPI dropped to levels in line with the inflation target of 2% (+/- 1%) in January this year and has remained within the target band since.

In our forecast horizon until year-end 2025, we expect CPI to remain consistently within the target band, which puts Czechia in a unique position among the CE4. It is not only headline but core CPI that also remains within the target band, suggesting that unlike other EMs, the improvement in the inflation outlook is not only attributable to cyclical corrections in food or energy.

## Time to adopt more balanced monetary policy

After remaining as one of the most hawkish central banks in the region or even globally, the CNB adopted a more balanced approach from 2024, as inflation moved back to target levels. In its communications, the CNB focused more on the growth outlook compared to the past.

Despite some acceleration in the last few quarters, Czechia has by far the slowest growth performance since the beginning of COVID-19. Compared to 1.2% cumulative economic growth in Czechia, economic growth in other CE4 members was stronger, at 11% on average. Quick normalisation in wage-growth pressure, a disciplined fiscal outlook and the rate trajectories of global central banks should help the CNB to continue its measures cuts of 25bp. We expect two more 25bp cuts in the remaining three MPC meetings of the year, likely to be delivered on 25 September and 7 November, to 4.0%. That said, the risks on our call are clearly skewed to downside as the CNB might deliver one more 25bp cut in the last meeting of the year on 19 December. We expect an additional 75bp cut to 3.25% in 2025, likely to be delivered in H1 25.

## Egypt: A window of opportunity

## Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Egypt's generous financial support has ofered a window of opportunity to deliver on structural reforms while being insulated from market pressure in coming years. Yet, downside risks are non negligible due to the scale of medium-term funding needs.

• Credit: Egypt credit remains stuck between limited near-term/liquidity concerns and a more uncertain medium-term fundamental trajectory. This makes it primarily a carry investment, in our view. However, the still elevated yields continue to justify an Overweight rating, and we also note that Egypt’s USD bonds have lagged some African and Middle Eastern HY peers over the past few weeks, as well as the EUR-denominated bonds, which now screen as expensive relative to the USD bonds based on historical trading ranges. We recommend a switch from EGYPT EUR 2026s into \$7.5% 2027s.

## Ras el Hekma and the window of opportunity

Egypt's sovereign risk perception has drastically shifted after securing an unprecedented UAE lifeline from Ras al Hikma, compounded with an upsized IMF loan. Yet, Egypt's medium-term challenges remain complex and significant, with a narrow window of opportunity to put the financial support to work to deliver socially challenging structural reforms.

So far, the macro adjustment has focused on closing the funding gap, reducing arrears, and liberalizing the exchange rate and regulated prices. For example, Egypt's ambitious asset sales have targeted hard currency revenues from the Gulf and less so on improving competition, governance, transparency, to name a few. As such gross funding needs and FX misalignments have often been central to markets' concerns. Yet, harnessing the capacity of Egypt's sizable and younger population remains the country's biggest challenge; one that will shape the outlook for years to come.

FIGURE 11. Growing importance of seignorage and inflation tax
![](images/502f9b9c63e697648080acb5e26905abd2f4e5a740b005add7eae18dad8ae0b3.jpg)
Source: CBE, WB, Barclays Research

FIGURE 12. Growing interest burden
![](images/712c4c174a3ae5249cbc571590ffb9fd77ce37d6a7876516561ef349cd65082f.jpg)

## Source: MoF, Barclays Research

Egypt's population has nearly quadrupled over the past six decades and, according to UN projections, should almost double to 200mn by the end of the century, under a medium fertility scenario. Growth of the labour force has thus been steadily increasing by an average 533k/yr over the past two decades, putting downward pressure on labour participation. The outsized share of the parallel sector clearly understates pressure in the labour market. For example, the 16-64 working-age population increased by 1.2m/y over the past two decades. As such, the

combination of an already sizable population, higher fertility rates, and a relatively younger population will be crucial to economic growth and social stability over the medium term. UAE's unprecedented financial lifeline may thus fail to meet lingering financing needs in the absence of key reforms to unlock full economic potential.

## Sizable challenges: the interest burden's headache

Economic volatility has been high over the past six decades, with the economy failing to sustain a relatively long period of higher growth. Structural bottlenecks and economic rigidity have kept growth well below potential and unable to structurally alter the allocation of resources, a prolonged downtrend in relative TFP vs regional peers, and fiscal dominance.

Egypt's elevated debt levels and prolonged budget deficits have been sustained for several decades but the interest burden has reached very challenging levels, diluting the impact of other fiscal reforms. In fact, savings from reduced subsidies have been more than ofset by higher interest payments in recent years. The latter's share in total revenues fell by nearly 10pp to 54.5% in May (thanks to one of Ras el Hikma transfers) but is projected to rebound to 69% in FY 24-25, one of the highest levels in the world.

## External rebalancing between tailwinds and headwinds

FX shortages temporarily helped narrow the current account deficit by \$11.8bn to \$4.7bn in FY22-23, thanks to an import contraction, before widening again to \$17.1bn over the first nine months of FY23-24. The negative impact of the Israel-Hamas war has been only marginal since current account receipts have embraced a steady downtrend from their Q4 22 peak of \$105bn. In fact, the \$18.5bn decline in receipts was almost equally split between goods' exports and services. Petroleum exports fell by \$10.2bn in 2023, with most of the decline preceding gas supply disruptions from Israel. A lack of investments and a natural rate of decline has reduced output from Zohr at a time when domestic demand has remained steady, leading to blackout periods during the peak summer season.

Structural factors including the lack of diversified and competitive non-energy exports, sticky imports led by mega projects, lower global remittances, higher interest burden, etc, more than cyclical factors have weighed on external rebalancing. As such, the record increase in reserves to \$54bn in July is unlikely to be repeated and the total should face steady downward pressure over the medium term, led by temporary drags (eg Suez Canal due to ongoing Houthi attacks) and more structural factors (eg, net energy exports). In the meanwhile, we believe the central bank is likely to extend its focus on FX stability, limiting volatility within the implicit target band of 46.5-49 against the dollar. This should remain a key anchor for disinflation although renewed price liberalisation should extend upward pressure on headline inflation limiting the central bank's ability to start the easing cycle. We hence expect all policy rates to stay on hold through end-2024.

## Ghana: Diferent election, same playbook

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

Incoming fiscal data point to prudent fiscal management in the first seven months of the year.
However, we remain concerned that there could be significant pre-elections slippage ahead.

• Credit: Ghana launched its exchange ofer and consent solicitation on 5 September. We see fair value exit yields in the 11-12% range and hence think that exit yields implied by current market prices of c.13-13.5% still leave some further potential upside for bonds post-exchange and in the medium term. We hence maintain an OW rating on Ghana sovereign credit. However, we are less certain that such upside will be realised in the short term, given the uncertainties around the elections and the potential for profit-taking by investors that have participated in the debt exchange but may now prefer to stay on the sidelines until election uncertainties clear. We believe a successful conclusion of the sovereign debt restructuring would be positive for Ghana-related corporate credits such as Tullow Oil and Kosmos Energy. However, Tullow still needs to address sizeable bond debt due in 2026; the balance of risks leads us to a MW rating. We prefer Kosmos Energy (OW) which benefits from greater geographic diversification of assets, and as bonds have lagged sectoral performance QTD.

## Election promises pile up

Both the ruling centre-right National Patriotic Party (NPP) and the opposition centre-left National Democratic Congress (NDC) have published manifestos aimed at addressing gaps in infrastructure, health, education, taxation, and corruption. The ruling party has touted its success with its flagship free senior high school (SHS) education programme, and has promised to extend the programme by incentivising teachers and increasing the stock of student accommodation. The opposition’s manifesto does not just promise significant improvements to the free SHS programme, but also has a ‘no academic fees’ policy for all first-year students in public tertiary institutions. Both parties promise tertiary education scholarships for people with disabilities. As has often been the case in past elections, both parties have promised to subject themselves to sound fiscal discipline, including taking advice from an independent fiscal council/public debt management ofice, and to embark on several tax reforms.

• The NPP has reiterated its commitment to fiscal consolidation through expenditure rationalisation and revenue measures including a broadening of the tax net, simplification of the tax system, deepening digitization of tax administration, reducing leakages, harmonizing the VAT regime, and bringing down selected withholding taxes.

• The NDC has promised to reset the entire fiscal framework via a four-year fiscal consolidation programme, public expenditure management reforms, and a review of the VAT and importlevy regime with a view to lighten the tax burden while improving compliance. In addition, it plans to introduce a ‘24-hour economy’ with the intention of running the economy in three eight-hour shifts to help drive growth and employment, while supporting the fiscus. In addition, the NDC has said it will scrap the COVID levy. Both parties intend to reduce the size of government, abolish the controversial E-Levy and cancel the gaming tax.

## External debt restructuring on track

The Minister of Finance launched the much-anticipated commercial debt exchange ofer agreed with bondholders on 5 September, to allow the country to achieve some USD4.4bn in debt service savings and USD4.7bn in debt cancellation over the life of the IMF programme. The ofer, which gives investors the choice between a low coupon par bond or higher coupon alternatives with a 37% notional haircut, closes on 20 September for early consent. A successful execution would lend positive support to the upcoming third IMF review that was oficially scheduled for 1 November but which the finance minister now expects to happen in late December. Given the

elections, we believe there is a clear risk that it is in fact delayed until February/March 2025, especially if there is a change of government. According to the Minister, the IMF will deploy a mission to the country from 24 September to 4 October 2024, for the third review.

## Budget deficit within target, but risks remain

Incoming data show the country’s budget deficit rose to 2.4% of GDP in the first seven months of the year – marginally above the 2% that was reported for January-June, and below the targeted 2.8% of GDP, thanks largely to 1.2% of GDP in interest savings from external debt restructuring, a low capital budget implementation rate, and tax-revenue growth that is broadly in line with budget. Looking forward, we note that, in line with previous election cycles, a number of key capital projects have recently been announced and will likely lead to a rebound in capex outlays ahead of the 7 December elections. Requests for additional funds to address the dry spell in the northern parts of the country despite the onset of rains, including the potential provision of household cash transfers, farmer inputs, produce purchases, etc, are an extra source of spending concern, in addition to the usual overshoot in wages. We stick with our forecast of a full-year deficit of 5.5% of GDP vs the oficial estimate of 4.2%.

## Gold and oil revenues buoy external account

With regards to the external sector, we note that the trade balance is reported to have improved from USD1.6bn in H1 23 to USD1.81bn in H1 24, thanks to strong gold and crude oil exports at a time when import growth – especially oil – was less buoyant. The improved trade surplus, aided by higher inward remittances helped to lift the current account to a surplus of USD1.28bn in H1 24, from USD863.0mn in H123. The capital and financial account showed some improvement too, with net outflows declining to USD367mn from USD1.04bn in 2023. This allowed the headline BOP position to swing from a deficit of USD341mn in H1 23 to a surplus of USD942.3mn in H1 24, and the accumulation of international FX reserves during the first six months of this year. Even so, we note that the currency continued to weaken throughout the first half of the year, suggesting that there could be some FX leakage/excessive cedi liquidity. During election years, it is not uncommon for such additional liquidity to be driven by fiscal excesses.

## Hungary: Need for speed

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

We lower our growth forecasts by 0.9pp to 1.6% for 2024 and 0.5pp to 3.0% for 2025, due mainly to dim EU funding prospects remaining a major drag on the growth outlook. We expect the NBH to deliver two more 25bp cuts in the rest of this year, likely at the September and November meetings. The appointment of the new governor in March 2025 is likely to be the most important development in terms of the monetary policy trajectory.

• Credit: Hungary credit's medium-term outlook appears uninspiring, amid a lower growth outlook and continued tensions with the EU limiting funding flows. However, supply-demand technicals look supportive in the near term, given likely no further eurobond supply this year and notable demand from Chinese investors (see The China bid goes global, 1 August 2024). We maintain a Market Weight rating on Hungary sovereign credit, but see some value in USD bonds in the 5y sector (buy REPHUN \$ 2028s, 2029s), which also screen as wide relative to CDS and EUR bonds.

• FX: We expect HUF to remain under pressure due to its highly positive beta to global risk sentiment, which could deteriorate sharply as concerns on a US recession intensify. In addition, we expect Hungary's still-poor economic momentum and ongoing carry erosion to add further pressure on the HUF in the medium term. That said, in the near term, we think that any excessive HUF depreciation would be followed by hawkish turns in NBH's rhetoric. This should cap the near-term weakness in HUF.

• Rates: Our economist expects the NBH to deliver two more 25bp cuts in 2024, followed by another 125bp worth of cuts in 2025, broadly in line with current market pricing. The risks, however, are skewed towards a slower easing pace and higher terminal rate amid mounting pressures on the HUF. Consequently, we continue to see value in paying 1y1y HUF rate. We believe the back end of the HUF curve should continue to ofer significant term premium as slower economic growth will translate into looser fiscal policy. We therefore expect the HUF curve to steepen over the forecast horizon.

## Growth lagging behind peers

Hungary, together with Poland, led CEE growth performance in the post Covid-19 catch-up period. However, this has gradually reversed since mid-2022 and performance continued to deteriorate in H1 24. Hungary grew only 0.5% in H1 24, making it the worst performer in CE4, behind Poland at 2.5%, Czechia at 0.8% and Romania at 0.6%. Hungary's fixed investment growth at -15.4% y/y in Q2 24 was its weakest since the series started in 1996. This is likely related to the limited EU funding given both structural and cyclical factors, exacerbate by the freezing of public investment.

## Lower funding from the EU

In December 2023, the EC released €10.2bn to Hungary from normal budgetary funds for the next seven years while keeping a total of nearly €9bn frozen until the country achieves 27 supermilestones. In its latest oficial report published in July, the EC noted that there has been no major progress and no change from its recommendations last year. Total RRF funds of €9.8bn (of which €4bn in loans and the remaining grants) also remain frozen. Against this backdrop, it appears that total available funding will remain below the historical average and that of regional peers, which does not bode well for the growth outlook. We lower our growth forecast for 2024 by 0.3pp to 2.2% y/y and for 2025 by 0.5pp to 3.0% y/y.

## C/A surplus to remain around current levels in H2 24

Despite weaker growth prospects in the EU afecting export performance negatively, Hungary's C/A surplus improved to €4.2bn (2.1% of GDP) in June 2024, up from €1bn (0.5% of GDP) in 2023. The strong performance was related mainly to support from finished FDI, especially in electric car battery investments. We expect the positive trend to stabilise, with domestic demand recovering over the rest of year, and for the C/A balance to be at 2.5% of GDP at YE24.

## Budget deficit is very likely to exceed targets in 2024 and 2025

The budget registered a deficit of 6.1% of GDP in 2023, well above the revised target of 5.2% (up from 3.9% previously). The fiscal target for 2024 was also revised up, to 4.5% of GDP from 2.9% previously, as the Ministry of Finance now envisages a gradual path for fiscal consolidation, aiming to bring the budget deficit down to 1.9% of GDP by 2028. There was some improvement in the budget deficit to 5.1% of GDP as of July 2024. Based on the latest oficial communication, budget balance targets are likely to be unchanged at 4.5% for 2024 and 3.7% for 2025. The updated plans will be submitted in November. We expect slower economic growth to make it dificult to achieve fiscal targets.

## Scope for two 25bp cuts in the next four meetings

At its latest MPC meeting in late August, the NBH kept its policy rate on hold at 6.75% and the accompanying statement highlighted the importance of reducing/anchoring inflation expectations and the stability of financial markets as the most important components of achieving price stability on a sustainable basis. The recent volatility in financial markets, especially in the first half of August driven by a series of global events (fears of recession in the US, financial market developments in Japan and geopolitical tensions in the Middle East) in addition to a limited deterioration in the domestic inflation outlook in July were highlighted as the main drivers of the on-hold decision in the statement. The statement notes possible scope for cautiously lowering interest rates further in the coming period, depending on the: 1) policy rate trajectories of global central banks; 2) the domestic inflation trend; and 3) Hungary's risk perception. The next meetings of the ECB and Fed are scheduled to be held on 12 and 18 September, respectively, with the next NBH meeting on 24 September. At the press briefing, Deputy Governor Virag said that this is the most important criteria. Against this backdrop, we still expect the NBH to deliver two more 25bp cuts in the rest of this year, likely at the September and November meetings. At his post-decision press briefing, Deputy Governor Virag said that although the NBH still sees room for one or two 25bp cuts, the balance is tipped towards two, in line with our expectations. Based on our inflation forecasts, we see scope for 150bp of cuts to 4.75% in 2025. The appointment of the new governor in March 2025 is likely to be the most important development in terms of the monetary policy trajectory.

## Israel: Walking a fine line

Zalina Alborova, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK | Valentina Stoykova, Barclays, UK

• FX: We continue to view the ILS as fundamentally undervalued amid large risk premia embedded in USDILS. Shekel should remain volatile and driven by geopolitical headlines in the near term. In the long term, however, we expect ILS to appreciate as soon as geopolitical risks start fading.

• Rates: The BoI cutting cycle is very uncertain and contingent on war developments, but our economist's base case sees 75bp worth of cuts in 2025 against market pricing of 65bp. Belly and long-term rates have deviated significantly from their US counterparts amid local geopolitical risks. In fact, it seems that some of the large risk premia initially seen in USDILS are now more reflected in rates space. Even though in the near term the geopolitical situation remains very uncertain, in the medium term we see value in Israeli rates and consider them to be among the laggards in the current global easing cycle. Consequently, we recommend receiving 2y2y ILS IRS.

• Credit: Our MW rating on Israel Corporates is driven by ongoing geopolitical uncertainties despite relatively wide spreads within EEMEA and solid fundamentals of both banks and corporates. Among banks, we prefer subs over seniors on demonstrated resilience of fundamentals and high carry in Tier2s, which is also the key reason for our OW rating on HAPOAL and our UW rating on IDBILI. Among the gas credits, we see Leviathan (OW) as the most defensive exposure given its long life asset base, solid track record of free cash flow, and plans to address its 2025 bond maturity with cash flow/bank facilities.

Prolonged geopolitical uncertainty. Since our last EMQ in June, hostage/ceasefire talks have not progressed, while many aspects of a potential deal remain unresolved, including the control of the Philadelphi corridor – a major obstacle to a deal. Alongside this, risks of a wider escalation have increased after the assassination of the Hamas political leader in Iran at the end of July. However, while conflict remains contained between Israel and Hamas, we think fears of wider escalation with Hezbollah and/or Iran will remain until a ceasefire in Gaza is reached.

The complex dynamics of internal politics. Increased pressure from the far right exacerbates domestic tensions but also limits the chance of a hostage-ceasefire deal. In particular, Ben Gvir and Smotrich have warned that they would exit the coalition (Jerusalem Post, 4 September 2024) if Israel agrees to withdraw from Gaza-Egypt border, while their hawkish politics in the West Bank and holy sites of Jerusalem have led to increased confrontation with the Arab countries in the region. Despite the nationwide strike urging the hostage deal to be prioritised, PM Netanyahu reiterated (BBC News, 4 September 2024) that control of the Gaza-Egypt border is essential for Israel's security. In addition, increased pressure to appoint the president and two justices of the Supreme Court may bring judicial discussions onto the agenda, posing risks of a constitution crisis. This, together with a potential return of the Haredi conscription discussions, a bill on funding religious councils and/or the 'Rabbis Bill' (both promoted by the Shas party) may heighten tensions within the coalition, while Ben Gvir will likely reiterate his threats (Times of Israel, 5 August 2024) to block voting and demand inclusion to security meetings and warrelated decisions.

Prolonged conflict adds pressure to the Israeli economy. According to the preliminary estimates of the Israeli Stat Ofice (CBS), weaker than expected economic activity in Q2 24 was driven by deceleration in most expenditure components (private consumption, investments, exports and imports), while only government consumption growth increased in Q2 24 relative to the previous quarter (8.2% q/q saar from 2.6% q/q saar in Q1 24). Alongside this, the CBS

upgraded its Q1 24 estimate by 2.9pp to 17.3% q/q saar and revised the 2023 quarterly path. The labour market remains tight<sup>38</sup> : labour force shortage is more noticeable in the construction sector, while mobilisation of the reservists and the prolonged war increase pressure on aggregate economic activity. Marking to market, our GDP growth forecast for 2024 drops to 0.9% y/y from 1.9% y/y at the time of the June EMQ. We see both upside and downside risks to economic activity in H2 24, given uncertainty about the prospect of ceasefire talks, risks of larger protests (including new strikes) and potential retaliation from Iran and/or Hezbollah in the near term.

Inflationary pressure manageable but elevated. Over the last few months, inflation has been gradually accelerating, reaching 3.2% y/y in July (vs 1-3% target range), driven largely by nontradable components. The summer months saw increased contributions from transport (travelling costs amid volatile FX and a low number of flights) and housing (upward revision of rental contracts). We expect inflation to broadly stay at this level with upside risks from the prolonged conflict and supply-side restrictions, a tight labour market and expansionary fiscal policy, and FX volatility in a scenario of wider escalation.

Widening deficit in focus for rating agencies and the market. By the end of August, the 12m cumulative deficit had widened to 8.3% of GDP vs 6.6% in the 2024 budget plan). We think that the recent announcement of the MinFin on plans to approve the next year budget by the end of 2024, including ILS35bn of adjustments to narrow the deficit to 4% of GDP (in line with the BoI’s recommendations) is a positive signal, especially for the rating agencies. The next reviews by Moody's and S&P are scheduled for 8 November. However, MinFin did not provide details of revenues and spending, while commenting that measures will include a freeze on public sector salaries and on taxation on corporates' and multinationals' extra profits. Likewise, a narrowing of the 2024 deficit to 6.6% of GDP (as in budget plan) by the end of this year seems unlikely amid ongoing military actions.

BoI will wait for more clarity longer than expected. At the end of August, the BoI stayed on hold for the fifth straight time, reiterating cautious guidance and focusing on stabilising the markets amid high geopolitical and budget uncertainty, and pro-inflationary risks. Despite the Fed's dovish tone at Jackson Hole, we expect the BoI to be guided mainly by local factors and to stay on hold until mid-Q2 25, projecting in total three 25bp cuts next year. However, recent comments from Governor Yaron signal that easing may be postponed to H2 25. Next MPC meeting: 9 October 2024, 14:00 UKT.

## Kazakhstan: Weaker growth but stable prospects

Zalina Alborova, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

• Credit: Kazakhstan sovereign bonds are well anchored by solid fundamentals and local ownership, but tight valuations make them unattractive in a global EM sovereign context, in our view, in the absence of new issuance. We hence maintain an Underweight rating on KAZAKS sovereign credit. We see value in the bonds of KazMunayGas (Market Weight) as an alternative to the sovereign, ofering a c.150bp pickup at the long-end of the curve. Fundamentals remain robust — adjusted net leverage was 0.8x at Q2 24 — and we expect further organic free cash flow during H2 24 and 2025 (after dividends). The any and all tender for the \$1bn 2027 bonds, which was announced on 3 September, should also support technicals on the curve.

H1 24 growth slows on the back of abnormal floods and lower oil production: Preliminary estimates of the Kazakhstan Bureau of National Statistics show a slowdown of economic activity to 3.3% y/y in 6M 24 vs 3.2% y/y in 5M 24 and 3.7% y/y in Q1 24. While we expected weaker growth in Q2 24, the deceleration was slightly more pronounced in manufacturing, mining & quarrying sectors, and fixed capital investments. Given these developments, we trim our growth forecast by 0.6pp to 3.9% y/y in 2024 but our forecast for 2025 is unchanged at 4.9% y/y. At the end of August, S&P afirmed Kazakhstan's credit rating at BBB- with a stable outlook<sup>39</sup> , noting positive expectations from planned economic and governance reforms and overall strong macro fundamentals and asset bufers. Following that, Moody's upgraded Kazakhstan's rating by one notch to Baa1, but changed the outlook from positive to stable. The latter was driven by geopolitics and risks of secondary sanctions, while observed improvement of institutional and policy frameworks, and continued diversification of the economy strengthened its credit rating.

Preserving political stability: In his September address to the nation, President Tokayev reiterated his multi-vector foreign policy, the continuation of social-economic reforms (including infrastructure projects, development of the business environment and efective governance regulation) and the upcoming referendum (6 October) on construction of the nuclear power plant (reportedly<sup>40</sup> near the Balkhash lake or at the Kurchatov site). Kazakhstan is one of the world leaders in uranium production, but President Tokayev also supported this project as it can help the country diversify energy sources<sup>41</sup> and reach carbon-neutrality goals. However, local news stories <sup>42</sup> warn about safety issues and risks of increased Russian influence, while potential construction vendors include also Chinese, Korean and French companies.

Drivers behind weak and volatile tenge: After gradual strengthening during the first five months of the year, the tenge weakened by almost 7% at the beginning of summer and stayed volatile and above USDKZT470 in July and August. Such rapid depreciation was driven mainly by reduced FX sales (transfers) from the National Fund (NF) and a seasonal increase of local demand for FX amid the summer holidays. In June, monthly FX sales from the NF dropped to USD420mn from an average of USD853mn in 5M 24 (driven by advance payments, social transfers and compensation to regions afected by floods), while its share in total FX trade fell to 11%. Given the recent announcement on purchase of 12% of Kazatomprom by MinFin (to the NF) and widening budget deficit, we expect increased transfers from the NF in the next few

months, which should support the tenge, but partially ofset by importers' demand for FX and lower oil prices.

Fiscal discipline remains a challenge: In January-July 2024, the budget deficit widened to KZT2.8tn vs KZT3.5tn in 2024 budget plan, driven mainly by lower-than-planned tax revenues (78.3% of the period plan and 38.5% of the annual plan) and stable growth of spending (95.5% of the period plan and 55.4% of the annual plan). Alongside this, by 1 September, MinFin had used almost 94% of planned 2024 transfers from the NF, including KZT2tn guaranteed and KZT1.4tn targeted transfers. This dynamics together with weaker economic activity could widen the deficit to 3.0% of GDP, on our estimates, and lead to further (above the plan) transfers from the NF, while high costs of borrowing will likely limit new issuance. MinFin has not commented yet on deficit funding for the remainder of this year, but its 2025-2027 budget plan implies an increase of the targeted transfers next year by \~KZT1.7tn to KZT3.25tn vs KZT1.6tn in 2024 plan. The new budget plan implies narrowing the deficit from 2.7% of GDP in 2025 (KZT4.1tn) to 1.9% in 2027 (KZT3.7tn). However, the upward revision of spending and transfers from the NF will delay budget consolidation and put at risk fulfilment of the plan to increase NF savings to USD100bn from the current USD61.1bn.

Amid high inflation risks, the NBK paused its easing cycle with more hawkish rhetoric: The NBK's gradual easing cycle began in August 2023, and it has since delivered seven 25-50bp cuts from 16.75% to 14.25%. At its recent MPC meeting in August, the NBK left the base rate unchanged at 14.25%, signaling the likelihood of staying on hold until the end of the year. It also reiterated that moderately tight conditions are necessary due to inflationary pressure from the ongoing reform of utility prices (easing of regulations), elevated inflation expectations, weak FX and high pro-inflationary risks (associated mainly with fiscal spending and the planned increase of transfers from the National Fund next year). The Bank left unchanged its 2024 GDP and CPI estimates at 3.5-4.5% y/y and 7.5-9.5% y/y respectively, while its GDP estimate for 2025 was revised up by 0.2pp to 5-6% y/y, mainly driven by domestic demand, fiscal stimulus and increased oil production in 2025-26. We expect the NBK to continue its gradual easing cycle next year. We do not rule out another 25-50bp cut this year if inflation and expectations continue to ease, but this seems a less likely scenario. Next MPC meeting: 11 October 2024, 8:00am UKT.

## Kenya: Battling revenues

## Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Tax revenues have undershot budgeted estimates, and the withdrawal of the FY2024-25 Finance Bill will likely result in a re-examination of some macro targets in the Staf Level Agreement that was reached in July. We look forward to a successful IMF board meeting in September/October.

• Credit: Investor concerns about Kenya’s fiscus led to a bear-flattening of the curve in July/ August. While this has partly reversed, we expect further normalisation of the curve. The deterioration in the fiscal outlook is partially mitigated, in our view, by Kenya’s good relationship with the IMF and international institutions, a stronger FX, which has resulted in lower debt/GDP, and the fact that after the large eurobond maturity in June 2024, the external maturity profile is now much lighter. In stark contrast to some SSA peers, most notably Angola, Kenya’s external position also stands to benefit from lower oil prices. We reiterate our Overweight rating on Kenya credit and think that Kenya should revert to trading through ANGOL in the belly of the curve: switch from ANGOL 2028s into KENINT 2028s.

## Revenue challenges have arisen...

When the FY24/25 budget was initially tabled, we were quite comfortable that the revenue targets would be met. However, events took a diferent turn. First, the Kenya Revenue Authority indicated that it had missed its FY23/24 target, lowering the base for FY24/25 estimates. Second, two consecutive finance bills aimed at increasing revenues by KES214bn (1.2% of GDP) in 2023 and KES345bn (1.9% of GDP) in 2024 were withdrawn, significantly compromising the overall tax take , especially VAT, excise and income taxes. Thankfully, the newly appointed Minister of Finance has indicated<sup>43</sup> that he would like to resubmit some of the revenue measures contained in the 2024 Finance Bill to parliament in the form of amendment bills that he hopes will pass constitutional muster once the correct procedures are followed to solicit public opinion. He also hopes that eforts to digitise, simplify, and subsequently reduce VAT and corporate taxes will help buoy economic activity and increase the tax take. Earlier attempts to raise revenue by simply raising taxes have not worked as expected.

The government also called upon the Supreme Court to review the Court of Appeal ruling that some of its main 2023 tax measures were unconstitutional and managed to obtain a conservatory order suspending the appeal court ruling, pending a two-day hearing which started on 10 September. In a possible worst case scenario, should the Supreme Court rule against the government, and such a ruling be delivered by end-September, we believe that the revenue loss in FY24/25 would be less than KES214bn, as the fiscus would have already benefited from the contested revenues for one quarter. Further, revenues are likely to be collected during the transition period over which government systems and platforms are amended to reflect the new tax rates. We also note that the appeal court ruling excluded revenues from the housing levy, which came in at KES63.2bn in FY23/24.

## ... while spending pressures abound

The authorities acted swiftly after the president withdrew the 2024 Finance Bill to table a revised budget cutting spending by some 1.1% of GDP. Yet, tit is far easier to promise budget cuts than to deliver them. Of the 24 recurrent expenditure handles that are up for rationalisation, as many as 14 are to be obliterated, while four are to be halved and the remaining six to be reduced by 20%. It may take a while to wind down some of these operations, and there will no doubt be other costs involved. Also, the anticipated cuts/delays to capex outlays may turn out to be expensive if one considers potential associated penalties.

Thankfully, the government has already initiated talks to securitise a portion of its outstanding arrears estimated at some KES516bn or 2.8% of GDP as at June 2024. Even so, the remainder of the book presents an additional expenditure burden. Given all the above, we keep our FY24/25 fiscal deficit forecast at 5% of GDP, which is roughly a percentage point of GDP smaller than the FY23/24 outcome of 5.9% of GDP, but wider than the revised FY24/25 budget estimate of 4.3%.

## What does this mean for the IMF programme?

Such slippage would no doubt create problems with the ongoing IMF programme, and may well call for a renegotiation, especially if one considers that the authorities did not meet the end-June 2024 IMF quantitative performance criteria on primary budget balance and tax revenue, but did manage to reach staf level agreement with Fund staf on the back of strong commitments to front-load the significant decisive consolidation required to return the fiscus to course. Furthermore, Kenya’s current programme ends in April 2025, and may have to be further extended or renewed. With total lending already close to the 600% of the quota ceiling, and upcoming repayments totalling just about USD1bn over the next three years, Kenya may need to apply for exceptional access. Thankfully, the IMF has been unrelenting in its eforts to support the authorities to overcome these dificult economic challenges, and has committed to working together with Kenya to chart a course towards sustainable and inclusive growth.

## Inflation and policy rates

After peaking at 9.6% y/y in October 2022, headline CPI subsequently decelerated to a low of 4.3% y/y in July before ticking up to 4.4% y/y in August. Although eastern parts of the country, such as the Garissa and Tana River areas, are still facing depleted stocks and food shortages following earlier flooding, and La Nina is likely to hit by October, improved weather conditions in some parts of the country have combined with positive base efects to help place a lid on food inflation, where we look for further deceleration in the months ahead as the harvest season kicks in. On the whole, we forecast CPI to decelerate moderately, to close the year around 4% y/y and dip below 4% y/y in early 2025, before re-accelerating over the second half of the year on negative base efects to breach 5% y/y by the end of 2025. With this trajectory, we still expect the CBK to implement two 50bp rate cuts this year, followed by a further 300bp worth of cuts in 2025.

## Nigeria: Fiscally frugal

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

Some renewed reform momentum is a possibility as the authorities take advantage of on-going fuel shortages, the onset of domestic fuel production and the drop in oil prices to implement what could well be the final stage of the removal of fuel subsidy. Further action is needed, we believe, to cushion the impact on households.

• Credit: Although the rapid reform momentum at the beginning of the Tinubu administration appears to have been slowed by socioeconomic and political challenges, we believe that the country’s longer-term trajectory remains on a positive path and we maintain an Overweight rating on Nigeria credit. Issuance plans have been focused on local markets (including FXdenominated issuance), which should support the external Eurobond curve at the margin, especially if such issuance were to attract local FX holdings back into the system. We think Eurobond issuance still remains a possibility over the next few months, should a window of opportunity open up. With such issuance likely focused on the belly of the curve given current yield levels, we think the front-end would benefit and recommend buying NGERIA \$27s/\$28s for attractive carry. We remain constructive on IHS (OW) as we see the outcome of the contract renegotiations with MTN (announced in August) as credit positive. IHS retained USD/ diesel and CPI linkages, providing ongoing protection to currency volatility, while the master lease agreement was also extended to 2032 (the bulk of the contracts mature prior to 2029), allowing greater revenue visibility. Planned asset sales of c.\$0.5-1.0bn by May 2025 could help reduce the debt load.

## Steady progress this year, with reform benefits likely in the months ahead

Contrary to our expectations of a deceleration to 2.5% y/y in Q2 from 2.8% y/y in Q1 24 as monetary conditions tightened and strike action unfolded, GDP growth, in fact, accelerated to 3% y/y in Q2, led in the main by continued strong performance in services (finance, telecommunications, trade), a rebound in domestic crude petroleum, and a snap back in agricultural crop production. Looking ahead, we note that the combination of high input costs as a result of the ongoing FX reforms have combined with FX shortages to crimp manufacturing activity. We expect this to further combine with tighter monetary conditions and the current dip in business confidence and weak demand conditionsas reflected in the July/August PMI, to cap GDP growth at 2.5-3.0% in H2 24. It is worth noting that the onset of domestic fuel production by the Dangote Refinery is likely to free up transportation and other logistical bottlenecks that have hamstrung the economy for decades and could become a game-changer for macro lubrication, especially when combined with all the reforms that the Tinubu administration is undertaking - though the initial social costs will be painful. Marking our forecasts to market, therefore, we revise our full-year 2024 growth estimate up from 2.5% to 2.8% and lift our 2025 forecast from 3.0% to 3.5%. Our baseline assumes the government continues to work hard to contain the negative socioeconomic and political efects of the reforms, especially in the relatively more impoverished northern parts of the country.

## Commendable willingness to implement fiscal tightening

Incoming data on Q1 24 fiscal metrics published by the CBN show that, at just NGN1.4tn, federal government revenue fell far short of the NGN4.9tn target, thanks to a significant underperformance in oil revenue and, to a lesser extent, independent revenue of state-owned institutions. Given this disappointing revenue performance, the authorities prudently pursued the unpopular path of fiscal frugality and slashed government outlays to size. While the resultant capex implementation rate of less than 10% does not bode well for economic growth, the fact that all other expenditure handles were cut by some 20-35% in order to keep the fiscal deficit in line with target is commendable. It is also worth noting that with the onset of domestic fuel production, Nigeria will no longer have to pay the expensive fuel subsidy that has eroded oil revenue. As a result, we are revising our revenue estimates for 2025 upward. On the whole, we maintain our view that despite a more than doubling of the minimum wage from NGN30k/ month to NGN70k/month, the headline federal government deficit is unlikely to come in above 4% of GDP in 2024. We are also revising our 2025 estimate down from 3.8% to 3.5%.

## Easing to start in Q4 24 despite some slowing of the deflationary trend

Inflation peaked at 34.2% y/y in June, and is now on the way down, thanks in large measure to positive base efects from the sharp rises in domestic fuel prices and the weaker exchange rate resulting from the fuel subsidy and FX reforms introduced a year ago. Yet, while the import duty waiver on staples is firmly in place, and some cereal (millet) and tuber harvests have begun, the key maize harvest has been afected by pest infections, pointing towards some stickiness in food CPI. Also, the unexpected 40-50% increase in domestic fuel prices just before local fuel production commenced in early September points to some upside potential in domestic price formation.

Even so, we expect the combination of tighter monetary policy, lower real disposable income and a more-stable FX to help place a lid on inflation over the coming year, with the headline aggregate closing the year around 27% y/y, and to average some 18.3% in 2025 (previously 18.2%). With this trajectory, we continue to expect the CBN to commence easing in November (175bp), followed by a further 100-150bp of easing at each meeting in 2025, taking rates to 18.50% by the end of 2025. The unexpected rise in fuel pries, however, raises risk that easing may be delayed until Q1 25.

## Oman: Banking on reforms

## Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

Oman's embraced reform trajectory has yielded tangible improvements to sentiment thanks to consistent deleveraging of the sovereign's balance sheet pointing to the importance of governance. Still, reforms and fiscal prudence have not been tested by protracted periods of lower oil prices. We hence expect any sovereign rating upgrade to be delayed until further progress on deleveraging is achieved.

• Credit: Oman's fundamental trajectory has been positive. And although we expect new Eurobond supply before year-end, net supply from Oman has been limited, with local demand providing a strong anchor for technicals, too. However, we consider valuations for a BB+ credit as relatively tight and are mindful of the risks that lower oil prices could bring to reforms and to supply-demand dynamics. We hence rate Oman sovereign credit MW. On the curve, we see value in the 5y sector of the curve (buy Oman 2029s), given the flatness of the belly part of the curve and the significant premium over CDS.

## Deleveraging, the name of the game

In Sultan Haitham's first speech when he accessed the throne, he vowed to reform the state and deleverage its balance sheet. Nearly five years later, Oman has been among the standout performers across the GCC and EM, quickly regaining investors' confidence. Deleveraging has been centre to Sultan Haitham's reforms, with excess oil savings primary channeled to reduced external debt while the combination of domestic reforms and higher oil prices has boosted primary surpluses. Public debt has nearly halved since the pandemic to 35% of GDP thanks to a OMR6bn fall in debt and higher nominal GDP. The quality of this adjustment difers from its regional peers (eg, Bahrain) and Oman's past experience, thanks to a sustained improvement to the non-oil revenues-to-expenditure ratio which has doubled in less than a decade. Fiscal consolidation eforts, including capex rationalisation, higher tax revenues and the expansion of gas output and oil products have pushed the ratio to a 15-year high, reflecting the true pace of fiscal reforms. Oman's experience highlights the importance of governance, as we have previously noted (see Oman Quarterly Outlook: An inflection point, 23 September 2020).
