## 2024 fiscal performance, mixed so far

Lower oil prices and a 5% drop in crude oil output weighed on fiscal performance over the first seven months of the year. Still, oil revenues increased by 8.9% y/y in January-July thanks to the Duqm refinery, which was completed at end-2022. The refinery relies on 65% of feedstock from Kuwait while sourcing the remainder domestically, and is set to operate at 10% above capacity which should help ofset some of the impact from lower oil output. Even so, the gas sector has been a drag, with revenues down 14.6% y/y over this period, with non-oil revenues also down, by 2.7% y/y, with to a 10% y/y fall in both corporate and other taxes. In addition, expenditure increased by 6.4%y/y, led by a strong 24% y/y jump in capex (capex remains small though at 1.2% of GDP) and with broadly stable military expenditure over recent years. Despite faster expenditure, the budget remained in surplus, narrowing by nearly half to OMR427mn and, should the government extend the fiscal stance, will likely outperform the 2024 budget, which targeted a OMR640mn deficit.

## Investment grade still a long way of

Oman's improving macro economic fundamentals have boosted investor confidence and placed the country on a clear upward trajectory towards regaining an investment grade rating, in our view. We previously noted that sustained reforms and resilient revenues would be crucial for an upgrade this year, with the three main rating agencies all one notch below IG (see The 2024 rating calendar, 3 January). Still, Oman's public debt remains elevated albeit rapidly falling to more sustainable levels since the pandemic, and fiscal performance has not been tested yet by a protracted period of lower oil prices. The government is planning some ambitious fiscal reforms but near-term gains may prove too small. For example, Oman aims to become the first GCC country to implement income taxes but the elevated thresholds (\$100k for expats and \$1mn for locals) will keep the tax base very narrow. With oil prices recently extending downward pressure, we believe rating agencies will likely remain cautious and delay any potential upgrade to IG to next year, assuming energy prices remain firm and the commitment to fiscal reforms and deleveraging continue unabated. We hence see limited likelihood of an upgrade in the coming months.

## Poland: Growth champion

## Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

Poland delivered stronger growth in H1 24 than its peers. Although deterioration in the budget deficit was largely due to cosmetic/one-of factors, the positive fiscal impulse will continue in 2025. Inflation is likely to return to the target band starting in Q3 25 if no more energy hikes are delivered. We see room for 100bp of cuts to 4.75% in 2025, starting in June.

• FX: We expect the PLN to remain stable in the near term and to outperform its CEE peers. We believe the NBP will continue to err on the cautious side and push against premature easing. Hence, the PLN should benefit from a prolonged carry advantage over the EUR. However, wobbles in global risk sentiment could assert pressures on the PLN, ofsetting its carry advantage. Moreover, Polish growth remains robust, which should further support the PLN, especially against its CEE peers.

• Rates: We expect NBP to start its easing cycle in Q2 25, against market expectations that price in 35bp worth of cuts by the end of Q1 25. The market's dovish expectations come from the fact that the NBP has been responsive to global rates trends historically. However, we believe that the NBP will refrain from cutting in an environment of rising inflation in Poland, in line with the latest guidance from the NBP Governor. As a result, we continue to see value in paying FRA 5x8. In our view, the back-end of the Polish curve is more driven by global factors, hence it should remain supported by weaker global growth backdrop. However, we expect increasing funding needs amid expansionary fiscal policy to continue to weigh on PolGBs relative to tenure matched IRS. We thus expect Polish asset swaps to remain at current wide levels.

## Outpacing peers in growth

Poland has enjoyed better economic growth than its regional peers so far in 2024, with 2.3% GDP growth in H1 24 vs 0.6% on average for its CE4 peers. Private consumption, which grew 2.0% y/y in Q1 24 and 3.2% in Q2 24, was the biggest contributor to GDP growth, primarily supported by strong wage growth at 10.6% y/y in nominal terms and 6.1% y/y in real terms in H1 24. Also, investment growth surprised to the upside, at 1.0% y/y in Q2 24, the strongest quarterly performance since Q3 22. Marking to market the stronger than expected Q2 24 GDP growth performance, we raise our growth forecast by 0.2pp to 3.1% y/y for 2024 and keep our 2025 forecast unchanged at 3.9% y/y.

## Public consumption to remain a positive contributor to growth

One important driver of relatively strong growth is public consumption, which contributed 0.3pp to the 1.5% q/q GDP growth in Q2 24. According to the government's fiscal targets, there will be a cash-based deficit of PLN289bn in 2025 (7.3% of GDP), up 57% from the expected deficit of PLN184bn for 2024 (5.1% of GDP). The big deterioration is due to two factors included in the 2025 budget: 1) PLN63bn to cover the cost of buying back BGK and PFR bonds; and 2) PLN25bn for restructuring municipalities. Adjusting for these, the deterioration would be limited to 9% – still a real deterioration of c.5% y/y on top of the already elevated deficit of 2024, but much better than the headline deficit figure suggests. Despite a deterioration in central government budget, the government is aiming to deliver a slight improvement of 0.1pp in the general budget deficit to 5.6% of GDP.

## Higher financing needs are unlikely to be a big issue

Financing needs will increase 70% y/y in 2025. The authorities aim to keep net domestic borrowing roughly flat from 2024 (8% y/y nominal growth), using the external side to fill the gap. The plan is to raise €10bn (net) in foreign issuance via eurobonds in 2025 vs €6.7bn in 2024 (or,

in gross terms, €15.5bn vs €13.9bn). Poland also aims to get almost €6bn in RRF loans in 2025. Given positive investor interest to Polish assets, we think financing of higher budget deficit is manageable unless there is a major change in global risk appetite.

## A cyclical acceleration in CPI in H2 24

After peaking at 18.4% y/y in February 2023, CPI dropped steadily to 2.8% in February 2024 then remained within the target band (2.5% +/- 1%) until July when some energy subsidies were reversed, pushing CPI above 4% y/y. Domestic consumption is recovering while wage growth is still strong, which does not bode well for the inflation outlook. On a more positive note, the hawkish stance of the NBP and strong inflows of EU funds in addition to the positive C/A balance are helping to keep the PLN strong, an important catalyst for disinflation. As a result, we expect CPI to end 2024 at 4.4% y/y then remain around 4-5% until July 2025 when high baseefect kicks in. Assuming no major change in energy subsidies in 2025, we expect CPI to return to the upper band of the inflation target at 3.5% y/y in H2 25.

## Rate cuts to start in Q2 25

The NBP was the most dovish central bank in the region in the previous two years in terms of expost real policy rates. This year, however, has been diferent. Citing potential risks from fiscal policies on inflation in addition to strong wage growth, the NBP made a radical shift in its policy stance at the January meeting and signalled that it would remain on hold over the rest of the year, according to its baseline scenario. At some stage, communication even turned briefly more hawkish with the governor arguing for no rate cuts before 2026. That said, in a recent reversal to communications, the NBP noted that rate cut talks were likely to start after Q1 25. Against this backdrop, we expect the NBP to start easing in Q2 25 and deliver a total of 100bp cuts to 4.75% next year.

## Romania: Waiting for fiscal reforms after the elections

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

Weaker growth in Q2 24 was mostly due to cyclical, not structural, factors with the main growth drivers mostly unchanged for 2024. The current fiscal trajectory shows a similar performance to 2020 when the fiscal deficit to GDP was almost in double digits. We expect a fiscal consolidation programme to start early next year following two critical elections in Q4 24.

• Credit: Romania has notably underperformed peers, including Hungary, over the past few weeks as concerns about Romania's fiscal position heading into the Q4 elections have come into focus. The wider fiscal deficit has also brought more supply, and we expect another c.USD3bn over the rest of 2024. Against this backdrop, it seems unlikely that Romania spreads will tighten meaningfully in the near term. However, we remain optimistic for the medium term and, given attractive valuations in the global EM IG sovereign context, we maintain an Overweight rating.

• FX: Over the next quarter, we continue to expect the NBR to manage the EURRON around current levels. The NBR treats the exchange rate as a main anti-inflationary anchor; hence, recent faster-than-expected disinflation has opened scope for some RON devaluation. We expect the NBR to do a one-of adjustment to its current FX policies and allow for a small RON depreciation in Q1 2025, as it views RON as overvalued at the current level. Alternatively, the NBR could reduce their FX operations, allowing for a wider variation in the EURRON exchange rate.

## Growth is losing steam

GDP growth slowed to 0.7% y/y in Q2 24, down from 2.1% y/y in the previous quarter. Likewise, sequential growth performance slowed to 0.1% q/q in Q2 24, the second lowest quarterly print since Covid-19. Despite this slowdown, Romania remains the best performer in CE4 since the onset of Covid-19, with its GDP now 14.2% above the pre-pandemic level. For three main reasons, we view the weaker Q2 24 performance as mostly due to cyclical, not structural, factors with the main growth drivers mostly unchanged for 2024.

1. Real wage growth remains strong at 7.2% y/y as of June despite some moderation in inflation to around 5% y/y in Q2 24. Likewise, the unemployment rate at 5.4% remains close to historical lows despite some moderation in economic activity. Consequently, domestic consumption remains strong, and retail sales expanded 8% in H1 24.

2. EU funding continues at full speed. The total allocation for next seven-year budget is €55bn, up from €43bn for the previous seven-year budget. There is also a €29bn allocation from the RRF facility including loans. While there is some cyclical slowdown in cohesion fund flows in 2024 because it is the first year of the new seven-year period, this is the case for all countries and will normalise from next year.

3. Expansionary fiscal policy continues at full speed. The budget deficit in Jan-Jul to 4Q total GDP reached 4.2% as of July and the current pace of expansion in the budget balance is very similar to 2020, when the budget deficit reached 9.6% of GDP. With the budget deficit continuing to quickly deteriorate ahead of the elections in Q4 24, the Ministry of Finance announced two new measures to rein in public finances: first, a fiscal pardon for total receivables of RON72bn – MinFin expects to collect cRON9bn (c0.5% of GDP); and second, some spending cuts. The EC's YE24 fiscal deficit forecast of 6.9% is likely to be exceeded based on the current trend and MinFin is currently negotiating with the EC to lower the budget deficit below 3% in the next seven years. These negotiations should be finalised in Q4 24 and Romania will embark a new fiscal consolidation to bring down the deficit to GDP by around 0.7pp on an annual basis. Possibly measures that could be implemented in the upcoming period include a VAT hike, a wage freeze, an income tax hike, a hiring freeze and investment spending cuts. This will require strong political capital and sustained coordination – not an easy task.

## Two critical elections in Q4 24

The presidential and general elections will be held on 24 November and 1 December, respectively. The leaders of the current PNL-PSD coalition are running for the presidential elections, and are likely to get the most votes according to some surveys<sup>44</sup> . Compared to 32% support for Marcel Ciolacu (current Prime Minister) from PSD, Nicolae Ciuca from PNL is polling at 19% with independent candidate Mircea Geoana in third place at 15%. The second round vote will take place two weeks later on 8 December between the top two candidates if no candidate gets above 50% in the first round. Although the presidential position is mostly ceremonial, the president's decisions are important in a scenario of new government formation, which is this not uncommon in Romanian politics. In terms of the general election, the poll of polls calculated by Politico as of 27 June 2024 shows the PSD&PNL coalition at around 49% of votes, ie, maintaining enough seats to continue its coalition. For the medium term and assuming the coalition remains in government, there could be concerns about the cooperation between the two coalition partners in terms of fiscal consolidation eforts.

## Not much room for more cuts

The NBR delivered two 25bp in the summer to 6.50%. The ex-post real policy rate at 1.9% is roughly in line with Poland's and lower than Hungary's 2.7% and and Czechia's 2.6%. Given Romania's lower credit rating, looser fiscal policy and limited improvement in the inflation outlook, we think there is not much space left for the NBR to deliver more cuts in the rest of this year. That said, risks to our call are clearly to the downside (ie, for more cuts) depending on the growth outlook and global risk appetite following expected cuts from the Fed and the ECB later this year.

## Russia: Curbing excess demand

Zalina Alborova, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK

• FX: Recent RUB weakness came in on the back of a strengthening CNY, lower oil prices and reduced FX sales by the CBR within the fiscal rule. On the flip side, historically high interest rates and problems with import payments (international transactions) limited scope for imports, hence supporting the RUB. Looking ahead, we continue to expect the RUB to be mainly driven by the Russian trade balance, namely oil revenues, on the export side, and local demand on the import side. Trade sanctions and associated frictions may add to RUB gyrations in the near term. In the medium term, we maintain our expectations for RUB depreciation, as the government has already reduced the levels of the mandatory sales of FX revenues from exports, which should reduce RUB demand.

Despite first signs of moderation, economic activity is stronger than expected: In Q2 24, the Russian economy slowed to 4.0% y/y from 5.4% y/y in Q1 24<sup>45</sup> (or 4.5-4.8% y/y if adjusted for the leap year<sup>46</sup> ). It grew 4.6% y/y in H1 24. Fiscal spending, high corporate profits, lending and investment (8.3% y/y in Q2 24 vs 14.5% y/y in Q1 24) kept domestic demand strong, while tight monetary conditions (weaker credit growth at the beginning of Q3 24), labour shortages and weaker external demand led to gradual economic moderation. By sector, growth was mainly driven by manufacturing and retail trade, while July data also benefited from some recovery in agriculture and transportation<sup>47</sup> , ofsetting negative dynamics in mining and quarrying and a slowdown in construction. PMIs continue to show expansion in the manufacturing sector although at a slower pace (52.1 in August vs 53.6 in July and 54.9 in June), while the survey indicates weaker confidence and increased backlogs of work amid transportation issues and delays in input delivery times. Given better-than-expected data for Q2 24, July and August, we raise our GDP forecast by 0.3pp for 2024 to 3.6% y/y and cut it by 0.3pp to 1.8% y/y for 2025 given base efects and tighter monetary conditions<sup>48</sup> . Current dynamics of demand pose upside risks to our 2024 forecast, while risks of tighter monetary conditions and lower oil prices next year may slow down economic activity more than we expect.

Stable execution of the budget: Dynamics of the federal budget have been relatively positive, supported by strong economic growth, tax changes, high commodity prices and stable external demand. By the end of August, the budget deficit narrowed to 0.2% of GDP (RUB331.4bn) YTD vs deficit of 1.1% of GDP (RUB2.1tn) in 2024 budget plan. In January-August 2024, budget revenue grew 27.3% y/y, while oil & gas revenues provided 32.8% of total vs 28.5% in 7M 23 and 30.3% in 2023. We expect a weaker RUB (in Q3 and Q4 24) to partially ofset the impact of lower oil prices, while strong economic dynamics should continue to support budget replenishment via non-oil revenues. Funding needs will be covered mainly by domestic borrowing and transfers from the NWF, while high interest rates, which have significantly increased the cost of borrowing, and geopolitical uncertainty continue to pose downside risks for the budget. We forecast a deficit of 1.4% of GDP this year and 0.9% in 2025.

Price growth impeded after July peak but risks remain: Weekly estimates of Rosstat for August indicate that after a 0.5pp jump of inflation to 9.1% y/y in July (indexation of utility tarifs, gasoline and some food components), annual CPI remained around 9% y/y in August (vs 4% target). Driven by unanchored inflation expectations, strong demand (fiscal support, high wage growth, credit growth) and trade restrictions (transportations costs and dificulties with payment transactions), inflation has been developing above our June EMQ forecast. While highfrequency data indicate some easing of domestic demand and credit growth, inflation expectations remain above multi-year averages (July expectations picked in up by 0.5pp to 12.9% y/y for households and by 0.2pp to 21.7% for enterprises). Thus, we revise up our CPI path and expect average annual inflation at 8.0% y/y in 2024 and 6.1% y/y in 2025, 0.7pp and 0.8pp higher than we forecast in June.

Stronger hawkish guidance: After a pause since December 2023, the CBR delivered a 200bp hike to 18% in July, noting that it "will consider the necessity of further key rate increase at its upcoming meetings". At the press conference after the meeting, Governor Nabiullina explained that tightening is needed to prevent stagflation, while the overheating in H1 24 was the most significant the economy had seen since 2008. She added that the updated average interest rate forecast (16.9-17.4% vs 15.0-16.0% in April) does not imply a cut this year. The average interest rate forecast for the next year was increased by 4pp to 14.0-16.0%, and the estimate of the neutral rate by 1.5pp to 7.5-8.5%. The decision for further tightening was driven by three main factors: 1) ongoing price growth (see above), with the CPI dynamics developed "significantly above" the CBR's April projections; 2) the pro-inflationary balance of risks, driven mainly by strong consumer demand, supported by continued growth in lending, subsidised lending (cancelled on 1 July), wage growth (shortage of labour force), positive consumer sentiment and elevated inflation expectations; and 2) economic activity developing above the CBR's expectations, while "upward deviation for a balanced path is not decreasing".

Tighter for longer: Since then, the CBR has further strengthened macroprudential measures and announced a countercyclical bufer at 0.25% of risk-weighted assets from 1 July 2025. At the end of August, Deputy Governor Zabotkin noted that the balance of arguments for tightening vs being on hold had not changed substantially since the July meeting and that the CBR would consider the necessity of further hikes at upcoming meetings (TASS, 29 August 2025). He also noted reduced sensitivity of the economy to changes in the key rate, which may require more sizeable changes of the rate. We think that the next MPC meeting (13 September) will be a close call between staying on hold and a 100-200bp hike, as inflation dynamics pose risks to the CBR's end-year projection of 6.5-7.0% y/y. However, the arguments for a pause are likely to prevail, in our view, as little time has passed since the previous MPC (full transmission takes three to six quarters) and the CBR will likely prefer to wait for October, when it will also review its medium-term forecasts. We expect rates to remain on hold through end-Q1 25, with risks of further tightening to 19-20% in October if inflationary pressure does not ease. In such a scenario, we think easing may not happen earlier than H2 25.

## Saudi Arabia: Reassessing priorities

Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK | Luc Roberts, Barclays, UK

The kingdom's commitment to the OPEC+ deal has considerably weakened its market share, increasing its dilemma over the balancing act between the medium-term sustainability of its oil policy and near-term funding needs.

• Credit: GCC credit continues to benefit from broad demand, including Asian/Chinese investors (see The China bid goes global, 1 August 2024). However, we think that among the GCC IG sovereigns, supply-demand dynamics are less favourable for KSA than for its peers, given the heavy and recurrent supply from the KSA credit complex and potentially more limited intra-regional demand from Kuwait if/when the debt law gets passed. We maintain an Underweight rating on KSA credit and think a switch from KSA into UAE in the belly of the curve, for a limited spread give-up, makes sense (sell KSA 5.5% 32s, buy UAE 4.917% 33s). We believe an Underweight rating remains appropriate for the GCC Corporates sector where we see little value, given tight spreads and an active primary market. We perceive valuations of Aramco bonds (MW) as balanced, and recently downgraded DP World to Underweight given a divergence between valuations and fundamentals (see DP World: Downgrade to UW on weaker metrics, tight valuations, 3 September 2024).

## OPEC+ deal: From boon to a drag

The kingdom's oil policy has considerably evolved over the past decade, gradually shifting to defend an implicit floor for oil prices. Saudi Arabia and Russia have closely cooperated since 2016 (excluding the brief oil price war in 2020) in order to broadly share the burden of a sustainable rebalancing in global oil markets. A growing focus on achieving an ambitious economic expansion with medium-term capex plans near \$2 trillion has reinforced a reliance on oil prices. The kingdom's unilateral crude output cuts to sustain elevated oil prices has considerably pressured its market share, falling to its lowest level since the 2008 Global Financial Crisis. Such a strategy may prove challenging to sustain over time, as its experience in the mid-1980s and 2011-14 suggests.

FIGURE 29. Oil market share under pressure, again
![](images/c1834e2322b20ce77ae67d6c2e15402735e871ba7093c3ab126f6de52b7e65dc.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

FIGURE 30. The kingdom commands a large share of global spare capacity
![](images/3448c1f6d912aee594f808c463edcb62f01b1d5faeac0196b7907eed0e73e223.jpg)
Source: IEA, Jodi Data, Barclays Research

It is noteworthy that the latest Platts OPEC+ survey shows that Iraq, Russia, Kazakhstan and the UAE produced 720kb/d above their target in July while Saudi compliance has been broadly

stable, with output staying at a three-year low of 9mb/d, considerably shrinking hard currency revenue. Growing concerns about the health of the global economy and still-elevated capex needs should exacerbate the kingdom's oil policy dilemma. History suggests that sustained periods of lost market share have resulted in significant oil policy shifts and periods of excessive oil price volatility that ultimately weighed on capex plans.

## Growth feels the pinch of oil policy

The economic recovery continued in Q2 24 with a much smaller contraction of 0.2% y/y vs 1.5% y/y in Q1 24. Although details are yet to be published, private domestic demand and public investment have likely remained in the driver's seat while the drag from the oil sector should continue to ease through year-end, albeit remaining negative. The kingdom has in fact agreed to delay OPEC+ output increases due to further weakness in global demand, pushing favourable base efects in the oil sector to 2025. In the nonoil sector, point of sales transactions and real narrow money supply have continued to improve, with the former reaching its highest level since 2016. In fact, the correlation between the oil and nonoil sector remains elevated. We also see smaller risks for recent announcements to rescale public investments and the pipeline of mega projects due to lags in the construction sector that would not afect this year's performance. We continue to see balanced risks to 2024 growth forecasts, which we keep unchanged at 1.8% since our Q2 Emerging Markets Quarterly. Strong base efects in the oil sector and resilience domestic demand should boost growth to 4.8% next year.

## Conservative fiscal policy faces oil policy dilemmas

Fiscal expenditure has extended its steady pace on the back of growing diversification eforts, increasing by 11.7% y/y in H1 24, outpacing the 8.6% y/y increase in revenue. In fact, despite oil production still at historically low levels, the rebound in oil prices in H1 has partly ofset the efect on revenue. However, the downward pressure on oil prices should reverse that trend in H2 24. Details of fiscal performance show that nonoil revenue performance was mixed, with a sharp fall of 18.7% y/y in taxes on income and profit more broadly ofset by a strong VAT performance, increasing by 8.4% y/y. Conversely, capex was the main driver of expenditure, adding SAR30bn with a 43% y/y increase in H1. We believe this trend should remain strong, albeit capex may prove to be the adjustment valve should oil prices face further downward pressure over the remainder of the year. The fiscal deficit has widened to SAR27.7bn (\$7.4bn) in H1 from SAR8.1bn. The government remains well placed to reach its 2024 budget target of SAR79bn (1.9% GDP), in our view.

## Senegal: Majority matters

## Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK

President Faye's 6 September letter to the National Assembly informing it that Prime Minister Sonko would present his General Policy Statement on 13 September should break the current stand-of between the opposition-led assembly and the ruling party. A dissolution of parliament on or shortly after 12 September 2024 and a call for fresh elections to allow the ruling party to gain a parliamentary majority should support the pursuit of its economic agenda and give impetus to the impending IMF review.

• Credit: Markets have reacted cautiously to recent political developments. However, even if a dissolution of parliament may bring some near-term political uncertainties, we think it could bring some longer-term positives for the credit if the gridlock is resolved and the IMF programme brought back on track. Hence, we see recent volatility as a buying opportunity. Senegal's 10s30s spread curve is among the steepest in the SSA context and we recommend buying SENEGL '48s. Kosmos Energy (KOS) and partners are working on the start of gas production from Tortue in late 2024, which should be positive for both Senegal and the company. A commission has been set up to examine natural resource contracts and “rebalance them to match Senegal’s interests", according to the prime minister, which brings some uncertainty; however, we believe the authorities will likely have to balance their nearterm goals with the need to attract future investment in the oil and gas sector. We stay constructive on KOS (OW), with the planned tender issue/refinancing set to support the curve.

## Extraordinary sitting of parliament a precursor to dissolution

After careful consideration over several months, and following a narrow rejection of its request to parliament on 30 August 2024, to dissolve two institutions that it regards as superfluous (the Economic, Social and Environmental Council and the High Council of Territorial Communities) with the view to rationalise government expenditures and save the public purse some CFA15bn annually, the government of Diomaye Faye, which clinched a 54.28% first round victory in the March 2024 elections but has found itself without a parliamentary majority in the national assembly, fired the heads of the two institutions and called for an extraordinary sitting of parliament on 5 September 2024. This is likely a precursor to a possible dissolution of parliament in the coming days.

Unfortunately, the ruling party does not have a majority in parliament, and the opposition's last-minute cancellation of the 29 June parliamentary session where the minister of finance was to present his 2024 supplementary budget, suggests that the opposition could scupper the ruling party's economic and political agenda if the ruling party does not increase its seats in parliament. One way to do so is to call for fresh parliamentary elections, with the hope that the party is able to garner a majority of the seats. Indeed, article 87 of the constitution gives powers to the president, in consultation with the prime minister and the head of the National Assembly, to dissolve the National Assembly, although this can be done no earlier than two years after the parliament is formed. Once dissolved, all parliamentarians lose their seats, and a new ballot must be announced concurrently, with elections to be held within 60-90 days after the said announcement. The dissolved Assembly may not meet, although the mandate of the legislators only expires on the date of the proclamation of the election of the members of the new National Assembly. As the first sitting of the current parliament was held on Monday, 12 September 2022, the Sonko government would be able to dissolve parliament and call for fresh elections on or shortly after 12 September 2024. The vote would then be held between 11 November and 11 December, or shortly thereafter.

## Parliamentary majority matters

The IMF concluded a mission to Senegal on 19 June and indicated that performance under the current EFF/ECF/RSF programs have been broadly satisfactory, that good progress was made in discussions with the authorities regarding adjustments to the 2024-25 budget, and that to accommodate rising energy subsidies and interest payments, the government would propose a supplementary budget to parliament, raising the 2024 fiscal deficit from 3.9% to 4.5% of GDP. It also hinted that an executive board meeting to approve the second review of the program would be scheduled for the second half of July. Unfortunately this did not happen, presumably because the supplementary budget has not been passed. A majority position in parliament by the ruling party could help avoid such unnecessary delays.

## Budget on course to print around 4.7% deficit despite political uncertainty

Elsewhere, incoming fiscal data show that budget revenue rose 3.1%y/y in the first half of 2024, to reach CFA1.9tn, driven mainly by double-digit growth in corporate income taxes that used to account for just over a fifth of fiscal resources but have now reached a quarter of total resources during the first half of this year, thanks to the unwind of tax concessions that were ofered during the cost of living crisis, improved administrative efort, and better use of digitization to help reinforce controls. Taxes on goods and services (mostly VAT), accounting for some twofifths of revenue have also rebounded despite the political uncertainty that has negatively afected economic momentum. Grants and nontax revenue, however, remained weak. Government spending, on the other hand, rose by just 2.1% y/y to CFA2,513bn, compared to CFA2,449bn in H1 23, mostly driven by a jump in external interest payments and personal emoluments. Like Nigeria and Ghana, in order to remain within budget, the authorities appear to have reined in capital expenditures, but also transfers and subsidies. On the whole the fiscal deficit printed at CFA617.7bn (2.9% of GDP), unchanged from CFA614.5bn (2.9% of GDP) in H1 23. At this pace, we believe that a full-year deficit of some 4.7% of GDP is likely.

Elsewhere, oil and gas production appears to be on track, with the 145kbpd capacity Sangomar deep water project producing first oil in June as scheduled. In August, the authorities set up a commission to audit its oil and gas contracts, and work to ensure that they were aligned with the national interest – a far cry from the pre-election rhetoric suggesting that an imminent renegotiation/cancellation of existing contracts was on the cards. Earlier concerns about Senegal introducing its own currency have also subsided, as we expected (see EEMEA Outlook, 28 March 2024).

## South Africa: Looking promising

Michael Kafe, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

The formation of a government of national unity that is focused on tackling the country's macro economic challenges is a positive surprise, as are the incoming inflation prints that are below our forecasts. We continue to expect the SARB to ease policy by 50bp this year but see rising risk of 75bp.

• Credit: An improved policy outlook and supportive supply-demand dynamics motivate us to upgrade South Africa sovereign credit to Market Weight from Underweight. While we do not rule out potential opportunistic issuance in the Eurobond market, we still expect net supply to be structurally limited/negative while demand for BB sovereigns in the current environment is likely to remain solid. Given the steepness of the spread curve, we see best value in the long-end and recommend buying SOAF 52s. We are constructive on the corporate complex in South Africa as we expect attractive carry/returns versus the sovereign, especially at the short end of the curve. Our preferred exposure is among private sector credits – Sasol (Overweight) – which we see as self-suficient in cash flow and liquidity. We also expect quasisovereigns to outperform the sovereign given improving fundamentals and state backing: we recommend a switch into SAFTRA 2028s from SOAF 2027s for the c.180bp pickup.

• FX: We expect the ZAR to remain broadly stable in the near term as the positive impact of the weaker USD should be ofset by the deterioration in the global sentiment amid US recession worries. However, a continuously bullish SARB, which could push against current market pricing of a large easing cycle, should ofer some additional support to the ZAR in the near term. In the long term, we expect the ZAR to remain stable and benefit from its carry advantage over other CEEMEA currencies.

• Rates: We expect the SARB to cut its policy rate by 50bp in total in 2024 and by another 50bp in 2025, against the market pricing of 146bp by the end of 2025. However, we refrain from fading this excessive market pricing ahead of the first SARB cut, as the market may further overshoot the depth of the easing cycle around the first SARB cut. We remain constructive of belly and longer dated rates, but we have viewed the recent rates rally as too aggressive and we look for a market correction to re-engage in SA rates receivers.

## Some signs of economic improvement

South Africa’s GDP swung from -0.2% q/q in Q1 24 to 0.4% q/q in Q2 24, thanks predominantly to a rebound in manufacturing sector activity as power supply conditions improved ahead of the May 2024 presidential elections, and as consumer and business sentiment recovered, helped by the formation of a government of national unity (GNU) that promises sound macro management and reform. While mining production remained in negative territory throughout the first half of the year, the contraction in the second quarter (-0.8% q/q) was less than that of the first quarter (-1.7% q/q) as the sector's logistical network improved somewhat, and as platinum production temporarily rebounded in April following earlier shaft closures. Wholesale and retail activity also rebounded meaningfully after the successful presidential elections, buoyed by a positive outlook for the economy and jobs, a stronger currency, and what appears to be a modest rebound in credit. Looking ahead, following a relatively strong post election-July reading, the manufacturing PMI fell back into contractionary territory in August – of note, important components such as business activity and new sales orders rose strongly in July in response to robust pent-up post-election demand. That said, only three of the nine subcomponents of the August PMI rose above the 50 expansion mark, portending potentially uninspiring manufacturing output ahead. Yet, we expect the turnaround in electricity

production to be sustained, and this together with on-going positive political sentiment and the onset of easier money in the quarters ahead, to first stabilise growth at some 0.4% q/q in H2 24 to yield a full-year outcome of 0.8% in 2024, before picking up to 1.5% in 2025. For 2026, we lift our GDP forecast from 1.8% to 2% as macro reforms put in place by the new administration start to yield positive dividends.

## Mild inflationary pressures on the horizon

With regards to inflation, following recent lower than expected incoming prints, we revise our headline CPI forecast down from 5% to 4.7% in 2024 and from 4.5% to 4.2% in 2025. Our 2026 forecast comes in at 4.7%. This trajectory is quite diferent from the SARB’s: our forecasts are lower over the next year, but higher in 2026. While the first four quarters of our trajectory are mostly driven by a lower starting point after the significant downside surprise in the July CPI (4.6%y/y) relative to our forecast (4.9%y/y), we see a gentle upswing after Q2 25 despite backwardation of the international oil price curve, for a number of reasons. First, we expect fuel taxes, which were frozen in the 2024 budget, to rise in 2025 and beyond. Second, we expect momentum in vehicle inflation to normalise after what is turning out to be an unusually low vehicle price profile in 2024. Finally, although incoming food inflation readings have been surprisingly benign and are yet to reflect the country’s significantly reduced cereal production, we believe that lower agricultural output will eventually lead to higher food inflation in 2025/6, especially as domestic demand picks up in the period ahead.

## Our base case is for 50bp of easing in 2024

With this outlook, we continue to look for 50bp of easing this year, in two clips of 25bp in September and November, but now see risk of a larger 50bp cut in September that would take total easing for the year to 75bp if the US Fed were to cut by 50bp at its next meeting, and/or were August CPI to print below 4.5%. For 2025, however, we maintain our view that easing will be limited to 50bp, as we believe that the SARB will prefer that the US Fed does the heavy lifting to restore the spread between South African and US policy rates (currently around 300bp) towards its historical average of 450-500bp. Our rising CPI trajectory from mid-2025 is another reason we expect restrained monetary easing in 2025. In the meantime, however, a faster nearterm deceleration in CPI leads us to believe that the final rate cut will now occur in May 2025 (previously July 2025)

## Tunisia: Post-election adjustment path

Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK

Tunisia's protracted election cycle has long delayed pressing structural reforms. Presidential elections will thus be crucial to unlock the country's talk with the IMF to revive its \$2bn programme.

## Presidential vote should mark a turning point in policy direction

Tunisia's protracted transition since 2011 has been marked by recurrent political volatility, social tensions and economic malaise. Yet, the upcoming 6 October presidential election should be the last step in the broad-based consolidation of power over the past three years, laying the ground for a new period of longer policy continuity that is not punctuated by electoral schedules. President Said is set to extend his mandate by another five years (Jeune Afrique, 4 September) as the number of candidates has dropped to three, from 26 in 2019 and 27 in 2014. The electoral commission (ISIE) has not retained 14 candidates on the back of stringent criteria to collect 10,000 signatures with at least 500 from each electoral district, which has particularly challenged independent candidates that lack the support of bigger parties. The limited number of candidates and relatively unknown approved contenders has thus increased president Said's chances to potentially win the vote in the first round on 6 October.

FIGURE 35. Voter turnout since 2011
![](images/bd4ea69acfefde57c4c95168d0681d1791c8d4adf5a15d642111e10c3a6c5dd4.jpg)
P: Presidential, L: Legislative, CA: Constituent Assembly, R: Referendum Source: electionguide.org, Barclays Research

FIGURE 36. Debt redemption profile becomes more favourable
External debt maturities\$bn
![](images/e838969dc695ba8f409583b116c8e5f6527a6589e8628909736f1681f20066a2.jpg)
Source: IMF, Barclays Research

The end of the protracted election period should rapidly shift policy priorities to macroeconomic stability and long-delayed pressing unpopular reforms. IMF talks could thus be revived as soon as Q4 24. though the finalisation of Article IV discussions will likely be a pivotal precondition to re-engage in loan discussions. Tunisia's Article IV consultations have been delayed by more than 18 months and therefore the Staf-Level Agreement reached in October 2021 will likely require a new cycle of consultations ahead of any renewed programme talks. We believe the end of a protracted election cycle will allow the authorities to introduce socially challenging economic reforms, particularly those required by the IMF.

## Fiscal prudence keeps the balance of risks contained

Tunisia's 2024 budget has a record large gap of unidentified external funding during a year of lingering uncertainties on how to close the funding gap amid the election cycle, weak revenue performance, delayed IMF talks, and elevated sovereign premium. Still, budget performance has remained prudent so far, posting a surplus of TND488.7mn in H1 24 compared to a small

TND26.3mn surplus in the same period last year. Despite an 8.2% y/y increase in expenditure, revenues rebounded to 13% y/y in H1 24 thanks to a 10.3% jump in tax revenues, which contrasts with lacklustre growth. Details show how the wage bill was well-contained compared to subsidies and transfers, which grew 8.1% y/y, albeit slower than last year, reaching 25.7% of the full-year target. However, capex fell 0.4 % y/y to only 35% of the 2024 target. We note that fiscal performance has often been conservative at the start of the year, so we think it too early to draw any conclusions regarding the full-year performance especially amid likely fiscal pressure ahead of the October polls.

Despite some fiscal prudence, the 36.1% y/y increase in interest costs has weighed on overall performance primarily led by domestic debt which increased by 52% y/y in H1 while the external debt burden has benefited from reduced external debt (share in total debt fell to 58% this year from 63% in 2015) and the sizable share of concessional funding. Tunisia's hampered international debt access has excessively pressured domestic borrowing, which increased 3.6x to TND11.2bn this year while external borrowing halved sharply in contrast with the 3.1x increase in external debt reimbursements. This was largely driven by the repayment of an €850mn in February and other multilateral loans (eg IMF, WB, AMF), thanks to TND7bn (\$2.3bn) in direct monetary financing from the central bank. We estimate the Ministry of Finance has already used TND4bn for budget support and is likely to use the TND3bn remaining in 2025. External budget borrowing remained very muted at TND14.1mn in H1 24 compared to TND1.7bn in the same period last year, which leaves an untapped TND4bn to H2 24 and which has been penciled into the 2024 budget (from WB, Afreximbank, Algeria and Saudi Arabia).

## External funding and growing feedback loops: the Achilles' heel

The country's excessive reliance on domestic funding to meet large external funding needs has increased feedback loops between the balance sheet of the government, banks and the central bank. In fact, the Ministry of Finance has increasingly tapped local banks' FX resources through syndicated loans (eg, TND693mn in 2024) while banks FX deposits with the central bank increased from TND5bn in October 2021 to TND9.5bn in August 2023. Nearly 90% of banks deposits with the BCT are in foreign currency, representing almost two thirds of their nonresident deposits. Banks have also increasingly relied on central bank refinancing windows since 2021 with the volume of refinancing reaching TND14bn in July 2024 compared to TND7.8bn in June 2021. Such a growing feedback loop likely remains vulnerable to elevated external funding needs, especially given the current account deficit.

# Turkiye: Soft landing, softer rebalancing

Ercan Erguzel, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Marek Raczko, Barclays, UK | Valentina Stoykova, Barclays, UK | Evgeniia Bystrova, Barclays, UK

As the authorities aim for a soft landing in growth, the correction in two major macro imbalances, inflation and the C/A deficit, are likely to be softer than in previous rebalancing cycles. To keep the pace of disinflation on a path closer to its CPI targets, the CBT is maintaining its REER TRY appreciation strategy. We push forward our first rate cut call to November, from January 2025, and expect the ex-post real policy rate to turn negative from September 2025.

• Credit: Turkiye sovereign eurobonds continue to enjoy support from strong local demand and the improved fundamental trajectory, which is also reflected in recent rating upgrades. However, we believe that the positives are fully priced, while likely further eurobond supply could lead to temporary pressure points. With sovereign spreads significantly below the index average, we maintain an Underweight rating on Turkiye sovereign credit and see better opportunities elsewhere in the Turkiye credit complex: Turkiye Financials (Overweight) and Corporates (Market Weight) currently ofer the highest carry within EEMEA Barclays-rated sectors, and we favour financials with a preference for high yielding private banks’ subs over seniors, underpinned by expectations for improving sector fundamentals in H2 24. We rate Turkiye Corporates MW as we expect further supply in the complex as well as pressure on credit fundamentals for corporates operating in cyclical industries. Within the complex, we prefer exposure to less cyclical Turkcell (OW) and Turk Telekom (OW).

• FX: We expect the TRY to continue to depreciate at a slower pace than implied by the forwards in the next quarter, meaning it remains an attractive carry destination. We expect the CBT to support further real efective exchange rate appreciation, but at a slower pace than in the past two quarters. The CBT needs to strike a balance between the disinflationary efect of REER appreciation and its negative impact on the current account balance. In our view, TRY is already overvalued, as a result of recent real efective exchange rate appreciation. So, in the long run, we expect this overvaluation gap to start closing once inflation stabilises at a lower level, or if the CA gap starts to deteriorate.

• Rates: Our economist expects the CBT to deliver the first cut of 2.5pp in November and continue its monetary policy easing at an even pace until achieving the terminal rate of 25%. The expected easing cycle should support the front-end OIS and CCY rates, but may limit carry inflows, increasing TRY volatility, which can lead to CCY rates underperformance relative to the OIS rates. In the medium term, we see belly rates benefiting from falling inflation driven by weaker economic activity, a disinflationary external backdrop and strong TRY (in real terms), hence we expect the TRY OIS curve to bull steepen.

## Growth is moderating

Q2 24 GDP growth at 2.5% y/y remained below our forecast of 3.3% and the Bloomberg consensus of 3.2%. On a sequential basis, growth fell to 0.1% q/q in Q2 24, its weakest since 1Q 23, held back by investment and net exports, despite relatively strong domestic consumption. 4Q trailing growth is at 4.9% y/y but we keep our growth forecast for 2024 unchanged at 3.0% y/y as all leading indicators point to a further slowdown from Q3 24. We also keep our growth forecast unchanged at 3.2 y/y % for 2025 as we expect lower interest rates to support economic activity while the authorities rein in public spending, with the two factors ofsetting each other.

## Two major macro imbalances to normalise

We expect easing growth to help two major macro imbalances, C/A deficit and inflation, to normalise, although slower than in previous rebalancing periods. We expect a materially lower C/A deficit at \$18bn or 1.6% of GDP, from \$45bn or 4.1% of GDP in 2023. Of the \$27bn y/y

improvement, we expect the core goods and gold balances to each have a \$11bn contribution, followed by energy at \$6bn. Although this would be a major improvement from last year, almost two thirds is driven by non-core items (gold and energy). The CBT’s strategy of maintaining a strong TRY is the most important driver of a expected softer pace of adjustment in core items.

Stronger TRY in REER terms helps disinflation, albeit not as fast as the CBT expected We forecast YE24 inflation at 44% y/y, above the CBT’s target of 38% y/y. More importantly, the current disinflation path signals limited improvement in the inflation outlook next year. We regard the post-2002 period as a good benchmark, ie, when Turkiye managed to bring inflation down to single digits from c.70%. Four important characteristics of this period were: 1) supertight monetary policy, with the ex-post real policy rate at almost 10% over 2002-10 2) supertight fiscal policy with consecutive years of primary budget surpluses over 2004-13 except during the Global Financial Crisis in 2009 and 2010; 3) a series of structural reforms helping to bring longer-term financial inflows, such as FDI; and 4) TRY being stable in nominal terms at 1.6-1.8 thanks mainly to the significant depreciation of 55% during the 2001 economic crisis. In the current situation, we think the authorities could meet the first two factors relatively easily by: a) holding the policy rate stable when inflation falls below 50% (not our base case); and b) delivering major fiscal tightening as signalled in the recently announced targets for 2025-27 (Turkiye Medium Term Programme: Four takeaways from new targets, 5 September).

## We revise our first rate cut expectation to earlier November from January previously

In the context of an economic slow down and with increasing domestic pressure on the CBT to ease financial conditions, we expect the CBT to start easing its policy rate in November, earlier than our previous forecast of January 2025. We expect the CBT to maintain 250bp cuts in every meeting until August 2025 taking the policy rate down to 25%. Based on our inflation forecast profile and forecast policy rate trajectory, the ex-post real policy rate will turn negative from September next year.

## Ukraine: Mapping a roadmap

Brahim Razgallah, Barclays, UK | Andreas Kolbe, Barclays, UK | Stella Cridge, Barclays, UK

Ukraine's medium-term outlook remains clouded with uncertainty despite recent progress on debt restructuring with bondholders. US election uncertainty adds significant risks to renewed bouts of escalation while a comprehensive peace deal is still highly challenging to achieve.

• Credit: We rate Ukraine sovereign credit MW given the still high degree of war-related uncertainties, which naturally leave medium-term risks two-sided, in our view. (Exit) yields on Ukraine’s post-restructuring bonds were initially above the 15% that we considered as adequate previously (see Ukraine’s Eurobond restructuring: A curve for the finishing line, 12 August 2024), but most bonds have performed solidly since, leaving less room for further upside, on aggregate. However, we think opportunities on the new curve still exist. We recommend buying the low cash-price B 2034s which we think ofer the best combination of downside protection in negative scenarios and the highest duration among the noncontingent bonds, which should create demand in a positive scenario (see Ukraine’s new bond curve: Pick your spots, 3 September 2024).

## US elections take centre stage

Ukraine's medium-term outlook will crucially depend on the upcoming US elections since a potential return of Trump to the White House may have considerable consequences for ongoing military financial support. Trump previously said he will quickly end the 2.5-year war, and according to the draft prepared by his advisers, further US weapon support would require Ukraine to enter peace talks with Russia (Reuters, 25 June). Still, Zelensky has already expressed his intent to share a peace plan with President Biden and the two presidential nominees (Reuters, 27 August), while rejecting any compromise with Putin at this stage. Note that Ukraine's first Summit on Peace in June highlighted divergent positions, particularly for some countries such as India and Saudi Arabia that called for some compromise.

FIGURE 40. Policy remains tight
![](images/fea07165875e33582849154dd38416a2113ea6259b8d7b6dcba7dff677cad384.jpg)
Source: CBU, Barclays Research

FIGURE 41. Improving high frequency indicators
![](images/fdde28ee832a5f4064dd0165b06b5f5e83c6e4b38551be525cef4c3e6f16ae73.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

Ukraine's territorial integrity remains the most challenging issue to resolve, especially that presidential elections have been delayed after Zelensky's term ended this year. Any compromise on Russia-controlled areas may prove detrimental to Zelensky's re-election prospects due to the domestic unpopularity of such a decision. It was no surprise that Zelensky has already floated the possibility of a referendum on territorial integrity in exchange for peace, although he has not provided any potential timeline for such a vote (Interfax, 1 August).

Prospects for an enduring peace agreement appear increasingly tied to unpopular measures on both sides, which will likely be heavily influenced by the winner of the US presidential race. Investors have been wary of risks to Ukraine's funding needs in case of a Trump win, although any new US funding would ultimately be controlled by US Congress. Meanwhile, US election uncertainty with two very diferent potential outcomes is likely to continue to shape the conflict's intensity. For example, after Russia's strong campaign targeting Ukraine's electricity infrastructure in Q2, Ukraine surprised by capturing Russian territory. Prospects of potential peace talks have thus fueled increased near-term volatility with both sides looking to increase their leverage in upcoming talks.

## Stabilising activity thanks to an improving energy situation

Economic activity started to stabilise in August after severe electricity disruptions in Q2 24 forced prolonged blackouts that ultimately weighed on industrial output and the construction sector. Yet, according to the grid operator, the energy situation has improved thanks to the end of maintenance and the heat wave. Ukraine lost about 9.2GW in power capacity due to airstrikes in March-June and while a gradual normalisation has been underway, the most recent escalation has seen the biggest attacks on Ukraine's power and water supply since the start of the conflict. Improvements to business sentiment thus remain fragile and sensitive to bouts of escalation. Both industry and construction have been improving and the harvest season is almost over with grain exports up 54% y/y since the start of the season in July, thanks to favourable base efects. Domestic demand has also been resilient, supported by a steady improvement in lending to households, growing at its fastest since the start of the war, despite relatively tight policy. Anticipated tax changes have also front-loaded car sales, which jumped to their highest level since end-2021. The ongoing war, however, continues to weigh heavily on the corporate sector, with mobilisation often identified as the major hurdle facing businesses. We believe the growth outlook faces downside risks over the rest of the year amid heightened tensions and risks to renewed supply bottlenecks. Hence, we expect the economy to slow to 3.4% this year from 5.3% last year. The authorities and the market consensus expect a stronger rebound in the next two years. But this would require a considerable easing of ongoing tensions. While recent restructuring reduces the debt overhang and could be generally positive for the medium-term trajectory, elevated uncertainty keeps risks skewed to the downside, in our view. Moreover, funding uncertainty from 2025 should pressure NBU reserves pressing for a renewed devaluation of the currency, especially following the easing of capital restrictions.

Latin America

# Paths of Disparity

Growth in Latin America is slowing, except for Brazil, which has shown positive surprises. Inflation is falling, despite some volatility. Central banks are expected to cut rates, except in Brazil, where we expect hikes. Fiscal prudence remains a challenge for many countries.

UPDATE This publication is an update to 'Latin America: Paths of Disparity', originally published on 11 Sep 2024 at 05:00 BST to correct the Mexico nominal GDP numbers.

Trade Summary | Argentina | Brazil | Caribbean | Central America | Chile | Colombia | Ecuador | Mexico | Panama | Peru | Uruguay | Venezuela

## Overview

Emerging shadows, Brazil in the light. First quarter growth was better than expected for almost all countries in the region, except Argentina, which is experiencing a full-fledged recession. Nevertheless, except for Brazil, for which we recently revised up our growth projection to 2.9%, from 2.7% (and 2.2% in the previous EMQ), we have started to see some disappointment lately, with a clear slowdown in Mexico and the Chilean economy visibly struggling (Figure 1). Mexico is not only facing a slowdown alongside slower growth in the US, but also the fading of tailwinds from AMLO's large electoral spending. A growth slowdown is particularly afecting Peru, making it more dificult for the government to keep up with its fiscal rule, up to the point of increasing government spending, expecting the multiplier to make the denominator increase more than the numerator in the debt-to-GDP ratio.

Growth in CAC has mostly been in line with expectations supported by US resilience, although capped by slow manufacturing recovery. The main changes in the region were in Panama, where we now expect growth of 3.0%, up from 2.2% in the previous EMQ, as construction and services sector have more than ofset the mining shock. On the other hand, we adjust El Salvador to the downside to 1.8%, from 2.8% previously, as the economy has been afected by stronger deceleration in remittances inflow while manufacturing and construction are contracting.

Fiscal struggles as we head into the FY25 budget season. Fiscal stories in the region are drawing attention as authorities prepare end-of-year plans and submit their 2025 fiscal budgets. We see mixed trends, as some countries are set to deliver on their targets, although with some cautiousness on sustainability over the next few years, but others will likely underperform as soon as this year. We see an increasing likelihood that Brazil will be able to keep to its fiscal framework this year, but challenges are on the rise as new revenue measures worth BRL168bn (1.4% of GDP) will be needed to comply with it in 2025. Although there is still uncertainty over Colombia's fiscal outlook, preliminary data support our view that authorities could outperform the target. Similarly, Mexico, Argentina and Ecuador are likely to meet their respective governments' latest targets for this fiscal year, although with difering momentum. In Mexico,

Core CPI kept trending downward, despite an abnormally volatile noncore. We have continued to observe core inflation trending downward across the large economies in the region. This has happened despite an abnormally volatile noncore component, afected by El Niño, reloaded by climate change. Some idiosyncratic factors have also afected CPI, particularly headline inflation. This is why we now expect higher inflation by year-end in all Latin American countries except for Ecuador and Argentina, with respect to our last EMQ (Figure 2).

We highlight three idiosyncratic issues afecting CPI or that might afect the CPI outlook of Chile, Colombia, and Mexico. In Chile, the most important idiosyncratic issue is the reduction of the pandemic-era subsidies on electricity tarifs, which has significantly changed the inflation outlook, complicating matters to the central bank. This is why we revised our year-end inflation forecast to 4.9%, from 4.0% in our last EMQ, and to 3.9% by YE25, from 3.5% previously. In Colombia, the recent blockades by truckers demanding a more-gradual reduction of diesel price subsidies could end up succeeding, but inflationary pressures could increase due to the supply disruptions that the road blockades have caused. While transitory, this might delay CPI movement toward the central bank's target. In Mexico, AMLO’s push for the approval of his reform agenda likely has been causing a significant depreciation of the currency, which could bend the country’s inflation trajectory upward (Mexico: Oktoberfest). Nevertheless, slower growth is also likely to compensate for these additional inflationary pressures. As a result, we keep our 4.3% forecasts for headline inflation by YE24, and 3.8% by YE25. In Brazil, the disinflationary trend in the IPCA and its core measures seems to have lost ground recently, with multiple gauges of services inflation suggesting accommodation at or above the uncomfortable level of 5% y/y, which would not be compatible with a headline inflation target of 3%.

## Charted their own course, but now "getting help" from the Fed’s upcoming rate cuts.

Central banks in Latin America tackled their own inflationary issues timely and decisively when they had to hike rates in 2021, not “waiting” for the US Fed to do so. In this context, these central banks were also able to ease when inflationary pressure receded. Not all of them acted in unison. The BCCh (Chile) started the cutting cycle in EM back in July 2023, followed by the BCB (Brazil) in August, the BCRP (Peru) in September, and then some months later, Banrep (Colombia) in December last year, and Banxico (Mexico) in March this year. However, certain idiosyncratic factors have also shaped these rate cycles. Even though these central banks have been riding their own wave, a Fed easing cycle is a balm that can soothe FX pressures across the globe. This should help central banks in Latam to be able to continue to ease (Banxico, Banrep), or ease more than they thought so a few months ago (BCRP, BCCh), or not to have to hike too much (BCB).

Expect rate hikes in Brazil, but rate cuts from the rest. In the particular case of Brazil, we expect the BCB to hike 25bp in September, from previously expecting it to hold rates for some time. We expect a relatively small tightening cycle of 150bp, lifting the Selic rate to 12%. This not only responds to a stronger economy, and reflects the fact that the BCB was one of the first central banks to start its easing cycle, but also addresses the additional de-anchoring of inflation expectations that ensued from May's monetary policy meeting, when Lula-appointed board members contrastingly voted for a faster pace of cuts, raising credibility concerns. Conversely, we expect rate cuts in the rest of the large economies in LatAm. We anticipate the BCRP and the BCCh to be the first to reach their "terminal rate" after cuts of 150bp and 125bp, respectively. For the "latecomers," we expect Banrep and Banxico to reduce their respective monetary policy rates by a total of 475bp and 325bp. Nevertheless, we no longer expect Banrep to increase the pace of cuts in September and October, given the CPI issues, but still see optionality to do it later on.

Still some interesting political developments to come this year. Even though Latin America has experienced a plethora of electoral processes this year, including general elections in Mexico, Venezuela, El Salvador, the Domincan Republic, and Panama, we are not done yet. Market participants will take a closer look toat Mexico's president-elect Claudia Sheinbaum's inauguration on October 1, as well as the local elections in Brazil (October 6), and the general election in Uruguay on October 27 (Figure 3). Moreover, the campaign for Ecuador's upcoming elections in February (2025) has begun with a high number of presidential candidates (17 so far), and more competition in the center-right that adds uncertainty and warrants some caution. President Noboa still has the upper hand, but in these conditions, a first-round victory is less likely and he may be forced into a potentially tight run-of.

Peru spreads have slightly underperformed EM IG peers, reflecting a gradually weakening fundamental picture, compounded by higher-than-expected supply in 2024. However, prevailing strong technicals have remained a mitigating force in cash markets. The fiscal pressures we envisaged have materialized, but terms of trade and growth remain tailwinds. The political situation remains unstable, and as 2025 approaches, we see politics playing a greater role in driving spreads and spread volatility. As a result, we are not constructive on the Peru sovereign and believe the recent widening pressure on Peru CDS is driven by the Petroperu situation. However, we think that the consequences for the Peru sovereign are negligible; therefore, we see the recent spread widening as excessive and recommend buying Chile 5y ČDS and selling Peru 5y CDS.</td></tr><tr><td>Suriname</td><td></td><td>Remain Overweight. Suriname is on the verge of a final investment decision for oil &amp; gas development that could propel it into being one of the largest per capita oil producers in the world by the end of the decade. We recommend buying SURINM 7.95% 2033s and SURINM 9% 2050s.</td></tr><tr><td>Uruguay</td><td></td><td>Remain Overweight. Uruguay valuations have compressed to IG peers due to stable credit metrics and extremely supportive curve technicals. While there are headline risks, we think that the strong starting level of fundamentals and technicals will lead to EM-dedicated buying upon any weakness.</td></tr></table>

Source: Barclays Research

## Argentina: Caught in limbo

Ivan Stambulsky Barclays, UK  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US

The Milei administration has made substantial progress on the fiscal consolidation and disinflation fronts since December. Meanwhile, the real exchange rate has appreciated considerably and is now overvalued, in our view. The balance of these factors has been politically favourable for Milei, who remains more popular than his predecessors at a similar time frame. We think disinflation can continue in the coming months and the zero deficit target will be hit this year, but risks to the durability of these achievements are material.

• Credit: Remain Underweight. The economic framework launched in December 2023 has generated fiscal savings YTD but has been unable to bring in enough hard-currency required to pay external debt and re-build reserves (see Argentina: Time for a checkup; remain Underweight, June 2024). To make it worse, president Milei has indicated that there will be no course correction and will not devalue the peso. While the peso devaluation (ending the "dollar blend") is not per se a solution, it would be a necessary step for a new, sensible, comprehensive economic plan. Moreover, a peso devaluation ould likely weigh negatively on the president's popularity levels, which investors have seen as a strength YTD. Accordingly, the market continues to price in a substantial probability of a default event. We think that the bond markets could react negatively to a decline in net reserves in the remainder of 2024 or a devaluation-induced drop in president's Milei's popularity. Markets have been recently supported by global macro tailwinds and the local tax amnesty that increased the local bid. In this context, we think that 2025 external debt payments are challenging and recommend a defensive switch trade out of Argentina 2030s into Argentina 2041s, which picks up c.16 points.

The economic team is on track to hit the ambitious target of zero fiscal deficit this year; longer-term durability is still a challenge. The elimination of the fiscal deficit is the boldest economic promise this administration has made. We can say the adjustment so far has been impressive and deeper than expected by most analysts. In 2023, the primary balance had stood at -2.7% of GDP, while the total balance was -5.9% of GDP. The outlook was darkened by the virtual elimination of the personal income tax in September 2023. Over January-July 2024, however, public accounts reverted to a primary surplus of +1.3% of this year's estimated GDP, while the total balance was +0.3% (see Figure 5). We think the Treasury can hit the zero deficit target this year even if Congress were to insist on the pension adjustment formula that Milei recently vetoed (see Fiscal clouds coming from Congress). Durability challenges should become more focal.

Revenue should recover as economic activity rebounds, while spending ex-pensions should remain contained. Tax revenue contracted 3.5% y/y in real terms during January-July. The contraction was bigger for activity-related levies such as VAT (-8.5% y/y), debits and credits (-8.6% y/y), or social security contributions (-16.5% y/y) amid a pronounced recession and declining real wages. The expanded PAIS tax on imports only  attenuated this contraction, raising its contribution to tax revenue to 9% from 2% a year ago. However, VAT and social security contributions represent 45% of revenue, and we expect a recovery in economic activity to boost these sources. In addition, the reinstatement of the personal income tax should add 0.15% of GDP to revenue this year, and a moratorium on penalties another 0.3%. All in all, we estimate total revenue for 2024 of close to 17% of GDP.  As for spending, 30% of the adjustment is explained by lower pensions, 23% by capex and 15% by subsidies. Figure 6 shows more details. Milei remains popular, so we do not expect a fiscal loosening designed to compensate for political problems this year and we estimate primary spending close to 15.5% of GDP this year, with a pick-up at the margin explained by higher pension spending.

FIGURE 5. The fiscal adjustment has been sharp...
![](images/7d49d46699c70c22647f79b4b565619ba9796f1fa14b9581c2467151320e694c.jpg)
Note: The figure shows cumulative primary surplus Source: Ministry of Economy, Barclays Research

FIGURE 6. ... but the composition raises durability challenges
<table><tr><td></td><td>Jan-Jul change, %</td><td>Incidence in spending adjustment</td></tr><tr><td>Social spending</td><td>-21</td><td>37</td></tr><tr><td>CapEx</td><td>-81</td><td>23</td></tr><tr><td>Subsidies</td><td>-39</td><td>15</td></tr><tr><td>Operating spending</td><td>-22</td><td>12</td></tr><tr><td>Transfers to provinces</td><td>-75</td><td>7</td></tr><tr><td>Universities</td><td>-34</td><td>4</td></tr><tr><td>Other</td><td>-46</td><td>3</td></tr></table>

Source: Ministry of Economy, Barclays Research

Focus should shift to longer-term durability of the fiscal consolidation. Even if Congress does not insist on the new pension adjustment formula (see Fiscal clouds coming from Congress), the previous formula was established by an executive emergency decree. This means it is liable to be rebated by the Supreme Court. That could restore the previous formula, which would be fiscally costly when inflation comes down. At the same time, capital expenditure has been nearly frozen (-81% y/y during January-July), which makes for an unlikely longer-term equilibrium. Meanwhile, the PAIS tax (which should be around 1.2% of GDP in revenue for 2024) is set to expire. The reinstatement of the personal income tax was an important step to support the durability of the fiscal adjustment, but more institutionalized measures are needed for us to become more confident the adjustment can extend beyond the initial emergency. A pension adjustment formula that keeps pension spending in check, negotiated with the moderate opposition, would be another important step in this direction.

On the FX front, overvaluation and the removal of capital controls remain key sources of uncertainty. In recent statements the president and the economic team have been emphatic that conditions do not yet warrant the removal of capital controls. Net reserves are still very negative and the Milei administration does not appear willing to pay the political costs of a cheaper peso at this stage. Instead, it looks increasingly likely the government wants to deliver fast disinflation to solidify popular support into the 2025 midterms. Recent reports on repo negotiations to help pay Jan-25 and July-25 coupons and amortizations are consistent with this view, as this source would reduce the urgency to lift capital controls and regain market access. The main risk around this path is the possibility of a problematic self-reinforcing loop where the BCRA loses reserves, the FX gap widens and markets price higher chances of an FX jump. Real exchange overvaluation and low initial net reserves favour the emergence of these dynamics. We expect inflation at 123% for 2024 and 40% for 2025.

The outlook for reserve accumulation in the coming months is challenging. A few factors complicate the reserve accumulation outlook for the coming months. First, activity appears to be picking up at the margin as real wages recover. High frequency indicators for July suggest the rebound has started. This implies increasing demand for imports, as Figure 7 shows. In fact, imports jumped 8% in July, after contracting 20% over seven consecutive months. Second, seasonality is typically unfavourable for the central bank in the Sep-Oct-Nov quarter (see Figure 8), as soybean exports decline abruptly and wheat is not harvested yet. Third, the sequential

calendarization of imports will likely imply payments well above accrued imports for the next three months, contrary to what happened when the BCRA was accumulating reserves amid an already shrinking trade surplus. Finally, the 10pp cut to the PAIS tax implies a discrete appreciation of the currency and an efective increase of the FX gap, two unfavourable developments for reserve accumulation. In this context, we expect the BCRA to become a net seller in the FX market, making it harder for authorities to anchor expectations around the current FX policy.

FIGURE 7. Recovering activity is associated with higher imports
![](images/dcf70ed399c7bdbc26896594e90609a2fe5d9fd6f23a9673911aa597928eed29.jpg)
Source: INDEC, Barclays Research

FIGURE 8. Sep, Oct and Nov are seasonally challenging months for reserve accumulation
![](images/48bf1b4ff4c5d6ac57603b43dc0fd4b53c1a136d7aaf9b441dbd95a2cce8d186.jpg)
Source: BCRA, Barclays Research

On the activity front, we expect the rebound to consolidate in Q3 as real wages keep recovering. Economic activity took a big hit earlier in the year from the fiscal adjustment and the abrupt devaluation. Nonagricultural economic activity contracted nearly 9% into June. But zooming in at the margin, high frequency indicators suggest a rebound in nonagricultural activity might have started in July, with a host of metrics such as credit growth, cement dispatches, vehicle registrations and consumer confidence showing more convincing signs of expansion than in previous months. We expect this recovery to gain traction in the reminder of Q3 and to continue in Q4. First, because of the normalization in real wages in motion after the inflation shock of December and January. Nominal wages have been growing consistently above inflation since February, a trend that will likely continue as the real exchange rate appreciates. Although problematic in the medium term, we expect these dynamics to help a recovery in consumption after a sharp 6.7% y/y contraction in Q1. In addition, diminished uncertainty around the macroeconomic outlook should also boost both investment and consumption, as fears of spiralling inflation amid an unconstrained fiscal expansion have been abated. Fiscal policy remains a drag on domestic demand, preventing an even-faster recovery in the short term. In this context, we project a 3.5% contraction in 2024 and a modest rebound of 3.0% in 2025, as financing remains a constraint.

Politics remain supportive, but there are still important questions open. Early this year, one of the main challenges for the Milei administration was to withstand the initial inflation shock and the associated recession with no major political turmoil. This challenge has been overcome, helped by some postponement of energy price adjustments and real exchange rate overvaluation. Milei is more popular than his predecessors at the same stage, and should the historical correlation between the ICG-UTDT confidence in government index hold, he would get above 40% of the votes if midterm elections were held now (see Figure 9 and Figure 10). Two relevant questions are still open. Around a fairly optimistic path, the question is whether an

outlook of gradually declining inflation and a mild recovery in economic activity is enough for Milei to preserve popularity levels that secure him more congressional support in the 2025 elections. Unexpected political contingencies apart, we expect this to be enough. Risks around this outlook are considerable, given the fragility of the disinflation trajectory. If a self-reinforcing feedback loop between declining FX reserves and a widening FX gap takes hold at some point, disinflation could be interrupted, likely eroding Milei's popularity. A second factor of political uncertainty is how society will process the removal of capital controls when it takes place, as it will likely come along with an acceleration in inflation. The political impact of these developments is hard to calibrate, but harder to ignore, given the low base of institutional power embedded in the Milei administration.

FIGURE 9. Milei is more popular than his predecessors at this point...

Source: UTDT, Barclays Research

![](images/f46aaf55c2337e6b6cf8a2feb27c9518ebb5e06c758253f13ad76bcfb8db86c4.jpg)
