## Brazil: Course correction

## Roberto Secemski BCI, US  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US  |  Andrea Kiguel BCI, US

After interrupting its monetary easing cycle in June, we expect the BCB to initiate a relatively small monetary tightening cycle of 150bp this month to respond to increasing inflationary pressures: a tighter output gap amid strong labor markets, weaker BRL, deanchored inflation expectations, and resilient services inflation. The fiscal push should subside in H2 24, increasing the likelihood that the government meets the lower bound of this year's primary balance target. However, structural fiscal challenges continue to abound, imposing risks to the 2025 budget discussions in Congress in Q4 and beyond.

• Credit: Remain Overweight. In terms of valuations, Brazil is cheap to peers such as DomRep and SOAF. GDP growth has surprised on the upside and the likelihood of meeting this year's primary fiscal target has increased. On the curve, we recommend switching out of Brazil 5 5/8% 2047 into Brazil 4.75% 2050 to take advantage of recent dislocation.

• FX: We expect the BRL to outperform within EM in the coming months and recommend shorting USDBRL (entry: 5.66, target: 5.40, stop-loss: 5.82). Brazil spent much of the year in a self-inflicted confidence crisis, but the coordinated message across the administration and the BCB suggests the peak pessimism has been reached for now. The fiscal framework remains vulnerable but, for now, we think authorities have done enough to calm markets on this front as risks for 2024 have subsided. Moreover, the BCB is set to start a hiking cycle in September, building carry back into the FX while the rest of the world—most importantly, the Fed—is easing rates.

• Rates: The BCB is gearing up for its first rate hike, with hints towards an initial 25bp move. Stronger fundamentals support the case for some monetary tightening, along with the deanchoring of inflation expectations and a confidence crisis which has come with a weaker BRL. Our economists are calling for hikes totaling 150bp in the cycle, with the markets pricing something similar. While things feel fairly priced, we acknowledge uncertainty remains high and risks remain towards higher yields in these early stages of the cycle. Thus, we prefer to stay on the sidelines in rates.

We view the current monetary stance in Brazil as contractionary, with the real, ex-ante Selic interest rate hovering around 6.5%, against a neutral level that we believe to be around 5.0-5.5%, with an upside bias (thus above the BCB's oficial view of 4.75%). Similarly, real market rates (one-year DI swaps) are currently around 7.5%, or well above their 10- and 20- year averages of 4.6% and 5.8%, respectively (Figure 12). However, the BCB, the market and us have been repeatedly surprised with the strength of the labor market, with the unemployment rate reaching its lowest level in over 10 years in July, at 6.8%, driving real wages up 4.7% year to date, while also sustaining a significant expansion in the real wage mass of 7.6% in the same period.

Economic activity remains very resilient, with consumption being supported by the labor market but also significant fiscal slippage as federal spending is up 15% in real terms in the 12- month period ended in June. Indeed, the latest GDP data for Q2 24 surprised higher once again, rising 1.4% q/q, up from an upwardly revised 1.0% q/q in Q1, as private consumption grew 3.8% in the first two quarters of this year compared to Q4 23 levels (Figure 13). This, despite the expected negative efect from catastrophic floods in the south in late April and early May, which, in hindsight, had very little efect on economic activity, despite the humanitarian loss the event represented.

FIGURE 12. Real interest rates are above their estimated neutral levels of 5.0-5.5%...
![](images/e0621b7a597cc8929651f023a19b92946c85cf59f0f277d358067296ffc6474c.jpg)
Ex-ante real interest rate (360d DI swap minus 12m infl exp) Ex-ante Selic real rate
Source: B3, BCB, Barclays Research

FIGURE 13. ... but the economy is running hot, particularly private consumption
![](images/b142c2eec3c04828e4d4d76c1c1894ba9d16146425b195e2933a49cdc69d3fdb.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Barclays Research

We expect the Brazilian economy to grow 2.9% in 2024, essentially the same rate as in 2022-23, which is well above our original forecast of 1.9% made more than a year ago, which itself was more optimistic than the prevailing consensus at that time of 1.3%. However, we do not subscribe to the idea that Brazil's potential growth has significantly increased in recent years (we estimate it around 1.5-2.0%), but instead view upside surprises since 2022, to a significant extent, as connected with fiscal impulses as government spending accelerated on multiple occasions, for example, with the "Kamikaze PEC" in 2022, the "Transition PEC" in 2023 and more recently with the lump-sum payment of almost 1% of GDP in delayed court sentences known as precatorios at the turn of the year, among other front-loaded payments of certain social benefits and flood assistance.

The BCB's oficial growth forecast for 2024 is currently 2.3%, which was upgraded from 1.9% in late June in the quarterly inflation report. On that occasion, the bank already foresaw Brazil's output gap as being "around neutrality" (with some estimates suggesting it was positive), but projected it to end the year at -0.4%, up from the previous estimate of -0.6%. In our view, the recent upside surprises in economic activity will lead the BCB to revise its own GDP growth forecast upward, which should lead to a tighter output gap, in turn driving its inflation forecasts higher, all else equal.

Perhaps not surprisingly, the disinflationary trend in the IPCA and its core measures seems to have lost ground, with multiple gauges of services inflation suggesting accommodation at or above the uncomfortable level of 5% y/y, which would not be compatible with a headline inflation target of 3% (Figure 14). Even if it is normal for services to typically run above other components of inflation, such a level would demand that foodstuf, regulated and industrial prices grow below 3% on a consistent basis, which strikes us as unlikely. We see upside risks to our 2024 and 2025 IPCA forecasts of 4.1% and 3.7%, respectively.

As previously discussed in Brazil: Nonlinearities in monetary policy (22 August 2024), there are fundamental reasons that could justify interest rate hikes by the BCB due to a tighter output gap, a strong labor market, weaker BRL, deanchored inflation expectations, and resilient services inflation. We think the latest upside surprises in economic activity and the labor market, combined with a more-hawkish stance since the 31 July decision and, most importantly, recent Copom communication, support the beginning of a relatively small 150bp tightening cycle on 18 September. We note that it is not uncommon for Selic cycles to contrast with interest rate movements in other countries, including in the US (Figure 15).

On balance, it seems more likely that the board would opt for an initial hike of 25bp, based on the latest BCB communication — for instance, Governor Campos Neto said on 30 August that "If and when there is an interest rate adjustment cycle, it will be gradual." In fact, a 25bp hike may be the only common ground to be found for a unanimous decision, despite having less of an efect in terms of credibility and on long-term inflation expectations compared with a 50bp increase. After the 5-4 split decision in May, we believe the board would rather vote unanimously for 25bp than to have a divided vote between a hike of 25bp or 50bp, to prevent the undesired market interpretation that certain board members are more politically constrained (or "dovish") than others, as was the case before.

In any case, we would not discard the possibility of a punchier, 50bp hike, as we believe it would make more sense, from a credibility-building perspective, for the Copom to front-load the adjustment in policy. The latter would reduce the risk of the board falling behind the curve, a situation that would be detrimental at a time when the Copom is supposedly looking to strengthen its commitment to the center of the inflation target.

FIGURE 14. The disinflation process has halted
![](images/f21474dacb4f2c43510a60f94bb2f861b1cd9bba5faa586dfc44cfb36fcf34cf.jpg)
Source: IBGE, Barclays Research

FIGURE 15. Selic cycles have contrasted with the Federal Reserve moves in the past
![](images/384fef49f65e4b0d3f9d2e6db624b3a5212c76c7cd5b597b2c230b1a482da7c8.jpg)
Source: BCB, Bloomberg, Barclays Research

On the fiscal front, the latest developments support our expectation for pragmatism to prevail in the short term, with the government muddling through in an efort to abide by the constraints imposed by its own fiscal framework (both the primary balance target and the spending rule). The likelihood of meeting this year's primary fiscal target of 0% +/- 0.25pp of GDP is on the rise as the government implemented a budget freeze, economic growth surprised higher, and the Senate approved a set of measures to help ofset the cost of the extension of the payroll tax break (which was approved in late 2023 against the government's wishes, representing revenue losses of some BRL26bn, or 0.2% of GDP). As that cost is already reflected in the government's latest bimonthly budget update, any help in bringing in revenue to help cover it (pending approval in the Lower House) would be welcome, bringing fiscal accounts closer to the lower bound of the target range, or a deficit of 0.25% of GDP. On the downside, virtually all the initiatives proposed to ofset the payroll tax break are one-ofs that help bring in revenue in the short term but do not represent structural improvements from 2025 on.

Indeed, the 2025 budget draft (PLOA) presented at the end of August maintained the "status quo" in fiscal accounts, that is, confirming expectations but not being a reason for comfort: for the primary balance target of 0% for next year to be met, BRL168.3bn (1.4% GDP) in new revenue is needed, whereas the efort in containing expenditure growth sums to just 15% of that amount, or BRL25.9bn, thus obviating the government's preference for adjusting taxes over spending. Such a hefty expected increase in revenue is on a fragile basis: BRL46.7bn depends on congressional approval (and House Speaker Lira said that "it is almost impossible" that lawmakers would raise taxes, particularly the CSLL and the income tax on JCP), whereas the remaining BRL121.5bn would come from one-of collections of taxpayers' liabilities and other situations that have been mostly unsuccessful thus far in 2024. It seems likely that ofsetting the cost of the payroll tax break should continue to create political headaches ahead.

For now, market focus in particular is on a government proposal presented to Congress that could bypass fiscal rules to expand a social benefit program that distributes cooking gas vouchers for low-income families ("Auxilio Gas"). The Ministry of Energy wants to expand this program from the current BRL3.4bn to c.BRL5bn in 2025, reaching BRL13.6bn in 2026, an election year, as its target audience would jump from 5.6mn to 20.8mn families. The bill presented would allow the government to bypass the federal budget and existing fiscal rules by having entities transfer revenue from the sale of pre-salt oil production directly to Caixa Economica Federal, instead of the Pre-Salt Social Fund (which is part of the budget). Caixa would then be responsible for operating the gas voucher program, meaning that such revenue and expenditure would no longer transact through the fiscal accounts (thus also not being counted against the spending rule). The 2025 PLOA already reflects that possibility as the amount allocated under the Auxilio Gas next year dropped to a meager BRL0.6bn, supposedly implying that the remaining BRL4.5bn or so would be paid of budget. After strong push-back from markets and the local press, MoF Haddad said the original proposal would be "reviewed" with Lula's chief of staf, minister Rui Costa.

While every budget draft in Brazil is subject to significant changes by the time it is approved by Congress in December, the challenges to deliver a balanced result in 2025 remain sizable, which will likely mean an extended muddle through with potential for back and forth volatility, particularly in November-December, when Congress debates should gain traction, after local elections on 6 October. Moreover, there are structural fiscal challenges waiting to be tackled from 2025 and beyond, including the fast growth in mandatory expenditures that compresses discretionary ones, as discussed in Brazil - Fiscal Accounts: When the rubber meets the road (11 July 2024).

## Caribbean: Surfing diverse wave sizes

Nestor Rodriguez BBMX, Mexico  |  Jason Keene BCI, US

The perennial risk of hurricane season to the Caribbean region was once again manifested after Beryl hit Jamaica back in July. Although the Bahamas and the Dominican Republic have not been meaningful afected so far, the risk remains latent, with NOAA warning that the season could rank among the busiest on record.<sup>49</sup> We reduce Jamaica's growth forecast to 0.8% this fiscal year, but fiscal consolidation should not be afected. Meanwhile, in the Dominican Republic, the focus in next couple of weeks will likely be on fiscal plans with an expected reform and 2025 fiscal budget. We publish here for the first time macroeconomic forecasts for the Bahamas, where we see economic deceleration ahead amid certain commitment to fiscal improvement.

• Credit: We upgrade Bahamas to Overweight and recommend buying BAHAMA 6% 2028s. While we briefly turned less constructive at the start of hurricane season, the Bahamas delivered an impressive FY24 fiscal outturn at 1.3% of GDP. The country realized a primary surplus of more than 3% of GDP for the first time in decades. Hurricane season is halfway over, and while we do not rule out several more named storms in September and October, the Bahamas’ improved fiscal position and lower Treasury yields should mitigate longstanding refinancing risks. We also recommend buying DOMREP 6.6% 2036s.

## Bahamas: Not at desired pace, but fiscal continues to improve

We project the economy to grow 1.9% in 2024 and then approach long-term trend in 2025. Tourism continues to strongly perform, particularly sea-related, with total arrivals growth at 13.0% y/y during the first six months of 2024. However, this is a marked deceleration from 33.2% y/y growth achieved in 2023. Other indicators also show some moderation, such as Bank Credit only growing 0.8% y/y up to June. All in, we estimate growth to moderate to 1.9% in 2024, down from 2.6% in 2023, and to approach its long-term trend, growing 1.5% in 2025. However, we highlight that risks are to the downside amid an expected US slowdown and climate risks.

Government achieved a lower deficit in FY 23/24 at 1.3% of GDP. The preliminary data for the fiscal year running from July 2023 through June 2024 showed the government achieved an overall deficit of US\$186.7mn. In terms of GDP, at 1.3% it would be markedly lower than the 3.8% reached in 2023. Although the deficit ended up higher than the \$131.1mn originally marked in the budget, we highlight that authorities embarked on expenditure containment to partially ofset lower-than-expected revenue. Tax revenue was just 94% of the budgeted amount, afected by VAT underperformance, particularly related to real estate transactions. Furthermore, the overall deficit is also lower than the \$364mn projected by the IMF in its last report in January.

For FY 24/25, the budget aims to keep reducing the deficit towards 0.5% of GDP, but we are less optimistic. We forecast moderate growth in revenue as the economy is expected to keep decelerating into next year and we do not identify meaningful revenue measures inside the budget for the new fiscal year. Still, this moderate growth, to 21.3% of GDP from 20.9% in FY 23/24, should more than ofset expenditures growth. We estimate the interest bill to still grow by 0.1 pp of GDP, while government would need to increase capital expenditures to align with its medium-term goal of 3.5pp of GDP. Overall, we forecast a deficit of 1.1% of GDP in FY 24/25.

Nonetheless, expected fiscal performance is in line with declining debt levels. We estimate public debt to have ended at 80% of GDP by June of this year. This would be lower than the 83% reached just a year ago. Since government should continue to achieve a strong primary surplus at 3.3% of GDP in FY 24/25, up from 2.9% in 2023/24, public debt should continue to decline towards 79% of GDP by the end of June 2025.

We think authorities will continue to mostly rely on domestic financing sources. Our wider projected deficit would push financing needs in FY 24/25 towards \$1.9bn, instead of \$1.8bn. We would expect authorities to stick to their financing plan outlined back in July and mostly finance it through domestic sources, c.80% of their total needs, and the rest from external bank loans. We think an eurobond issuance would only be opportunistic and to perform liability management operations. For now, authorities are remaining focused on a potential debt-fornature swap. Expectations are such a swap could materialize before year-end but would involve less than \$500mn of debt.<sup>50</sup>

## Dominican Republic: Expectation of fiscal news

Fiscal reform and 2025 budget in the pipeline before September ends. The latest news headlines indicate that fiscal reform is to be presented in next few weeks before September ends while the 2025 fiscal budget would be done on September 27.<sup>51</sup>

## The local press has already reported that the fiscal reform could aim to generate

additional resources of 1.5-1.7pp of GDP<sup>52</sup> in next three to five years. This would be achieved by modifying tax exemptions, including ITBIS (VAT), and special treatments, as well as improvements on tax administration. The proceedings would be used to raise spending on transport, water management, health and social protections. But it also plans to increase central bank capitalization by 0.3pp of GDP (from the current 0.6%) and to the electricity sector by 0.2pp of GDP. Although the authorities have not laid out specific plans on central bank recapitalization or the resumption of the electricity pact, such transfers could be positive signs.

According to the budget director, the 2025 fiscal budget, will imply a fiscal deficit of 3.1% of GDP in 2025, similar to the one for 2024. The proposal would comply with the recent fiscal responsibility law, with an increase on the expenditure side of just 2.7% y/y in real terms.

Meanwhile, it will not initially include changes from the fiscal reform as it is not clear to them if that would be approved by Congress before year-end.

Although timing for approval is uncertain, we continue to think momentum is supportive. The timing of when the fiscal reform could be approved is not certain as, first, the authorities need to advance constitutional reform at the legislative level (see CAC Radar: Into budget season). However, we think that Abinader's party and allies' overwhelming presence in Congress, with 29 seats out of 32 in the Senate and 147 out of 190 in the Lower House, coupled with the recent approval of the Fiscal Responsibility Law focused on containing expenditure growth, should ease discussions on the fiscal situation. Nevertheless, it should be important to monitor street response to potential changes in the tax structure.

Economic activity continues to be the strongest in the region. Through July, the economy expanded 5.0% y/y (Figure 19), driven by a strong manufacturing recovery, construction and services, with lodging even growing 8.7% y/y. This performance is in line with our growth forecast of 5.0% for 2024. Although we expect some deceleration into next year, afected by a global slowdown and the upcoming fiscal consolidation, we estimate healthy 2025 growth at 4.5%.

Fiscal afairs and growth should also be supported by a resumption of the central bank's easing cycle. On August 29, the central bank restarted its easing cycle by cutting 25bp, to 6.75%. The bank had been on hold since December 2023 after cutting 150bp between May and November. The cycle was mainly interrupted by external concerns that resulted on FX pressures this year. We expect gradual cuts to continue until the bank reaches a neutral stance, likely around 5.00%.

Although USDDOP is still trending higher, with the spot around depreciating around 5.6% versus a year ago, expectations that the US Fed will start cutting soon and reserves still in a strong position at USD\$14.9bn — around 6 months of imports — after the last bond issuance in July, should ease concerns. Also, stable oil prices and positive performance from FX inflows through June such as tourism (14.1% y/y), exports (2.3% y/y) and remittances (4.4% y/y) should help to maintain a manageable current account deficit this year at 3.0% of GDP with steady FDI inflows covering most of such deficit.

## Jamaica: Beryl aftermath

The economy was running near its long-term trend before Hurricane Beryl hit.  GDP decelerated to 1.4% y/y during Q1 2024, down from 1.7% in Q4 2023. This was consistent with a timid quarterly growth of 0.3% q/q sa in Q1 (Figure 21), dragged down by negative prints in agriculture, construction and retail services. The only ofsets were positive performance in manufacturing and hotels and restaurants, although those were decelerating. Overall, the economy ended up growing 1.9% during FY 2023-24 (April 2023 through March 2024).

The 2024/25 FY is expected to be in a weaker spot. Back in July, the southern part of the island was afected by Hurricane Beryl, a category 4 storm, which will likely be enough to push GDP into a contraction during Q3 2023.  Although we would expect to see some rebound in Q4 2024, it likely will be limited by soft activity already registered in manufacturing, tourism and construction prior to the shock. We now forecast the economy to grow 0.8% during FY 24/25, then rebound to 1.6% in FY 25/26.

Central bank to support economic recovery. On August 20, the central bank cut its policy rate by 25bp to 6.75%. This was the first cut since the end of the hiking cycle in February 2023, aiming to support an economic recovery amid controlled inflation, anchored on the target range (4.0-6.0%), at 5.1% y/y. The central bank also mentioned it envisaged low inflation risks from post-hurricane recovery, with limited damage to the agricultural sector.  This is being coupled with eforts to improve liquidity in the financial sector by reducing BOJ's absorption through market operations.

The fiscal cost should be limited, with Jamaica tapping existing resources. Although the hurricane shock was not enough to trigger the catastrophe bond with the World Bank, which provides US\$150mn in insurance coverage and was renewed back in April, authorities have mentioned that the response to Beryl would come from existing savings, insurance and redirecting existing expenditure lines. We expect the fiscal balance for FY 24/25 to be at -0.2%, slightly down from the 0.3% surplus registered in the previous year, but with the primary surplus strong at 5.5% of GDP. Hence, public debt should continue to decline towards 68% by March 2025, in line with the target of being below 60.0% by the end of FY 27/28.

Star finance minister to depart, but policy continuity should not be in play. The IMF recently announced that Jamaica's Minister of Finance Nigel Clarke will become Deputy Managing Director at the Fund starting October 31, 2024. Clarke has been MoF since March 2018 in charge of the successful fiscal consolidation eforts. The government said it has identified his successor but has not disclosed who it is yet. We wouldn't expect much efect on fiscal policy in Jamaica since fiscal prudence has been well embodied in Jamaica's economic policy spectrum for more than a decade. In fact, the prime minister stated that the public can expect policy continuity and a continuation of the economic achievements.

## Central America:  Elusive Plans

Nestor Rodriguez BBMX, Mexico  |  Jason Keene BCI, US

As we are in fiscal budget season for next year, we identify the varying plans and risks across the region. In Costa Rica, recent fiscal performance will likely start to weaken amid a shrinking primary surplus, while Eurobonds might not be in the picture until 2026. In El Salvador, we remain cautious on an IMF program being reached soon but incentives remain in place. In Guatemala, Arevalo's administration is reinforcing its plan to increase the fiscal deficit. Finally, delays on IMF program review in Honduras remain in place, while recently the political temperature has been rising.

• Credit: We remain Overweight Costa Rica, Market Weight Guatemala and Honduras, and Underweight El Salvador. We recommend selling ELSALV 8.625% 2029s.

## Costa Rica: Fiscal slippage becomes base case

We think the 2025 fiscal budget recognizes the deteriorated fiscal outlook, but there might be further risks to it. Next year's budget, submitted to Congress, recognizes some fiscal slippage, as it estimates a primary surplus of 1.5% of GDP, down from the 1.8% originally budgeted for 2024. It also estimates a slower decline in public debt, as it would remain above 60% of GDP until the end of 2026 (previously at the end of 2024). Nonetheless, we estimate there are further risks to the fiscal outlook next year as the budget relies on optimistic forecasts for revenue growth (5.3% y/y) and expenditure containment (1.1% y/y).

We forecast the primary surplus will be closer to 1.0% of GDP next year. This is lower than the 1.3% we estimate for this year and lower than the 2025 budget projection, given our more conservative forecast for revenues, but it is slightly better than our previous forecast at 0.9% of GDP since the budget reflects some willingness to contain expenditures (Figure 23). Our forecast for revenues assumes that the weak trend from this year will persist, with total revenues only growing 1.6% YTD through June this year, as we expect economic growth to decelerate to 3.6% next year, down from our forecast of 4.2% in 2024.

The government’s base case now is no eurobond issuance this year: In line with its latest request to make the targets embedded in the eurobonds law more flexible and to delay issuance by one year (see Costa Rica: Eurobonds in doubt), the government expects to close the financing gap for the rest of 2024 with just internal debt. This was also previously confirmed on August 22 as part of its updated financing strategy<sup>53</sup> .

But 2025 issuance is still part of the base case. According to the government’s financing strategy for next year, it expects to issue \$1.0bn of external bonds. The strategy highlights the importance of this transaction not pressuring domestic rates or the exchange rate, and to ease refinancing risks.

However, we maintain our view there are risks of further delays given political tensions. As we highlighted in Costa Rica: Eurobonds in doubt, the political appetite for issuance might be limited due to the recent confrontation between the executive branch and Congress, particularly after the proposal to conduct a public referendum, called "Ley Jaguar," that aimed to reduce the powers of the General Comptroller's Ofice. The proposal was eventually deemed unconstitutional, but it fanned political tensions<sup>54</sup> .  Also, deputies from the opposition have questioned past eurobond issuance and highlighted that they were not pleased with the authorities’ reports and their efect on fiscal savings <sup>55</sup> <sup>56</sup> .

In addition, 2025 will be marked by elevated political tensions as the country prepares for a general election in February 2026. Hence, we do not think Congress, where the government only holds 9 out of 57 seats, will easily concede to lower the fiscal deficit target to 1.3% of GDP from 1.85% in 2024 and 1.85% of GDP from 2.25% in 2025. In our view, this poses risks that the next external issuance might only happen in 2026 under a new administration.

## El Salvador:  Elusive IMF program

On August 6, the IMF published a Staf Statement noting progress toward a potential program. According to the statement, there has been progress on: 1) preliminary understandings to improve the primary fiscal balance by 3.5pp of GDP over a three-year period with initial focus on rationalizing the public wage bill; 2) a plan to gradually strengthen financial system reserve bufers; and 3) preliminary understandings were reached on a comprehensive multi-year strategy to enhance governance, transparency, and the overall investment climate.

Although we read the overall statement as positive, we remain skeptical on a potential agreement before year-end with Bitcoin still the main hurdle. According to the IMF's statement, the Bitcoin project and how to address risks from it remain unsolved, and further discussions remain necessary. Salvadorean authorities seems to be open and flexible to discuss the changes required, following reports stating that authorities are planing to reform the current Digital Assets Law and Bitcoin Law to comply with some FTAF recommendations<sup>57</sup>  . However, as has been the case since 2021, we think that might not be enough, and changes to Bitcoin's status as legal tender will remain a contentious topic.

In addition, there is still uncertainty on fiscal execution and the commitment to contain deficit widening. We think the IMF might also want to see year-end fiscal execution, given the unfavorable YTD trend, and the 2025 fiscal budget to be submitted before September 30. The nonfinancial public sector fiscal deficit up to July was 0.8% of GDP, which is a slight widening of the 0.6% of GDP in the year-earlier period. There are some favorable trends: strong revenues, which rose 10% y/y YTD, and a willingness to contain current transfers, which were down 1.6% y/y YTD. But other items, such as consumption (mostly related to wages) grew 16.3% y/y YTD,

interest payments increased 8.0% y/y, and capital expenditures rose 9.7% y/y. This performance is in line with our deficit forecast at 2.6% of GDP, up from 2.3% in 2023.

Two other factors also might mean El Salvador will not rush to sign a deal: its financing needs are covered and the US elections. First, since we wrote after last unexpected bond issuance here, the fiscal space to muddle through this year and next year was reinforced, albeit at the cost of sustainability concerns starting in 2027. Hence, there is no urgency to obtain new financing in coming months.  Second, with upcoming US elections, and given that Bukele has maintained a certain activism in US politics via social media<sup>58</sup> , he could be inclined to wait and see if the outcome of the election could afect negotiations with the IMF.

Overall, we still think there are incentives to eventually reach an agreement on a program, particularly amid continued struggles to sustain economic growth. In addition to the clear benefit of providing fiscal sustainability, a program could also be important to provide greater economic policy certainty. That, coupled with security improvements, could help El Salvador to achieve higher potential growth. For now, the economy is still struggling to show sustain improvement (Figure 25). The monthly GDP proxy expanded just 0.4% y/y in H1 24 dragged down by contractions in construction and manufacturing. Although the economy could recover slightly in H2, any rebound will be capped amid already weak remittances (1.9% y/y in July) and exports (1.0% y/y) in July. We now estimate the economy will grow 1.8% in 2024, down from 2.8% in the June Emerging Markets Quarterly, and slowing markedly from the 3.5% expansion in 2023.

## Guatemala: Focus on a wider fiscal deficit

There have been reminders of political and governance challenges for Arevalo's administration. August was marked by a resumption of tensions between Arevalo's government and the political opposition led by the Attorney General (AG). On August 21, the AG’s ofice submitted a new request to strip President Arevalo of his immunity so he can face corruption charges concerning payments made to construction companies under public work contracts. Then, on August 26, the Constitutional Court applied a temporary injunction against the modified 2024 budget owing to legal challenges raised by opposition. Nonetheless, the

budget was eventually approved a second time two days later by avoiding the procedural issues that resulted in its suspension the first time.

Overall, we do not think real threats to his administration will materialize as long as he retains popular support. The latest CID-Gallup poll from May still showed his approval rating at 54%. That support should keep the Constitutional Court – the ultimate arbiter of constitutional matters – neutral, avoiding an institutional crisis, as was shown with the competition of the transition period. The next political tests are the renewal of the 13 magistrates of the Supreme Court next October and approval of 2025 budget by Congress. Although we expect the budget to be approved, it will be important to monitor potential legal challenges to it.

Arevalo's administration recently secured a win in Congress on the 2024 budget… The legislature approved the 2024 budget modification for a second time with 114 votes in favor, above the 107 required. The modification was mostly positive, in our view, as it updated expected revenues given that the current budget in place was from 2023, after the original 2024 proposal tabled by the previous administration was suspended by the Constitutional Court in January 2024. It also allows the government to allocate more money to social and infrastructure spending.

… but the deficit might be lower than planned. Although the modified budget aims for an overall annual deficit of 2.5% of GDP, we estimate it to be 1.0% of GDP, down slightly from 1.3% of GDP in 2023, owing to the challenges to meaningfully increase spending in just four last months of the year. Up to July 2024, there was an accumulated surplus of 0.4% of GDP, versus a deficit of 0.5% of GDP in the year-earlier period.

The fiscal deficit could widen next year. The 2025 fiscal budget is consistent with eforts to increase budgeted deficit toward 3.1% of GDP. This would be achieved mainly by increasing spending to 15.5% of GDP from 14.5% in the 2024 modified budget, on both current and capital. Estimated revenues would only partially ofset that increase, as they would rise 12.3% of GDP versus 11.9% in 2024. Since we think the administration is eager to spend more in order to deliver on campaign promises and bolster its popularity, the deficit could indeed be close to 3.0% of GDP, in our view (Figure 27).

A larger fiscal deficit focusing on investment could ofer some impulse to the economy after steadier-than-expected growth in 2024. Up to July, the economic activity index grew 3.4% y/y YTD, very similar to the 3.5% registered in 2023, but below our forecast of 3.8% for full-year 2024. Slow remittances deceleration and weak external demand are behind this performance. We now estimate the economy will grow 3.3% this year and only pick up to 3.6% next year, supported by higher public investment, but capped by an expected US slowdown.

The budget also implies debt issuance would double from the 2023 and 2024 levels. The plan is to rely on domestic or external bond issuance, at the authorities’ discretion. The budget estimates total issuance of GTQ25.104bn (or c.USD\$3.2bn). We think authorities might actually issue close to \$2.2bn of external bonds next year. Public debt would increase moderately to 28.5% of GDP from 26% at the end of June.

## Honduras: Impossible trinity and rising political tensions

IMF announced that a program review is now planned for the first half of October. The announcement came amid a delay of the first review since March, with the second review originally scheduled to be in September. The Fund's short statement on this also welcomed the 2025 draft budget bill. However, we do not think fiscal performance has been a concern or behind the review delays. The statement was limited, saying only that there have been productive discussions on economic policies to safeguard domestic and external stability.

Signs of abandonment of the “Impossible trinity,” but not enough yet.  On August 6, the central bank increased its monetary policy rate by 100bp to 4.00% for the first time since November 2020. This initial hike might be a positive step towards strengthening the role of monetary  policy, but it might not be suficient, particularly with persistence of discriminatory FX auctions. We think the authorities' policy of avoiding FX depreciation while sacrificing reserves is behind the key disagreement with the IMF and where the Fund will likely need to show flexibility along with a specific plan to strenghen it in the next review. This is evident as reserves continue to fall, currently at \$6,956mn down from \$7,700mn a year ago and  below IMF's initial target (Figure 29).

Although there has not been new public reports since April on potential external bond issuance<sup>59</sup> , we do not discount the possibility of the authorities pursuing issuance of \$600-800mn before year-end to support FX inflows and the reserve position.

Relatively stable outlook for the fiscal position and growth. Fiscal execution up to June was consistent with a nonfinancial public surplus of 0.9% of GDP, down only slightly from 1.15% of GDP in 2023 for the same period. This would be consistent with our forecast of an overall deficit of 1.5% of GDP for full-year 2024, slightly below last year’s projection by the IMF of a deficit of 1.7% of GDP. According to our forecasts, this would be consistent with public debt declining to 46% of GDP this year from 48% in 2023. We expect decent economic growth of 3.8% this year, supported by construction and services.

Political heat rose recently, with potential costs for the administration. President Xiomara Castro's administration has been in the eye of the hurricane after two recent developments: 1) on August 29, she announced the end of extradition treaty with the US after the US ambassador to Honduras expressed concerns about a meeting between top Honduran oficials and Venezuela’s defense minister; and 2) the resignations of the Honduran defense minister, Jose Manuel Zelaya (brother of former President Zelaya who is also the husband of President Castro), and his father Carlos Zelaya, deputy and secretary to Libre (Castro's party) to facilitate an investigation into a leaked video of Carlos Zelaya meeting drug trafickers to discuss campaign financing for Castro’s presidential bid in 2013. In an unexpected move, former finance minister Rixi Moncada was named the new Defense Minister. Moncada has announced her candidacy to be Libre party’s candidate for president in 2025.

President Castro has tried to distance herself from the situation and claims that the accusations represent an attempted coup d' etat in Honduras<sup>60</sup> . However, since her Libre party and political platform campaigned on anti-corruption and anti-drug-traficking rhetoric, these scandals could take a toll on their aspirations in the November 2025 elections. Focus will turn first to the primary elections scheduled for March 9, 2025. We think a more divided electorate could pose risks of an abandonment of fiscal prudence next year.

## Chile: Between Scylla and Charybdis

Ivan Stambulsky Barclays, UK  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US  |  Andrea Kiguel BCI, US

The inflation outlook suggests caution, but the BCCh appears ready to look through these pressures amid a likely cutting Fed and still-subdued activity growth. We expect the policy rate to end this year at 5.0%, with a terminal rate of 4.25%. We expect the fiscal deficit to be higher than the MoF forecasts, implying a deviation from the target for this year. In politics, October's local elections should provide a hint of the likely shape of the political landscape heading into the 2025 presidential election. Polls suggest the center-right is gaining traction, after consecutive wins by more extreme alternatives.

• Credit: Remain Overweight. Chile spreads have compressed to IG EM peers and screen relatively less attractive than earlier in the year. However, we expect supply for the remainder of 2024 to remain relatively low (\$1bn) despite some fiscal risks in 2H 24.  Moreover, polls are suggesting a more-favourable landscape for center-right candidates than in previous elections, which will likely be a supportive driver of spreads. On the curve, we recommend switching out Chile 2042s into Chile 2047s, which are currently trading at the higher end of their historical trading range.

• FX: We expect additional easing to weigh less on the currency, as the bulk of the normalization is behind us and further rate cuts by BCCh should be broadly in line with the Fed cycle. The CLP should thus trade more in line with fundamentals, and on this front, valuations look stretched, which suggests there is room for some recovery in the CLP. Copper remains a risk given ongoing weakness in China's economy, but terms of trade should not sufer too much relative to oil. Lastly, AFP flows appear to be more supportive, with inflows into Fund E (predominantly local currency bonds)

• Rates: Growth concerns are back in the spotlight, with private consumption weakening more than expected. While headline inflation was pressured by electricity tarif adjustments, core inflation has remained well behaved and 2y inflation expectations are largely anchored. Weak demand should keep core inflation in check in the coming months. In this context, the BCCh has guided for the policy rate to return to neutral over the next year. We think risks are skewed towards a terminal rate below the 4% neutral midpoint, and see room for lower 2y Camara rates.

The inflation outlook suggests caution... Inflation was already running well above target before the July electricity tarif shock. By June, 6m saar headline inflation was at 4.8% (up from a recent low of 1.9% in October 2023), with tradable goods inflation an even higher at 5.4% (up from 1.1% in December 2023, see Figure 31). This reacceleration was triggered by the depreciation of the CLP, in our view, which has not reversed (Figure 32). We believe high indexation early next year, still pending electricity adjustments (which could add around 1pp to inflation) and continued weak CLP will keep feeding into prices. In this context, we see inflation closing 2024 at 4.9% and 2025 at 4.0%.

## ...but the BCCh appears ready to look through these pressures amid a likely cutting

Fed. The BCCh cut rates by 25bp in its September meeting, as we expected. The bank also made a dovish revision to the policy rate corridor in the September IPoM, penciling in an extra 50bp of rate cuts by the end of 2025 compared with the June statement. The general tone of the report was dovish, claiming that the impact of the electricity tarif shocks will be transitory and a softer outlook for internal demand implies lower risks of inflation persistence. In this context, we expect the BCCh to deliver two additional 25bp cuts this year, with the monetary policy rate ending 2024 at 5.0%. For next year, we expect the BCCh to retain this stance, if the Fed cuts as markets are pricing, and see the policy rate ending 2025 at 4.25%.

FIGURE 31. Inflation was picking up well before the electricity tarif hikes
![](images/8b553230d77318ef837ec278777c8d367426413ab2911b9c5227ea6537d2f759.jpg)
Source: Haver Analytics, Barclays Research

FIGURE 32. The CLP still looks cheap in real terms given terms of trade strength
![](images/b6f35d302ca36d076fb4c00cd31adb2993839237ef7b57a24d64b0da972ddeb2.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Barclays Research

Growth surprised to the upside in July but the outlook remains modest. After a strong start in January and February, activity growth disappointed later in the year. Q2 GDP interrupted a streak of three consecutive quarterly expansions, dragged by soft consumption expenditure. The July Imacec surprised to the upside, posting an annual growth of 4.2%. However, 6m nonmining growth stands at a meager 1.6%, well below the 2015-19 average of 2.6%. For the remainder of the year, we think the economy will register a stronger growth than in Q2, although the performance will still be modest. Investment and exports look supportive at the margin and consumption is likely to rebound from the Q2 correction, as services growth normalizes and real wages keep growing steadily. But a modest pick-up in growth is unlikely to be enough to alter the balance of risks for the BCCh. We forecast GDP growth of 2.3% in 2024, with a 2.0% increase in nonmining output.

We are more pessimistic than the MoF on the fiscal outlook and expect further slippage this year. In the public finances report for Q2, the MoF reafirmed its 1.9% of GDP fiscal deficit projection for 2024, 0.5pp tighter than in 2023. These projections assume growth in real revenues of +6.2% y/y and +3.9% y/y in real expenditures. We consider these assumptions as too optimistic and expect a wider fiscal deficit of -2.9% in 2024. The performance during 1H was weak, with real revenues contracting by 4.7% y/y, spending up by 6.5% y/y, and the fiscal deficit widening to 3.7% of GDP. For the MoF's 2024 forecast to prove correct, government revenues would need to be 12% higher in 2H than in 1H, and spending only 1.4% higher. As Figure 33 and Figure 34 show, this would be against a regular historical pattern of +0% and +20%, respectively, which suggests that fiscal inertia will be a challenging force against the MoF's intention to reduce the budget deficit. In addition, the recent falls in copper prices limit the potential for help stemming from mining revenues. Finally, regional elections in October add a political disincentive for fiscal consolidation. We expect the recently announced eforts on the spending side in 2H to moderate the deteriorating trend seen in 1H, but not to prevent a substantial deviation from the fiscal target.

FIGURE 33. MoF revenues projection look optimistic...
![](images/41bbdfeb5724d78d2012bcb0dfec29930d2f3de0d688bd23e5e881ed0f9af106.jpg)
Note: The red bar is the ratio of 2H/1H revenues necessary for the MoF's latest projections to materialize.
Source: Ministry of Finance, Barclays Research

FIGURE 34. ... as well as its projected adjustment to spending
![](images/60540b198f2ae03ee76f4a64b48b22c464357f8389d298a0b4e77e2e7f502b44.jpg)
Note: The red bar is the ratio of 2H/1H spending necessary for the MoF's latest fiscal projections to materialize. Source: Ministry of Finance, Barclays Research

On the political front, no news is good news. The first three years of the Boric administration have been marked by frustrated reform eforts. Two constitutional referendums, a pension reform, and a tax reform have faced the dificulties of consensus building amid fragmented politics. The absence of relevant reforms to address concerns embedded in the 2019 turmoil leaves latent risks, but Chileans' priorities appear to have shifted away from re-foundational considerations for now. According to Cadem's survey, 70% of the Chileans think that combating crime should be the government's top priority, followed by immigration, chosen by 26%. After two rejections of radical constitutional reforms, this new consensus bodes well for the preservation of macroeconomic functionality.

Local elections would act as a political test for President Boric and give some clues around the political landscape before next year's presidential election. On October 27, Chile will choose governors,  mayors and regional councillors. According to Cadem's survey, 51% would vote for candidates from the opposition in this year's election while 36% would pick a candidate from the government's coalition, consistent with Boric's low support levels (at 35% over the past month). Although presidential elections are still far of, surveys suggest a more-favourable landscape for center-right candidates than in previous elections. Provicencia's mayor, Evelyn Matthei, former Labor Minister to Sebastián Piñera is the politician with the highest net approval according to Cadem, and would beat the rest of the candidates both in the first and the second round if elections were held today.

## Colombia: Half over

Alejandro Arreaza BCI, US  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US  |  Erick Martinez BCI, US

With President Petro's term now half over, economic fundamentals have not shown meaningful deterioration; on the contrary, the economy continues to advance toward a soft landing after macroeconomic imbalances have been significantly reduced. Although, there are still sources of uncertainty, potential damage to the fiscal accounts looks contained and there could be room for positive surprises. An increasingly negative output gap, declining inflation pressures, reduced external vulnerability and the beginning of Fed rate cuts gives Banrep room to continue the easing cycle with 475bp of rate cuts and potential to accelerate, but the truckers' strike reduces the likelihood of an acceleration of the pace this month.

• Credit: Remain Market Weight. Colombia valuations are cheap to EM rating peers. However, we  expect the combination of lingering fiscal uncertainties in 2024 and remaining \$3bn hardcurrency supply to prevent Colombia spreads from outperforming, as real money positioning is not particularly supportive. However, at current valuations, we think downside risks are contained as the country gradually moves to presidential elections (2026) and given that economic fundamentals have not deteriorated meaningfully.

• FX:  The COP has outperformed its high-yielding peers in LatAm, and we expect this to continue in the near term. Banrep is likely err on the cautious side, in our view, making the peso one of the few remaining carry currencies. Disinflation resumed in July after a flat Q2, but the recovery in domestic demand remains strong and investment is rebounding. And while the truckers' strike was short-lived, the impact on inflation remains uncertain. The COP could underperform into next year as carry erodes, which has been a consistent theme across EM.

• Rates:  Despite some near-term uncertainty (supply disruptions due to the trucker's strike) Banrep has significant room to ease. The market is currently pricing mostly 50bp cuts, with small chances of acceleration, and a terminal rate around 7%. Declining inflation, reduced external vulnerability, and the beginning of the Fed cuts should allow Banrep to deliver, but we think it is unlikely markets price a lower terminal for the time being. We do, however, see room for TES 42s yields to tighten. Risks are still on a challenging fiscal outlook amid continued underperformance of tax collection, but this could be ofset by a lower than budgeted interest bill.

## Soft landing continues

Colombia has challenges, and, in particular, the credibility of the authorities has remained under scrutiny since the country lost its investment grade rating in 2021. In addition, the political noise following President Petro's election in 2022 has contributed to the persistence of some concerns. Market participants have been pricing a large deterioration in fundamentals, placing Colombia on par with countries rated up to three notches below its worst sovereign credit rating. However, even if there is significant uncertainty and the government lacks a convincing narrative to help to change views, so far economic fundamentals have not been moving in that direction and even in the worst-case scenarios, a deterioration of that magnitude appears unlikely in what is left of Petro's presidential term.

With Petro's term now half over, the economy continues to advance toward a soft landing after having significantly reduced its macroeconomic imbalances. When necessary, the administration has shown willingness to adjust. While there could be room for improvements, there are also important bufers that should contain the potential fiscal damage and leave room for positive surprises. Institutions so far have proven resilient and, as the country moves into 2025, we expect succession to come into focus. There is still a year and a half before the

presidential elections, but candidates are already making themselves known, and practically all of the possible alternatives represent a more market-friendly approach.

In the interim, Colombia's twin deficits have not disappeared, but there has been a strong correction and they now seem manageable. There is still uncertainty on the fiscal accounts, but the external vulnerabilities have been significantly reduced. From a current account deficit of more than 6% of GDP in 2022, it declined to 1.6% in Q2-24, the lowest in nearly two decades. The recent decline in oil prices and a rebound of import demand as economic growth picks up is likely to lead to a widening of the deficit in the coming quarters, but the aggregate for the year might not exceed the 2.5% registered in 2023, which would be better than we previously expected (3.0%). Over the next two years, it is likely to stay well below the 10-year average (4.6%). Remittances have remained almost 1.5pp of GDP higher than pre-pandemic levels, becoming a strong source of support.

FDI continues to fully cover the current account deficit. The profits of foreign companies operating in the country, which represent a large portion of the deficit, re-circulate into the economy as FDI. This has contributed to a decline in the external debt levels of 7pp of GDP from the 2021 highs. The government's reliance in foreign investors has also continued to decline, with the participation of the latter in the local market falling to 19.7% in July, near 8pp below the 2022 peak.

Oil production, which was one of the major concerns due to Petro's reticence to grant new concessions, is up 34k b/d (c.5%) relative to when get he assumed ofice. The country could be missing opportunities such as the development of non-conventional oil; however, the number of operating rigs, which is a good proxy for investment in the sector, remains in line with the 10- year average, suggesting that production should at least remain stable. Regulatory changes, such as the non-deductibility of royalties that was included in the 2022 tax reform, have been overturned by the Constitutional Court. Whether new oil concessions are granted will be at the discretion of the next administration.

The weakest link is still the fiscal accounts; however, even in this area there seem to be significant bufers keeping risks contained. Whether or not authorities will meet the fiscal rule target remains to be seen, but judging by the country risk premium, the rule does not seem to have much credibility. The most important factor should be how the expected deficit afects debt dynamics, such as where it stands relative to peers and how it would be funded. Colombia's debt levels are 10pp of GDP below where they were when the country fell to high yield and 15pp below where they were projected to be now according to oficial estimates at the time of the downgrade. Even assuming the worst possible scenarios for the fiscal deficit, the debt is unlikely to reach the levels at the time of the downgrade before the end of Petro's presidential term.

While authorities have already presented the first draft of the 2025 budget, targeting a deficit of 5.1% of GDP, the focus remains on 2024. Within the 2025 budget COP12trn of expenditures are conditioned on the approval of a new tax reform that is supposed to collect same amount. Legislators are already resisting this new reform, which could force the government to cut spending again. Next year's result will depend to a great extent on the closing of this year and there still a significant level of uncertainty about that; therefore, it could be a premature discussion. Nonetheless, in the meantime it does not help to lower the level of noise.

The main concern remains the underperformance of tax collection; however, we think this is at least partly explained by one-of factors that could be at least partly reverted. A sharp decline in nominal tax collections and as a percentage of GDP do not seem consistent with our GDP growth forecast of an expansion of 1.8%, recovering from 0.6% last year, and average inflation of about 6%. For now, the expenditure freeze announced in June by the authorities helps to contain the risks.

If tax collection continues to underperform, it could potentially be ofset by a lower-thanbudgeted interest bill. The increase in the interest bill is the main driver of the widening of the deficit that authorities expect in 2024, to 5.6% of GDP from 4.3% last year; however, this seem to be due to an overly conservative estimate. The government expects interest costs to increase by more than 30% y/y, while the actual data, at least until April, is showing a 3% decline. Lower domestic and international interest rates and inflation, as well as an exchange rate that has on average been stronger, could result in the interest bill being approximately 1% of GDP lower than budgeted. Although the interest bill would not help with compliance with the fiscal rule, which is based on the primary balance, if revenues are in line with the government’s expectation, lower interest would leave room for the overall fiscal balance to be better than the government expects (5.6%), containing the risk of additional funding needs.

Concerns about the growth outlook have also declined. Year-to-date, Banrep has increased its 2024 GDP forecast twice and, along with consensus, has been converging toward our forecast of 1.8%. Although June GDP disappointed, the recovery in domestic demand remains strong and supported GDP growth of 2.1% y/y in Q2. One positive highlight in the economic activity data was the 4.2% y/y expansion in investment, after five consecutive quarters of contraction. The building component recovered, growing 15% y/y, while machinery and equipment – the factor most closely linked to the economy's production capacity – remained resilient, expanding 2.1% y/y in Q2 24. This supports our view that the moderation of investment is more of a cyclical than structural phenomenon.

The announcement of the reduction of the diesel subsidy is positive news that could support the fiscal consolidation process from a medium-term perspective. Fuel subsidies have declined from c.2.5% of GDP in 2022 but still represent about 0.8% of GDP. Authorities are facing resistance to this measure with a truckers' strike. However, whatever happens on this front should not afect the fiscal outlook for 2024-25. Given that the treasury pays Ecopetrol the accumulation in the fuel stabilization fund with a one-year lag, the main impact should be felt in 2026. In the short term, the main efect could be on inflation.

The direct impact of the increase in diesel prices is small and was already considered in Banrep's estimates, but the truckers' strike could cause supply disruptions that could create at least temporary pressures on prices. A benign inflation print in August helps to contain those risks (0.00% m/m vs consensus of 0.20%), but the uncertainty about the magnitude of the efect of the strike seems likely to make Banrep maintain a cautious approach, reducing the likelihood of an acceleration of rate cuts for now. Therefore, we now expect another 50bp in September and October rather than the 75bp we previously expected.

Despite some uncertainty about the pace, Banrep has significant room to continue with monetary easing and potentially accelerate at its December meeting. An increasingly negative output gap, declining inflation, reduced external vulnerability and the potential beginning of Fed rate cuts give Banrep room to cut rates 475bp from now to the end of 2025, keeping unchanged our terminal rate of 6.0%.

## Ecuador: Vote dispersion risk

## Alejandro Arreaza BCI, US  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US

A high number of candidacies and more competition in the centre-right adds uncertainty and warrants some caution ahead of February's presidential elections. President Noboa still has the upper hand, and his potential re-election could be a strong positive catalyst that could support the continuity of the reform agenda, but in the current conditions, a first-round victory is less likely and he may be forced into a tight run-of. In the meantime, economic measures adopted by the government are yielding results and together with the possibility of a debt-for-nature transaction help to contain short-term risks.

• Credit: Remain Overweight. Ecuador valuations are supported by fundamentals, given the central bank’s hard-currency liquidity and the country’s multilateral funding. Moreover, as the debt-for-nature-swap will take out bonds from the market, it will help to improve technicals. On the curve, we recommend switching out of Ecuador 2030s into Ecuador 2035s because it takes out cash, gains a higher current yield (after the July coupon step-up) and the Ecuador 2030s' amortization is still too distant.

## The electoral race begins with some warning signs

Although we still have a constructive medium-term view on Ecuador's economic outlook, we see some warning signs that should not be dismissed. Political parties' selection of candidates for the presidential election, scheduled for February 9, 2025, ended on August 17, resulting in a high number of entrants. In particular, there is more competition in the centre-right, which increases uncertainty and warrants some caution.

President Noboa still has the upper hand. Polls place him as the front runner, with 33% of support, which could put him above of the 40% threshold needed for a first-round victory if null votes and undecided voters are excluded<sup>61</sup> . However, the increasingly fragmented political spectrum makes a first-round victory less likely, which suggests Noboa could be forced into a run-of. That would imply a longer campaign, extending until April 2025, and potentially a tighter race. Progress on fiscal consolidation and the disbursement of multilateral funding are helping to contain short-term risks, but sustainability will depend in the outcome of the elections.

A total of 17 presidential candidacies have been announced, which could split the vote. Many of them are little known and seem unlikely to get a meaningful number of votes in isolation. Some candidates are using the presidential race to raise their profile and compete for other positions or to support their lists of legislators to the National Assembly. Nonetheless, the dispersion risk is still there and applies to both the right and the left.

In the case of President Noboa, he seems to be vulnerable, given that as the incumbent and front-runner, he will be the target of attacks from both sides, which could erode his support. His challenge will be to try to polarize the race as Correismo versus anti-Correismo to try to benefit from the strong anti-Correista sentiment that still exists and, in that way, consolidate that as his core base of support.

In the centre-right, the candidacy of Jan Topic is a new and potentially meaningful challenge for Noboa. Topic, who had dropped out of the race in May, announced his decision to re-enter the race on Saturday, August 17.<sup>62</sup>  Meanwhile, the Social Christian Party (PSC) announced National Assembly President Henry Kronfle as its candidate. Construye is nominating former Minister of

Government Henry Cucalon. While Kronfle and Cucalon so far do not show up as having meaningful levels of support, Topic could be diferent, given his platform is based on security, which so far has also been Noboa's focus and remains the electorate's top priority.

How far Topic could go is still unclear. Considering he got 14% of support in last year's presidential election, he at least seems to have potential to draw suficient votes away from Noboa to prevent the current president from reaching the 40% threshold needed for a firstround victory.

Nonetheless, Topic also faces challenges. Unlike last time, when he ran under the wellstructured PSC, this time he is running under SUMA, which is relatively small. Therefore, he needs to build a grass-roots organization to increase his competitiveness. If he becomes competitive, Topic could still be seen as a market-friendly option, but in the meantime, his candidacy fosters uncertainty that could potentially weaken Noboa's re-election bid.

Noboa's biggest advantage remains the weakness of his main adversary: Correismo. The party has again picked Luisa Gonzalez as its nominee, persisting with a strategy that appears to favor loyalty to the party leader (former President Rafael Correa) over electability, which has led to defeat in the previous two presidential elections<sup>63</sup> . Correismo keeps sidelining other, more pragmatic leaders within the party who could be more appealing to the median voter. This strategy has allowed Correismo to retain a core base of support that is enough to ensure a spot in the run-of, but it has thus far failed to reach a majority support in the second round. It keeps Correismo anchored in the image of the former president and the remembrance of his years in power, which has generated polarization and is a reality that is distant for young voters. This could still facilitate a re-election of Noboa in a potential rematch of last year's run-of.

Correismo's inability to refresh its leadership has triggered divisions in the centre-left as well. Gonzalez will face competition from Carlos Rabascall, who was Correismo's vice-presidential nominee in 2021 and is now running for president under Democratic Left. He is a charismatic leader with a more centrist agenda. Leonidas Iza from the indigenous movement, which did not have a candidate in last year's election, will also run.

The economic measures adopted by the Noboa administration earlier this year are yielding results and helping to contain short-term risks, but their sustainability may depend on the election outcome. The fiscal deficit in the first five months of the year narrowed by c.1% of GDP as a result of the increase in tax collections of nearly USD900mn; we estimate it will fall from 5.0% of GDP last year to 3.3% in 2024. Considering that the increase in the VAT entered into efect in April, tax-collection growth in the rest of the year is likely to be bigger. GDP growth also surprised to the upside in Q1 at 3.5% q/q and 1.1% y/y, which seems likely to prevent a recession. Last but not least, the increase in gasoline prices that was announced in June should support oil revenues in H2 24.

The government seems to be on track to comply with the first revision of the IMF program and get the next disbursement, due in November. So far, disbursements from multilaterals have boosted international reserves, helping to reduce external vulnerabilities. Supportive oil prices and a strengthening of the USD relative to currencies of neighboring countries, such as the COP, have boosted exports and reduced imports, leading to an increase in the current account surplus. Additionally, authorities are now suggesting that the closing of the ITT oilfield be conducted gradually, over three to five years, which could help prevent a reversal of these trends<sup>64</sup> .

A potential Noboa re-election likely would support the continuity of the reform agenda. Although the fragmented and polarized political system makes governability a structural problem, if Noboa is able to sustain a level of support similar to what he currently has in the polls, he could still nearly double what he received in the first round last year.

Such a result would bode well for his party, potentially leading to an increase in the number of seats in the assembly. That could place the administration in a better position to build alliances and pass reforms that could bring the government closer to market access, reducing the rollover risk of liabilities that will start to come due in 2026.

Even if implementation fails and a new debt restructuring is required, macroeconomic conditions suggest that with a market-friendly administration in place, recovery values could still be relatively high. Therefore, the main medium-term risk remains the possibility of a market-unfriendly government being elected, which could make regaining market access more dificult. The risk of such an administration taking a more opportunistic approach in a potential new restructuring is also significant. That, in our view, still makes the election a relatively binary event.

While political uncertainty could remain high in the short term, the possibility a debt-for-nature transaction could at least help to balance the risks<sup>65</sup> . The timing and conditions for any potential transaction remain unknown, but taking as a reference the precedent of the Galapagos transaction conducted last year, it could well be supportive. Determining the timing of this type of transaction is challenging, as it usually does not depend only on the authorities of the country. It involves donors and multilateral organizations and often takes time due to the need for studies to determine the environmental and social impact of the project.

Nonetheless, the window of opportunity to do such a transaction seems to be October-November this year, when the authorities could potentially take advantage of market weakness due to electoral uncertainty. Later than that would be dificult, due to the proximity of the elections, which could afect the administration's execution capacity. Earlier also seems dificult, given the work necessary to complete the transaction.

## Mexico: Yearning for transition

Gabriel Casillas BCI, US  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US  |  Erick Martinez BCI, US

• Credit: Remain Underweight. The Mexico elections injected volatility, and the lingering potential reforms pushed by President AMLO will keep investors on their toes, but we think that credit rating downgrades would be contingent on fiscal actions and economic growth - the latter our main concern - rather than institutional aspects associated with the reforms. The US elections could bring immigration and tarifs back to the fore, potentially adding spread volatility. In terms of upside risks, given current valuations, we think that President-Elect Claudia Sheinbaum's policies do not present a risk to Mexico's fiscal trajectory, and timely actions to put fiscal accounts on a positive track represent potential upside risk to our view. That said, we think markets will undergo more spread volatility before Sheinbaum’s team is fully in charge and a fiscal plan is put in motion.

• FX:  We do not expect AMLO's reforms to have a persistent efect on the MXN, as the judicial reform is advancing but with some dilution, the market is already pricing in a very negative reform outcome, and institutional change is a slow burn. However, the MXN might not be out of the woods just yet. The carry factor is unlikely to dominate in an environment with higher volatility and broadly falling rates. Moreover, trading the US slowdown will inevitably spill over to Mexico, given the strong fundamental links between the two economies. We think these factors will remain predominant drivers of the MXN, which we expect to underperform, especially as an uncertain US election approaches.

• Rates: Banxico resumed its easing cycle in August after a false start in March. The Fed constraint is loosening, and real rates at very restrictive levels imply ample space to ease. FX pressures are not likely to derail the cycle, in our view, as the bank estimates a moderate pass-through and it could opt for intervention if the currency takes another hit from external drivers. We think markets are underpricing risks of a faster cycle and recommend receiving 2y TIIE. The fiscal impact of AMLO’s reforms in their current state is a question mark, but our economists see space for a 2pp consolidation in 2025. A higher term premia associated with fiscal uncertainty and weaker institutions should be more evident in the back end of the curve.

A tough September… There is no doubt the MXN and Mexican local markets are undergoing a quite volatile September given president López-Obrador's reform agenda approval process, taking advantage of the newly elected legislators with a constitutional majority. On top of this, the US Republican Party presidential candidate, Donald Trump, is likely to an anti-Mexico rhetoric in his bid for president. Fortunately, we believe that in October, this may begin to change with the inauguration of president-elect Claudia Sheinbaum on October 1 (Mexico: Oktoberfest) and after the election in the United States on November 5.

… but not as tough as we thought some months ago. While Mexico is exposed to these two elections, we consider that there are three things that have changed, which may make September less volatile than we previously thought: 1) imminent US Fed rate cuts (US Outlook: Hang gliding from the Tetons); 2) a more competitive election in the US, which in our view, could make market participants less sensitive to the likelihood of Trump's anti-Mexico comments; and 3) some mild dilution of AMLO’s reforms. In the particular case of the judiciary reform, the reform proposal was recently modified to incorporate suitability exams, and staggered elections of judges, magistrates and ministers, instead of a single election for all members of the Judiciary (FX Insights: AMLO's reforms: Not a paradigm changer for the MXN).

All reforms are likely to be approved… Our expectation is that all 18 of AMLO's reforms will be approved (Figure 38), plus Sheinbaum's additional three reforms. These reforms are not fiscally burdensome, as they might seem at first sight (Mexico: Doubling down on the judiciary reform). However, they do weaken Mexico's institutional framework and, in our view, could have a similar efect as the pre-1990 reforms that kept the former hegemonic party PRI in power for 72 uninterrupted years.

… probably boosting volatility in local markets. The approval process of 18 of the 20 reforms that President López Obrador sent to Congress on February 5 could generate a lot of volatility in local markets. On the one hand, except for the minimum wage reform, none of the other reforms are positive for the country, in our view, either economically or institutionally. On the other hand, the impact of the reforms could be so negative that a large number of participants in international financial markets likely think the reforms will be stuck in a legislative debate or will be diluted. We believe that most of the reforms can be passed without many changes, so there is ample room for disappointment. Despite recent comments from President López Obrador about his willingness to make changes to the judicial reform, we believe that his intention is for it to be approved as sent.

## Oktoberfest

Focus on October 1… We continue to be optimistic about president-elect Claudia Sheinbaum, who will be sworn in on October 1. As this reform agenda does not mean fiscal deterioration per se, and the power will now be concentrated in the hands of someone we believe will be a good president, we are optimistic about it, starting on October 1. Hence we call it "Oktoberfest", as Mexico will have a festival of optimism, after the dire week we are experiencing. In our view, market participants trust that President-elect Sheinbaum will be fiscally responsible, and respectful of central bank autonomy.

Moreover, we think market participants see Sheinbaum's administration as more open to private-sector investment, particularly in energy, which we think would be an important step if she really wants to pursue an energy transition. Sheinbaum's decision-making process is also perceived to be based upon cost-benefit analyses, rather than on "gut feeling". Another important aspect is Sheinbaum's quite diferent approach to security vis-à-vis AMLO. As Mayor of Mexico City, Sheinbaum did not implement the "hugs, not bullets" soft approach to organized crime. She was a hardliner, fighting cartels with her Secretary of Security, Omar García-Harfuch.

… and FY25 fiscal consolidation. Market focus continues to be on next year's budget proposal, particularly the fiscal consolidation efort and how much will be assigned to Pemex. Theoretically, the government should not include a Pemex budget line in the FY25 budget proposal, as the oil company should have enough money to repay its debt maturities in 2025 with the funds the government gave this year. Nevertheless, its earnings show that in Q2 24, the company has been burning cash at a faster pace, so the government will likely have to provide the company with more money in 2025. As a result, we continue to expect at least 2pp of consolidation in FY25, to end with a deficit of 3.5-4.0% (Figure 39). This should be relatively easy to achieve with several one-of low-hanging fruit expenses such as AMLO's infrastructure projects (1pp), presidential elections and the economic census (0.5pp), spending transfers from FY23 to FY24 (0.3pp) and Pemex (0.8pp), as shown in Figure 40.
