Economic slowdown should not preclude fiscal consolidation. An important challenge for the new administration will be the economic slowdown, but we think this will not keep Sheinbaum's team from adjusting fiscal policy in FY25. Due to the election year, the deadline to submit changes to the budget proposal is November 15, instead of the usual September 8. However, there might be an earlier budget proposal around mid-October, as part of Sheinbaum's first set of positive signals to market participants (Latin America Outlook: Fiscal plans versus reality).

## Economy slowing down, but near-shoring holding

Disappointing first half of the year. Economic activity in Mexico has been disappointing throughout 2024 (Latin America Outlook: Growth disappointments). At the beginning of the year, we expected a positive growth outlook. From the external side, the US economy was growing at a fast pace, and manufacturing seemed to be recovering, at least judging by the global manufacturing PMI. Moreover, we continued to project an important tailwind from nearshoring-related investments. On the domestic side, we expected an additional boost from the significant increase in government spending that would likely elevate the fiscal deficit to nearly 6.0% of GDP this year. At the beginning of the year, we projected growth of 3.0% for 2024, along the same lines as Banxico's staf (consensus: 2.4%; see Latin America Outlook: A stronger-thanexpected recovery). Nevertheless, the economy posted quarterly growth rates of only 0.1% in Q1 and 0.2% in Q2, registering 1.8% y/y growth in H1 24.

Weak manufacturing, less-than-expected fiscal impact, and stagnant remittances. We believe the Mexican economy is going to grow 1.7% in 2024 (consensus: 1.6%). While we had expected a slowdown in workers' remittances and tourism flows from the US, manufacturing has been weak throughout the year, owing to the US slowdown. Government spending has not had a material efect on the economy, either. Moreover, agricultural production has been severely afected by El Niño, whose efects have exacerbated by climate change. Moreover, during election years, the economy usually decelerates in the second half, as ministries at the federal level and state governments prepare the transition, slowing budget execution. Furthermore, the first year of a new administration also typically sees slow growth. However, this time might slow further given the fiscal consolidation that the new administration will likely commit to (2025 GDP: 1.4%; consensus: 1.5%). In the end, we believe that the main force driving growth this year has been near-shoring, despite some investor skepticism and Tesla's recent announcement that its plans to invest in the factory in Northern Mexico are frozen. While quite emblematic, it is not the only near-shoring-related investment.

Near-shoring is happening, despite AMLO's reform agenda. AMLO's reforms move in the opposite direction to measures to attract capital. Nevertheless, Mexico's institutional bar is quite low. As a result, the local and global business community that knows how Mexico works should continue to operate regardless of the aforementioned reforms. We know near-shoring is happening. The question is whether it is having an efect in Mexico, especially given apparently low FDI vis-à-vis expectations. In our view, the main issue is that the way companies want to reallocate their lines of production has changed.

The near-shoring model has changed. Firms looking to near-shore in Mexico or other locations, no longer want to get into the hassle of building a factory from scratch. They now prefer to find a safe place that solves their water, electricity, and connectivity infrastructure issues, in order to "plug and play." Industrial parks ofer these spaces. As a result, there has been a material increase in non-residential construction since 2022, as industrial park developers have been building such places for foreign companies to relocate their lines of production to Mexico. These investments do not show in FDI, as industrial park developers are locally established companies.

We will see results in the balance of payments, but not much in FDI. At some point we will observe an important change in the behavior of export of services once companies start to pay rents, and eventually in manufacturing exports. But more importantly, in our view, from a medium-term perspective, is the significant increase in imports of capital goods. This is in line with our optimistic view about the efect of near-shoring in Mexico (Mexico: Near-shored up). Capital goods imports posted year-to-date growth of 10.5% in 2024 (July), vs. 23.2% during the same period in 2023.

## Banxico now in a continuing easing cycle

Two 25bp rate cuts so far… Banco de México cut the reference rate 25bp for the first time since the easing cycle began in March. Nevertheless, the board decided to skip June meeting, but decided to deliver another 25bp rate cut in August. In our view, this responded to the board's concerns about USDMXN dynamics, particularly around the presidential election in June 2, as well as a more volatile non-core inflation component. While the exchange rate has remained volatile, particularly given AMLO's push for his reform agenda, the depreciation of the peso has not been disorderly, and could eventually trade higher once the reform risks have dissipated in October and the Fed starts to cut rates. Moreover, the pressure from agricultural prices on noncore CPI has eased.

… but now ready to embrace a continuing cutting cycle. It is also worth noting that the exante real rate is back to 7.00%, as it was before both rate cuts (Figure 41), as 12-month ahead inflation expectations have continued to decline. In its Quarterly Report, Banxico underscored the downside risks to economic activity, which among other things, so far have ofset the depreciation of the currency (Mexico: Banxico Quarterly Report - An even gloomier outlook). As a result, we believe Banxico will embrace a consecutive rate cutting cycle, with 25bp (YE24: 10.00%) and a step up to 50bp by mid-2025, to take the reference rate to 7.50% by year-end 2025, despite the latest FX volatility (Figure 42). However, we recognize that the balance of risks to our call is leaning towards an earlier acceleration in the pace of cuts in 2025.

## Panama: Busy policy agenda

Nestor Rodriguez BBMX, Mexico  |  Jason Keene BCI, US

The new administration is working against the clock to deliver on expected fiscal medium-term strategy and pension reform before year-end. Although it has shown a willingness to pursue expenditure containment, questions remain on how lower deficits will be achieved without meaningful tax reform. Authorities plan to rely on domestic debt to satisfy its financing needs for the remainder of the year; however, this might depend on the capacity of the depth of domestic market, which is unknown at this point in time. Potentially reopening the copper mine Minera Panama remains part of next year's pipeline.

• Credit: We recently downgraded Panama to Underweight days before President Mulino assumed ofice. Fiscal performance through the first half of the year has continued to disappoint, and Finance Minister Chapman has publicly acknowledged that the 2024 deficit will be at least 4% of GDP (2% wider than the level established by the fiscal rule). Nevertheless, bonds have performed well, driven by positive signals from the new administration and a commitment to avoid Eurobond issuance in 2024. However, to ensure suficient absorption capacity, Panama may need to ofer more attractive interest rate diferentials to incentivize switching from foreigners. We therefore recommend selling PANAMA 3.875% 2028s, PANAMA 3.16% 2030s, and PANAMA 7.5% 2031s, which may come under the most pressure from investors rotating into local-law paper.

The economy has navigated the mine shock better than expected. According to the monthly index, economic activity has grown 2.1% YTD, with an acceleration in Q2 with growth of 2.9% y/y, on average, up from 1.4% in Q1. Despite the closure of the copper mine, and the overall suspension of mining activity, the economy has relied on transportation recovery, construction and manufacturing.

We now forecast GDP to grow by 3.0% this year, up from 2.2% previously, and to grow by 4.2% in 2025. This revision is driven by the more resilient activity in H1 24 and the fact that we expect the growth rate to accelerate in H2, supported by a recovery in Canal activity, which since August is already back to its usual capacity with 36 transits per day, and base efects in Q4, as a year ago the economy was afected by protests and the initial closure of the mine. Growth into 2025 would be capped by the expected deceleration of the US economy, fiscal consolidation plans and great uncertainty about the potential reopening of the copper mine, which we do not include in our projections. We estimate 2025 growth at 4.2%, slightly lower than our previous forecast of 4.5%.

We recently upgraded our fiscal deficit to 4.8% of GDP for this year, with public debt around 61.5% of GDP. As we wrote in Panama: Looking below the line, revenues were markedly weaker than expected in the second quarter of the year and are expected to remain depressed amid an absence of one-ofs, as was the case last year. Although we expect the current administration to contain public spending, it will be dificult to quickly reverse the trend amid pressure from current expenditures, including interest payments. This, coupled with recognition and settlement of arrears, would lead to public debt to GDP rising to 61.5% at year-end, up from 56% last year.

domestic market. In our report, we highlighted that authorities intend to avoid tapping international markets this year and to close their financing needs through domestic issuance and potential financing structures with commercial banks. However, as we estimate a financing gap of about \$1.6bn, following the September 3 issuance of 1-year treasury letters of \$60.25mn at 6.40% yield, questions remain if there is suficient market depth to finance such a gap and

how much the authorities would need to rely on non-traditional financing from commercial banks.

FIGURE 44. GDP growth should start to accelerate in the coming quarters
![](images/5e1676c2dc10065ad8ba4d15f0800d8779283aea692b56dfc0954bd004dcde91.jpg)
Source: INEC, Barclays Research

FIGURE 45. Wider deficit this year will push up public debt levels
![](images/3dff0ce4e1d3e175dba5c040c7f8fa7f9085f19754267cd17eb557b8dd2b61a1.jpg)
Source: Ministry of Finance, Barclays Research

We expect rating agencies to acknowledge Panama's policy challenges and recent fiscal deterioration but to provide time for its remediation. Panama's current rating profile has S&P at BBB with a negative outlook, Moody's at BBB- with a stable outlook, with just Fitch already at BB+. We do not discard potential negative actions from Moody's, such as putting the current rating on negative outlook, and S&P, downgrading by one notch, acknowledging the fiscal deterioration in the past year. Nonetheless, decisions on actual moves toward high yield might only come in the second half of 2025, awaiting more clarity on growth, which seems to remain a credit strength in Panama, and an upcoming busy policy agenda:

• Fiscal plans: We expect the government to update the fiscal position with a smoother path towards a deficit of 1.5% of GDP by 2027, instead of as soon as next year, similar to the IMF's recommendation at its 2024 Article IV consultation. Although there is not a specific timeline for this, we would expect to have more clarity along with the 2025 budget, which should be submitted for the Cabinet's approval at some point in September. The devil will be in the details to understand how they will plan to reduce the fiscal deficit amid the potential absence of a deep tax reform, as indicated by authorities<sup>66</sup> .

• Pensions: Mulino's administration has been very active, meeting continually with other sectors on potential solutions to the financing of the defined benefits sub-system—a deficit that could initially represent around \$1.1bn (1.3% of GDP)—and overall pension system sustainability. The initial plan is to have a proposal ready for Cabinet approval at some point in October, followed by discussion at an  extraordinary session of the legislature before yearend<sup>67</sup> .

• Mining: In past months, Mulino was vocal on his plan to only address the mining situation after pension reform is solved. Hence, we might only see potential proposals around the idea of "opening the mine to close it" in Q1 25. This is also in line with plans to first conduct an environmental audit on the  copper mine that could take between two to six months.<sup>68</sup> The

audit would determine the current condition of the mine and potential urgency to deal with the copper ore stored at it.

Nonetheless, we think that full legal certainty for mine to operate—either to run it commercially or to simply "open to close"— could only materialize by H2 2025. As we wrote in Panama & First Quantum: The future of mining in Panama, the new proposal would need to complete legislative approval of a new contract and repeal of mining moratorium law, and potentially the need to update current mining code in the country. Also it would be preferable to proceed with a public consultation, where we do not discard a public referendum.

Politic dynamics are supportive but not guaranteed. We maintain our view that Mulino's administration has paths to reach consensus in Congress. This would be the case as long as he is able to maintain equilibrium between 'Martinelistas' to secure support from PRD and his own party, and 'anti-Martinelistas' from other parties and independents. But even then, blind support is not guaranteed. This was shown recently after Congress rejected the Ministry of Finance proposal to increase the limit to transfer budget line items without congressional approval to USD5 million. In the end, the legislature approved an increase to just USD500,000 with 52 votes in favor, out of 71, in its third debate.

The main immediate test for the administration is to avoid confrontation and street protests. The outlined policy plan above has the risk of being tested by potential street protests. Potential changes to subsidies for certain sectors, parametric changes as part of a pension reform or environmental and corruption concerns around any new mining situation could disturb social peace. For instance, some groups have already started to express rejection of any parametric changes on the pension reform<sup>69</sup> . In that case, Congressional approval could be challenging and end up watering down any proposal, failing to provide sustainability to the system.

## Peru: Unrealistic targets

## Alejandro Arreaza BCI, US  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US  |  Erick Martinez BCI, US

Even if the fiscal deficit target has been revised higher, we believe that it would still be an unobtainable target for the authorities to meet. Missing the target for a second consecutive year is likely to lead to a new downgrade of the sovereign rating in the coming months, in our view. Additionally, a larger deficit, coupled with the erosion of the domestic funding capacity, is making the government more reliant on external  financing.

• Credit: Remain Market Weight. Peru spreads have slightly underperformed EM IG peers, reflecting a gradually weakening fundamental picture, and importantly, compounded by higher-than-expected supply in 2024. However, prevailing strong technicals have remained a mitigating force in cash markets. The fiscal pressures we envisaged have materialized but terms of trade and growth remain a tailwind. The political situation remains unstable, and as we approach 2025, we see politics playing a greater role in driving spreads and spread volatility. As a result, we are not constructive on Peru sovereign, and we believe the recent widening pressure on Peru CDS is driven by the Petroperu situation. However, we think that the consequences for the Peru sovereign are negligible, and therefore, we see the recent spread widening as excessive, and recommend buying Chile 5y CDS and selling Peru 5y CDS.

• FX: We expect the low-yielding, low-vol PEN to hold in relatively well in the challenging global environment, supported by the BCRP's active policy in the FX market. The BCRP resumed easing in August, but we do not see this as too destabilizing for the PEN with core rates also moving lower. The PEN has not reacted much to lower copper prices, as the weaker commodity price backdrop has ofset the negative impact on Peru’s terms of trade, but the currency could sufer if the drag from China extends. The political outlook remains fragile, but we do not see this as a likely trigger for FX moves just yet, with elections due in 2026.

• Rates: The BCRP eased in August after a two-month pause, as inflation is at target and real rates remain high. Furthermore, the economic recovery is losing momentum with fading base efects due to the reversal of El Niño, while political uncertainty is already weighing on construction and investment. Our economists project 150bp of additional easing to reach a terminal of 4%. We expect Soberanos to gain support from lower risk-neutral rates with the BCRP and the Fed back in easing mode.

## Staying on a downward trajectory

The Peruvian authorities recently presented their multi-annual fiscal framework, making some revisions to the targets but remaining overly optimistic, in our view. Although relative to the regional standards the deficit could be seen as moderate, a large deviation from the oficial targets for a second consecutive year would undermine credibility and create concerns about the medium- and longer-term outlook. A downgrade of the sovereign rating in the coming months thus seems increasingly likely.

The previous report included a deficit target of 2.0% of GDP for this year and 1.5% for 2025, which has since been revised to 2.8%  and 2.2%, respectively; however, as of July, the 12-month rolling deficit exceeded 4% of GDP, suggesting that it could even larger than our 3.7% forecast for year-end. Tax collection has failed to recover, and the government has been increasing expenditures at double-digit rates to try to boost growth and preserve governability.

There are additional potential sources of fiscal pressures, including the recently passed pension reform, as well as the crisis facing the national oil company, Petroperu, which needs a government bailout in order to meet its obligations. Authorities have discarded a privatization of the company as proposed by its board and the minister of finance has expressed discomfort with the possibility of a re-capitalization, which would risk further widening the fiscal deficit. Nonetheless, the government could at least grant some guarantees to Petroperu to help it raise financing, which would be a contingent liability for the government.

The larger deficit, coupled  with the erosion of the domestic funding capacity due to recurrent pension funds withdrawals, leaves the government more reliant on external funding, which in turn makes debt dynamics more sensitive to potential fluctuations in the exchange rate. In principle, the authorities financing plan contemplated zero external market debt issuance and, instead focused the coverage of its funding needs nn the local market, looking to reduce the proportion of external debt. We did not consider that strategy sustainable and expected the authorities to issue at least USD1bn this year. However, the government has exceeded our expectations and issued USD3bn in the external market. In absence of corrective measures, this trend is likely to continue.

Fiscal stimuli, particularly through a real increase in public investment of 28.8% y/y in H1 24, have contributed to a rebound in economic activity and lead us to increase the our GDP growth forecast for this year to 2.4% from 1.9%, previously. In aggregate, GDP expanded by 3.6% in Q2 24, although the sustainability of this expansion is unclear. This seems to be largely the result of base efects due to the reversal of the efects of El Niño and other weather phenomena that afected economic activity during Q2 23 (eg, the fishing sector grew by 184% y/y in Q2 24). However, private investment grew by just 1.4% and June GDP growth was a mere 0.2%, well below the consensus expectation of 1.8%.

The increase in copper and gold prices has translated into a strong improvement in terms of trade and are a tailwind, which contributed to a current account surplus throughout H1 24, which could lead to a better trade balance this year than previously expected. What is worrisome is that, unlike in the past, when this type of positive terms of trade shock was reinvested onshore to support increases in exports, this has not occurred this time. FDI in the country in Q2 24 was actually negative and YTD is showing a 12% y/y decline. The persistence of political instability seems to be undermining investment, which is likely to continue to constrain the capacity of the economy to grow and could imply a greater vulnerability to a potential reversal of the terms of trade shock.

The sovereign credit rating appears set to stay on a downward trajectory, in our view. S&P already downgraded the sovereign to BBB- earlier this year. Fitch (currently BBB) and Moody’s (currently Baa1) are both on negative outlook and given the current state of the economy, it is likely that both agencies could follow S&P's lead. Their outlook after a potential downgrade will be key. Similar to S&P, they could stabilize it, which could contain the risk of falling to high yield in the near future. However, in the absence of corrective measures, Peru’s investment grade status could be under scrutiny in the coming years. Relative to similar-rated peers, Peru has a clear disadvantage from an institutional point of view; therefore, it does not require a large deterioration in the economic fundamentals to add further pressures to the rating, in our view.

The good news is that inflation, in the absence of demand pressures, has already converged to the center of the target (2.0% +/-1pp). Lower-than-expected inflation and a more likely beginning of Fed rate cuts this month has allowed the BCRP to surprise the market by  cutting the policy rate by 25bp earlier than expected to 5.5%. However, base efects will become a headwind in the coming months, potentially leading to a rebound in inflation from now through to year end, which warrants some caution. However, we expect two more 25bp cuts this year and a total of 150bp in additional rate cuts to reach a terminal rate of 4.0% by the middle of next year.

## Uruguay: Ripples in calm waters

Ivan Stambulsky Barclays, UK  |  Sebastian Vargas BCI, US

Uruguay has avoided the swings between extremes that we commonly see in Latin America, and we think the upcoming elections will be no exception. The fiscal framework has improved with the rule, and an important reform stabilizing pension spending in the long run was passed in Congress last year. Meanwhile, sovereign spreads are at record lows. However, the outlook presents risks, as polls<sup>70</sup> suggest the center-left coalition (likely less concerned with fiscal consolidation) is competitive, and the referendum to overturn the pension reform is live. Also, Uruguay is set to deviate from the fiscal rule again this year, showing some rigidity to accommodate forecast mistakes.

• Credit: Remain Overweight. Uruguay valuations have compressed to IG peers, given stable credit metrics, which remain and extremely supportive curve's technicals. While there are headline risks ahead, we think that the strong starting level of fundamentals and technicals will lead to EM-dedicated buying upon any weakness.

Uruguay chooses a president this year. The first round of the presidential election will take place on October 27. If no candidate gets more than 50% of the votes, there will be a second round on November 24. The two most competitive candidates emerging from the June primaries are Yamandú Orsi, from the center-left coalition Frente Amplio, and Alvaro Delgado, from the National Party, which integrates the incumbent center-right Coalicion Republicana. Both candidates could be described as moderates (especially relevant in the case of the Frente Amplio, where the more radical Carolina Cosse was defeated in the primaries). The transition should not bring the kind of turbulences we have seen in neighboring countries like Argentina if either of these candidates is elected. That said, a Frente Amplio victory would likely displace fiscal consolidation eforts and structural reforms in the government's priority list.

Polls point to a second round. For the Frente Amplio, most polls show a stable trajectory between 42% and 44% in the first round since the beginning of this year. The incumbent Coalicion will not present just one candidate: the Colorado party, Cabildo Abierto and Partido Independiente will each have their own. Delgado has the lead among the Coalicion at 20-27%, but most polls show a deterioration starting in April, while Andres Ojeda from the Colorado party has gained some traction and is polling at a lower 9-15%. Ojeda is a somewhat more disruptive figure, as he did not take part in the Lacalle administration,<sup>71</sup> but his rhetoric is still moderate and he describes himself as "strongly complementary" with Delgado. Aggregated votes for Coalicion range from 37% to 49%, depending on the poll.

The referendum on pension reform in October presents another important choice. In May 2023, the Uruguayan Congress passed a pension reform project that increased the retirement age from 60 to 65 and introduced other changes to reduce pension spending in the long term. This reform substantially improved the long-term outlook for public finances in Uruguay by stabilizing prospective pension spending as a share of GDP. The country's current pension spending is above 12% of GDP, as high as the EU average, but with a younger population, and with no pension reform pending it was projected to increase by about 3% of GDP by 2070. But in July this year, the labor union federation managed to introduce a referendum to cap the retirement age at 60 in the Constitution. This would efectively overturn the reform if citizens support it. The referendum will take place alongside the presidential election on October 27. In a recent survey by Opcion Consultores, 41% reported they would vote in favor of the age cap (ie, against pension reform), while 38% said they would vote against it.

There is a chance that Uruguay elects a less fiscally committed center-left candidate and overturns pension reform this year. Uruguay is characterized by stable politics and gradual economic agendas. We do not expect this time to be an exception, but the 2024 elections could bring two relevant changes in a more uncertain direction for macroeconomic management. Although the two main candidates have made comments against the referendum initiative, polls point to an open scenario around a key macroeconomic achievement. That is not something to overlook in a context of historically low sovereign spreads.

Activity is picking up as the efects of the drought revert, but we expect modest growth next year. Uruguay was hit by a severe drought in 2023, and GDP growth slowed from 4.7% in 2022 to 0.4% in 2023. The economy is currently growing at a stronger pace as the efects of the drought reverse. Growth remained slow in Q1 24, with investment contracting at a double-digit pace, but the monthly GDP proxy points to 3.2% growth in Q2, and we expect this pick up in the reminder of the year and forecast growth in 2024 at 3.1%. However, in the absence of strong one-of drivers for 2025, we forecast modest expansion of 2.5% next year, underscoring Uruguay's structural competitiveness challenges.

We expect inflation to re-accelerate on economic recovery and a weaker UYU. Inflation fell from 8.3% in 2022 to 5.1% in 2023, helped by the unwinding of global supply shocks, a wider output gap and a strong Uruguayan peso (UYU). However, as these sources lose steam, inflation is re-accelerating. After hitting a 19-year low of 3.7% y/y in April 2024, inflation rebounded to 5.4% in July. We expect this trend to continue, as inertia remains strong and the sources of last year's disinflation continue to weaken. We forecast inflation at 6.0% at end-2024 and 5.9% next year, both at the upper bound of the BCU's target range.

The fiscal rule is at risk again this year. Last year, the government was forced to invoke the exception clause for the fiscal rule, due mainly to the efect of the drought on government revenues. The deviation from the target was not substantial, with the deficit excluding cincuentones at -3.3% of GDP, compared with the initial projection of -2.6%.  However, another deviation from the fiscal rule is expected for this year. In its latest report, the Fiscal Council (an independent technical government unit in charge of monitoring the fiscal rule) revised its forecast for the structural fiscal deficit to -3.4%, above the -2.9% ceiling. Also, real primary spending was projected at +4.8%, above the +2.8% ceiling. Although we expect government to remain contained in the medium run, this new likely deviation from the target suggests that fiscal management presents some rigidities.

## Venezuela: Back in a stalemate

## Alejandro Arreaza, BCI, US | Jason Keene, BCI, US

The escalation of repression by the government after the July 28 elections has pushed the opposition into a tactical retreat, including the exile of former opposition presidential candidate Edmundo Gonzalez. However, we do not believe this should be viewed as a capitulation by the opposition. This brings the country back into a complex stalemate, which reinforces our view that the outlook is highly binary. This results in an unstable equilibrium in which Maduro is more vulnerable, but keeps any possibility of an meaningful economic recovery and a debt restructuring dependent on the re-institutionalization of the country.

• Credit: Over the last two months, VENZ and PDVSA unsecured bonds are c.4-5pt (or 25-30%) lower. Using price assumptions laid out earlier this summer (ie, an upside case of \$35 and a downside case of \$12 on the VENZ curve), the market-implied likelihood of a transition have fallen to approximately 10%.

## In search of the tricky mix of carrots and sticks that could break the regime

Venezuela is back in a stalemate. The opposition has been unable to achieve a transition since the July 28 elections, and Maduro has been unable to secure the legitimacy and recognition he was seeking. In this context, the announcement on Saturday, September 7, that former opposition presidential candidate Edmundo Gonzalez has been forced to leave the country and seek political asylum in Spain is evidence of the obstacles the opposition still faces in achieving its objective<sup>72</sup> . However, it also shows the increasingly authoritarian character of Maduro's government, which made a series of miscalculations ahead of the elections (see Venezuela: The Calculus mistake). It was surprised by a well-organized opposition and now faces allegations of electoral fraud and has been forced to escalate repressive measures to hold onto power<sup>73</sup> . In our view, this has created a legitimacy crisis for Maduro and is pushing the regime further into isolation.

This has resulted in an unstable equilibrium that seems an unsatisfactory outcome for most stakeholders, who risk seeing the situation deteriorating further. Even if Gonzalez’s departure could be a short-term victory for Maduro in helping him cling to power, capitulating and accepting the consolidation of the regime does not appear to be an option that the opposition leadership – and the international community that has been supporting it – seem willing to accept, at least in the near term.

Maria Corina Machado, the main opposition leader who transferred her support to Gonzalez after she was banned from running in the elections, remains in the country and reiterated her commitment to continue leading the opposition movement, while Gonzalez now seems likely to undertake a more active role on the international front. The US, the EU and most LatAm countries have strongly maintained that Gonzalez received the majority of the votes for president and that Maduro should not be recognized. Even after Gonzalez’s exile, they have reiterated their commitment to continue to defend democracy and support the Venezuelan opposition<sup>74</sup> <sup>75</sup> .

In order to resolve the protracted crisis, a transition toward a re-institutionalization of the country remains necessary, which reinforces our view that the country’s outlook is binary. Maduro remaining in power as a de facto president signals a distancing from the roadmap<sup>76</sup>  set by the international community to consider any potential sanctions relief. This could cap the possibility of meaningful economic recovery and block a restructuring of both the sovereign and PDVSA's debt.

Given the resistance Maduro has shown so far to cede power, breaking this stalemate would seem to require finding a mix of carrots and sticks to encourage the regime to fracture – or at least create a significant risk of it happening – and generate incentives for members of the regime to be open to a negotiated solution. However, in our view, any agreement now seems to hinge on Maduro ceding power. While until now coercion and repression has helped Maduro retain support at the top of the regime and of the military, the situation is less clear among the middle and lower ranks. Moreover, the situation that led earlier this year to the arrest of former Vice-president Tareck El-Aissami and his inner circle for approaching the opposition and looking look for a way out reminds us that appearances can be deceiving<sup>77</sup> .

In that sense, an escalation of domestic and international pressure over the coming months, particularly ahead of the formal beginning of the new presidential term on January 10, 2025, still seems likely and will bring new tests for the cohesion of the regime. Therefore, after Gonzalez’s departure, which is likely to weigh on the opposition’s morale, the remobilization of its base is the key challenge for the opposition leadership.

For now, we think the biggest problem for Maduro is that he is unlikely to be able to deliver on promises he made during the campaign to some of his key constituents, which together with questions about his post-election legitimacy, makes him more vulnerable. His campaign tried to present him as a guarantor of stability and someone who could bring some normalization of the country’s relationships with the international community, including sanctions relief and economic recovery, seemingly in response to demands from factions within his own coalition. Instead of making progress here, those goals seem even further away, in our view. Therefore, Maduro could become a liability for his own allies, which could potentially push them to look for alternatives; however, how long this process might take remains unclear.

D: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate has received compensation for investment banking services from this issuer in the past 12 months.

E: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate expects to receive or intends to seek compensation for investment banking services from this issuer within the next 3 months.

FA: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate beneficially owns 1% or more of a class of equity securities of this issuer, as calculated in accordance with US regulations.

FB: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate beneficially owns a long position of more than 0.5% of a class of equity securities of this issuer, as calculated in accordance with EU regulations.

FC: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate beneficially owns a short position of more than 0.5% of a class of equity securities of this issuer, as calculated in accordance with EU regulations.

FD: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate beneficially owns 1% or more of a class of equity securities of this issuer, as calculated in accordance with South Korean regulations.

FE: Barclays Bank PLC and/or its group companies has financial interests in relation to this issuer and such interests aggregate to an amount equal to or more than 1% of this issuer’s market capitalization, as calculated in accordance with HK regulations.

J: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate is a liquidity provider and/or trades regularly in the securities of this issuer and/or in any related derivatives.

K: Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate has received non-investment banking related compensation (including compensation for brokerage services, if applicable) from this issuer within the past 12 months.

L: This issuer is, or during the past 12 months has been, an investment banking client of Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate.

M: This issuer is, or during the past 12 months has been, a non-investment banking client (securities related services) of Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate.

N: This issuer is, or during the past 12 months has been, a non-investment banking client (non-securities related services) of Barclays Bank PLC and/or an afiliate.

U: The equity securities of this Canadian issuer include subordinate voting restricted shares.

## Explanation of the Barclays Research Corporate Credit Sector Rating System

## Overweight (OW):

For sectors rated against the Bloomberg U.S. Credit Index, the Bloomberg Pan-European Credit Index, the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Grade Credit Index or the Bloomberg EM USD Corporate and Quasi-Sovereign Index, the analyst expects the six-month excess return of the sector to exceed the six month excess return of the relevant index.

For sectors rated against the Bloomberg U.S. High Yield 2% Issuer Capped Credit Index, the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield 3% Issuer Capped Credit Index excluding Financials, the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield Finance Index or the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Yield Corporate Credit Index, the analyst expects the six-month total return of the sector to exceed the six-month total return of the relevant index.

## Market Weight (MW):

For sectors rated against the Bloomberg U.S. Credit Index, the Bloomberg Pan-European Credit Index, the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Grade Credit Index or the Bloomberg EM USD Corporate and Quasi-Sovereign Index, the analyst expects the six-month excess return of the sector to be in line with the six-month excess return of the relevant index.

For sectors rated against the Bloomberg U.S. High Yield 2% Issuer Capped Credit Index, the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield 3% Issuer Capped Credit Index excluding Financials, the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield Finance Index or the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Yield Corporate Credit Index, the analyst expects the six-month total return of the sector to be in line with the six-month total return of the relevant index.

## Underweight (UW):

For sectors rated against the Bloomberg U.S. Credit Index, the Bloomberg Pan-European Credit Index, the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Grade Credit Index or the Bloomberg EM USD Corporate and Quasi-Sovereign Index, the analyst expects the six-month excess return of the sector to be less than the six-month excess return of the relevant index.

For sectors rated against the Bloomberg U.S. High Yield 2% Issuer Capped Credit Index, the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield 3% Issuer Capped Credit Index excluding Financials, the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield Finance Index or the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Yield Corporate Credit Index, the analyst expects the six-month total return of the sector to be less than the six-month total return of the relevant index.

## Sector definitions:

Sectors in U.S. High Grade Research are defined using the sector definitions of the Bloomberg U.S. Credit Index and are rated against the Bloomberg U.S. Credit Index.

Sectors in U.S. High Yield Research are defined using the sector definitions of the Bloomberg U.S. High Yield 2% Issuer Capped Credit Index and are rated against the Bloomberg U.S. High Yield 2% Issuer Capped Credit Index.

Sectors in European High Grade Research are defined using the sector definitions of the Bloomberg Pan-European Credit Index and are rated against the Bloomberg Pan-European Credit Index.

Sectors in Industrials and Utilities in European High Yield Research are defined using the sector definitions of the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield 3% Issuer Capped Credit Index excluding Financials and are rated against the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield 3% Issuer Capped Credit Index excluding Financials.

Sectors in Financials in European High Yield Research are defined using the sector definitions of the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield Finance Index and are rated against the Bloomberg Pan-European High Yield Finance Index.

Sectors in Asia High Grade Research are defined on Barclays Live and are rated against the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Grade Credit Index.

Sectors in Asia High Yield Research are defined on Barclays Live and are rated against the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Yield Corporate Credit Index.

Sectors in EEMEA and Latin America Research are defined on Barclays Live and are rated against the Bloomberg EM USD Corporate and Quasi Sovereign Index. These sectors may contain both High Grade and High Yield issuers.

To view sector definitions and monthly sector returns for Asia, EEMEA and Latin America Research, go to https://live.barcap.com/go/research/ EMSectorReturns on Barclays Live.

For important information about Asia, EEMEA and LatAm Credit Sector definitions, go to https://live.barcap.com/go/publications/link? contentPubID=FC2786307&restriction=DEBT on Barclays Live.

## Explanation of the Barclays Research Corporate Credit Rating System

For all High Grade issuers covered in the US, Europe or Asia, and for all issuers in Latin America and EEMEA, the credit rating system is based on the analyst's view of the expected excess return over a six-month period of the issuer's index-eligible corporate debt securities\* relative to the expected excess return of the relevant sector, as specified on the report.

Overweight (OW): The analyst expects the six-month excess return of the issuer's index-eligible corporate debt securities to exceed the six-month expected excess return of the relevant sector.

Market Weight (MW): The analyst expects the six-month excess return of the issuer's index-eligible corporate debt securities to be in line with the sixmonth expected excess return of the relevant sector.

Underweight (UW): The analyst expects the six-month excess return of the issuer's index-eligible corporate debt securities to be less than the sixmonth expected excess return of the relevant sector.

Rating Suspended (RS): The rating has been suspended temporarily due to market events that make coverage impracticable or to comply with applicable regulations and/or firm policies in certain circumstances including where the Investment Bank of Barclays Bank PLC is acting in an advisory capacity in a merger or strategic transaction involving the company.

Coverage Suspended (CS): Coverage of this issuer has been temporarily suspended.

Not Covered (NC): Barclays’ fundamental credit research team does not provide formal, continuous coverage of this issuer and has not assigned a rating to the issuer or its debt securities. Any analysis, opinion or trade recommendation provided on a Not Covered issuer or its debt securities is valid only as of the publication date of this report and there should be no expectation that additional reports relating to the Not Covered issuer or its debt securities will be published thereafter.

For all High Yield issuers (excluding those covered in EEMEA or Latin America), the credit rating system is based on the analyst's view of the expected total returns over a six-month period of the rated debt security relative to the expected total return of the relevant sector, as specified on the report.

Overweight (OW): The analyst expects the six-month total return of the debt security subject to this rating to exceed the six-month expected total return of the relevant sector.

Market Weight (MW): The analyst expects the six-month total return of the debt security subject to this rating to be in line with the six-month expected total return of the relevant sector.

Underweight (UW): The analyst expects the six-month total return of the rated debt security subject to this rating to be less than the six-month expected total return of the relevant sector.

Rating Suspended (RS): The rating has been suspended temporarily due to market events that make coverage impracticable or to comply with applicable regulations and/or firm policies in certain circumstances including where the Investment Bank of Barclays Bank PLC is acting in an advisory capacity in a merger or strategic transaction involving the company.

Coverage Suspended (CS): Coverage of this issuer has been temporarily suspended.

Not Covered (NC): Barclays’ fundamental credit research team does not provide formal, continuous coverage of this issuer and has not assigned a rating to the issuer or its debt securities. Any analysis, opinion or trade recommendation provided on a Not Covered issuer or its debt securities is valid only as of the publication date of this report and there should be no expectation that additional reports relating to the Not Covered issuer or its debt securities will be published thereafter.

Where a recommendation is made at the issuer level, it does not apply to any sanctioned securities, where trading in such securities would be prohibited under applicable law, including sanctions laws and regulations.

\*In EEMEA and Latin America (and in certain other limited instances in other regions), analysts may occasionally rate issuers that are not part of the Bloomberg U.S. Credit Index, the Bloomberg Pan-European Credit Index, the Bloomberg EM Asia USD High Grade Credit Index or Bloomberg EM USD Corporate and Quasi Sovereign Index. In such cases the rating will reflect the analyst’s view of the expected excess return over a six-month period of the issuer’s corporate debt securities relative to the expected excess return of the relevant sector, as specified on the report.

## Explanation of the Barclays EM Sovereign Credit Issuer Rating System

## Overweight (OW):

The analyst expects the six-month excess return of the country’s index eligible bonds to exceed the six-month excess return of the Bloomberg EM USD Sovereign Index.

## Market Weight (MW):

The analyst expects the six-month excess return of the country’s index eligible bonds to be in line with the six-month excess return of the Bloomberg EM USD Sovereign Index.

## Underweight (UW):

The analyst expects the six-month excess return of the country’s index eligible bonds to be less than the six-month excess return of the Bloomberg EM USD Sovereign Index.

## Rating Suspended (RS):

The rating has been suspended temporarily due to market events that make coverage impracticable or to comply with applicable regulations and/or firm policies in certain circumstances including where the Investment Bank of Barclays Bank PLC is acting in an advisory capacity.

Environmental, Social, and Governance (‘ESG’) Related Research: There is currently no globally accepted framework or definition (legal, regulatory or otherwise) of, nor market consensus as to what constitutes, an ‘ESG’, ‘green’, ‘sustainable’, ‘climate-friendly’ or an equivalent company, investment, strategy or consideration or what precise attributes are required to be eligible to be categorised by such terms. This means there are diferent ways to evaluate a company or an investment and so diferent values may be placed on certain ESG credentials as well as adverse ESG-related impacts of companies and ESG controversies. The evolving nature of ESG considerations, models and methodologies means it can be challenging to definitively and universally classify a company or investment under an ESG label and there may be areas where such companies and investments could improve or where adverse ESG-related impacts or ESG controversies exist. The evolving nature of sustainable finance related regulations and the development of jurisdiction-specific regulatory criteria also means that there is likely to be a degree of divergence as to the interpretation of such terms in the market. We expect industry guidance, market practice, and regulations in this field to continue to evolve. Any references to ‘sustainable’, ‘sustainability’, ‘green’, ‘social’, ‘ESG’, ‘ESG considerations’, ‘ESG factors’, ‘ESG issues’ or other similar or related terms in this document are as used in our public disclosures and not to any jurisdiction-specific regulatory definition or other interpretation of these terms unless specified otherwise.
